### **The common denominator in all living organisms: the celi** 

Our planet is populated by many different organisms, and by observing them we realize that they are different the one to the other. Notwithstanding, they all have something in common, and that something is the fact that they all are made by **cells.** So, cells are said to be the **fundamental units oflife.** When we talk about different living beings, they can either be **unicellular** organisms or **multicellular** organisms, but in any case, they will be composed of cells that originated starting from the division of a single previously existing cell. For instance, taking animals as an example, cell divisions take place after the formation ofthe zygote (the first cell of a new forming organism that derives from the union ofthe oocyte and the sperm). 

### _Cells variation in appearance and function_ 

Cells can vary a lot in terms of appearance and function. For example, in the first picture below a **nerve celi** is shown, with a cell body in the middle and a lot of other branches, which develop from the centre and are oriented towards the periphery and have the function of communicating with nearby cells. At the bottom of the image, a ruler is also found. This ruler expresses the approximative size of what we are looking at. But taking into consideration the **paramecium,** it is possible to easily understand the many visible differences that it has if compared to the nerve cell. Paramecium presents cilia, hair-like protuberances that consent free movement in the environment to the paramecium. Again, these structures are different from the ones found in a macrophage, a cell of the immune system whose role is to move around the body to find and destroy pathogens. Hence, **macrophages** need to be able to adhere to tissues and surfaces and roll on top of them to reach the target pathogen. In conclusion, we can say that cells can vary a lot in their appearance, they can have: 

- diff erent sizes 

- diff erent shapes 

- different chemical requirements 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
Nerve cell  Paramecium  Snapdragon flower  Macrophage  Yeast<br><!-- End of picture text -->

So, depending on the type of cell and the type of tissue to which the cell belongs, it will have different functions, since those are defined based on the above-listed properties. 

### _Common characteristics of cells_ 

Cells are the basic units oflife; in the previous paragraph it is described through some examples how can cell vary, and a few differences are pointed out. But cells also have lots of things in common: 

3 

- they are composed of the same molecules. 

- they all contain genetic information written in the same chemical code and replicated in the same way. Genetic information is conserved in DNA and then transcribed in RNA which is in tum translated into **proteins** (that carry out most ofthe fundamental processes of the cells). 

- The presence of the **plasma membrane,** a lipid layer that physically divides the content of the cell from the extemal environment. 

### _Definition of living organisms and viruses_ 

Based on this characteristic that cells share, it is possible to define the general group ofliving organisms. So, a living organism must be able to: 

- grow and reproduce. 

- maintain **homeostasis** ( a steady state). 

- communicate with other cells by responding to **external stimoli.** 

- have a metabolic activity to produce the **energy** necessary to carry out vital processes. 

- be exposed to **evolotion pressore** ( considering long periods of time). 

Both multicellular and unicellular organisms belong to the living organisms' group since they all have these characteristics, but what about **viroses?** Viruses are considered to be borderline organisms. They do not fully correspond to the previous stabilized criteria; it is true that they can replicate themselves and also evolve over time in order to escape the effects of drugs/immune system and hence reach a larger number oftargets. Nonetheless, viruses are not able to be independent and carry out metabolic processes on their own, since they must hijack the molecular machinery of a host cell and use it for their own good. Viruses are therefore considered as **obligated intracellolar parasites** and not as living beings; even though scientist are debating about this. 

## The discovery of cells and historical background 

### _Robert Hooke (1635-1702)_ 

The cell was first discovered by **Robert Hooke,** one of the first people who were able to use microscopes in order to observe different specimens. Hooke was observing cork slices, when he described their appearance as characterized by holes, which reminded him of the cells where the monks used to live in. What he didn't know was that he was actually in front **ofwall cells,** typical of plant cells. 

### _Antoni van Leeuwenhoek (1632-1723)_ 

Antoni van Leeuwenhoek was not a professional scientist, but a tradesman interested in analyzing the qualities of threads. He was able to build a **microscope** that could obtain a magnification of 200 times, which was incredibly avantgarde considered the period. He gave the first description of **red blood cells** and performed an approximate calculation of their size by comparing them to the size of the cell membrane, obtaining a quite accurate result. Moreover, he was the first person who observed **bacteria,** and he managed to do this by observing the plaques between the teeth of his own mouth: "I then most always saw, with great wonder, that in the said matter there were many very little living animalcules, very prettily a-moving". 

4 

### _Schleiden, Schwann and Virchow_ 

Schleiden was the first one to hypothesize that plants are made of cells. Schwann was the first one to theorize that animals are made of cells. Virchow stated **"Omnis cellula e cellula",** meaning that cells come only from other cells, they cannot come up by spontaneous generation, but they must derive by another pre-existing cell. 

### _Spontaneous generation and Pasteur s experiment_ 

The spontaneous generation hypothesis was a very popular theory of the time that stated that living creatures could arise from non-living matter, and it was Pasteur to demonstrate this theory wrong. He took two different long-necked flasks, one with a straight neck and the other one with an S-shaped neck, and placed equal amounts of broth inside of them; he later boiled both broths to kill any living matter eventually present in the liquid. The sterile result was then left exposed to the air; after severa! weeks, Pasteur observed that the broth in the straight-neck flask was cloudy, whereas the one in the S-shaped neck flask was identica! to before. So, if spontaneous generation were real, we would expect to have germs in both flasks, but germs in the air were only able to fall down the straight-necked flask and contaminate its broth only; concluding that **spontaneous generation is inconsistent.** 

### _The celi theory and lts lmplication_ 

The cell theory states that: 

- all living organisms are made of one or more cells. 

- the cell is the basic unit of structure and organization in living organisms. 

- all cells are derived from the division of pre-existing cells. 

The implications of the cell theory are that: 

- anything less than a cell cannot be a living organism. 

- functions of all cells are similar. 

- life is continuous (cells divide and generate new cells). 

- origin of life was the origin of cells. 

Also, in the 1880s, Walter **Sutton** and Theodor **Boveri** identified the chromosome as the hub for heredity, linking in that way genetics and cytology. 

### _Euka,ryotes and Prokaryotes: main distinctions_ 

The classica! definition (and distinction) that differentiate eukaryotes from prokaryotes relies in the presence or absence of a **nucleus.** Prokaryotes lack a nucleus and eukaryotes are characterized by an enveloped nucleus with genetic materiai inside of it. But this is not the only difference. Apart from the nucleus, in eukaryotic cells we also find other structures and organelles delimited by membranes, which are completely absent in prokaryotes. Moreover, in prokaryotic animai cells a **cytoskeleton** is also present. Between prokaryotic cells/organisms we can define bacteria and archaea, which are unicellular, while in eukaryotic cells/organisms we find protists, fungi, animals and plants, that can be either unicellular or multicellular. 

5 

### _Prokaryotic organisms_ 

Prokaryotes are the most diverse and numerous cells on earth, they are both inside us and around us , and can survive in many harsh environments. They can obtain energy from a diversity of energy sources ( organic, inorganic, light). Prokaryotes are mostly defined in two domains, which are **bacteria** and **archaea.** Those two are different for sure, but present multiple similarities: 

- both are unicellular. 

- lack of internal organization (found in eukaryotic cells instead). 

- generally, they have a **single circular chromosome** (vs. the eukaryotic linear one) 

- reproduce asexually through **binary fission.** 

- can share genomic information between them by horizontal gene transfer. 

- almost all prokaryotes have a cell wall outside of their plasma membrane which allows them to survive also in extreme conditions. 

Said that bacteria and archaea mainly differ due to: 

- differences at molecular level. 

- archaea can be found in environments that are too hostile even for certain bacteria. 

### _Structure of Prokaryotic celi: bacteria_ 

It's easier to find bacteria than archaea during our everyday life. But how are they organized? They are characterized by the presence of a cell membrane that allows them to be separated physically from the extemal environment. Bacteria also have one circular chromosome, and eventually, some of them can have **flagella** and **pili.** Flagella allow the cell to move, while pili are useful in order to adhere to other structures or even to mediate the horizontal gene transfer. Some bacteria also possess a **capsule** outside the cell wall. They divide by fission and the majority ofthem count **plasmids,** accessory DNAmolecules that give the cells the ability of doing something more. 

### _Celi wall_ 

The main function of this structure is to provide **support** and **protection** to the bacteria, but also to determine its **shape.** The cell wall is mainly characterized by peptidoglycan ( a macromolecular complex that contains modified sugars and a peptidic component), while in archaea is entirely composed of polysaccharides. 

Why is it important to describe the type of cell wall that a certain bacterium has? Because knowing how to classify bacteria on this basis can help us find the right treatment for our patient. According to their cell wall structure bacteria can be defined as: 

- **gram** + (positive): characterized by a thick layer of peptidoglycan. On the top, we find teichoic acid, centrai for the adhesion to target cells. 

- **gram** - (negative): characterized by an inner and outer membrane with a small layer of peptidoglycans in between. The outer membrane is marked by the presence of **LPS** (lipopolysaccharide) which forms an outer leaflet. This is crucial for bacterium survival since it's 

6 

a virulent factor that enables and increases the ability of bacteria to infect and target organisms; LPS outer leaflet is indeed toxic for target cells and induces their death. 

So, the difference between Gram + and Gram - bacteria lies in the structure of their cell wall. How can we distinguish Gram + from Gram- on a practical level? lt's easy to just perform **Gram staining.** The bacterial culture is stained with **crystal violet** and as a result, all cells in the culture will become violet; at this point, a fixative is added, which allows the fixation of the staining to the cell; after this, the cells are washed to decolorize them with alcohol. However, the crystal violet coloration will remain present on the Gram+ cells because it highly interacts with the peptidoglycan layer. On the other hand, Gram-, which has a smaller layer of peptidoglycans, will decolor. In the end, safranin is applied to color also Gram- cells but in a diff erent shade. 

Knowing the Gram reaction of a **clinical isolate** (isolated bacterial species taken from a patient) can help the health care professionals make a more accurate diagnosis and hence choose the most appropriate antibiotic for the treatment. 

Until now we talked about bacteria, and we are used to thinking about them as something negative. But on the contrary, they can also be an integrai part of our body and be beneficiai for us. For example, our skin and intestine are full ofuseful bacteria that benefit our body. 

7 

### **Eukaryotic celi: the celi membrane, its structure, functions, and mechanisms of transport** 

Inside eukaryotic cells it is possible to observe a very strict organization and compartmentalization. Apart from the nucleus, we can already start to see new compartments and organelles, through which the cell also manages to compartmentalize different functions, so that each compartment has a specific process and function that are carried out only there. 

The cell membrane physically divides the internal part of the cell from the extemal environment. What are its functions? The main functions ofmembranes are: 

- **receiving information,** because the cell needs to communicate with other cells or even with the environment itself, and to do that it must receive stimuli and answer to them in the appropriate way. For that, we can find proteins that act like receptors and then transiate the extemal stimuli in biochemical cascades, that in tum will induce an answer by the cell which activates biochemical cascades for the cell to communicate. 

- **physical barrier,** but it is not isolated, it also needs to exchange molecules with the extemal environment ancl/or with other cells. Thus, we need to move molecules across the membrane thanks to certain proteins on the cell surface. 

- having the potential for **movement** and **expansion.** Not all the cells move around (it depends on the considered tissue ). The membrane needs to be flexible in order to allow movement and expansion. The ability to expand is necessary because cells need to grow in terms of size, for example in cellular division, when the membrane component has to be equally divided without generating ruptures. 

In conclusion, it is possible to say that the cell membrane allows the exchange of information, growth, shrinkage, and movement. 

What is the structure ofthe membrane on a macromolecular level? It is mainly composed **oflipids** and **proteins,** but the formers are responsible for the formation of the lipidic bilayer. 

### _Lipids_ 

Phospholipids are the main components of the membrane but other two important classes of lipids in the membrane are **glycolipids** and **cholesterol.** We'll see that glycolipids help protect the cell surface and cell adhesion, while cholesterol is essential to modulate the membrane fluidity. 

Phospholipids have a very peculiar structure, with a hydrophobic part ( **tail)** and a hydrophilic part **(head).** Due to this specific characteristic of being both hydrophilic and hydrophobic, those kind of molecules are defined as **"amphipathic".** Since phospholipids have this property, when put in an aqueous environment, they tend to compose a bilayer structure, thus excluding water molecules from their interactions. Phospholipids are sometimes represented with straight or bended legs, composed by fatty acid. They are represented like this because they can be **saturated** or unsaturated (where the chains of carbon atoms that are not bound to the maximum number of hydrogen atoms because of the presence of double bonds). 

The most energetic favorable struc"ure is thus a **sealed compartment** that avoids the presence of free edges and manages to avoid the interaction between phospholipids tail with the aqueous part. 

8 

### _Celi membrane_ 

In the cell membrane there are both lipid and protein components. 

Phospholipids are amphipathic molecules, since they have both a polar and non-polar part. The hydrophobic part is composed of **triglycerides** which can be monosaturated or polysaturated. There are two kinds of phospholipids: 

- **Phosphoglycerides,** which consist of fatty acids esterified to a glycerol molecule. 

- **Sphingolipids,** based on sphingosine, important in membrane structure and cell signaling. They are mainly present in the outer monolayer of the plasma membrane bilayer. 

Phospholipids are key components of plasma membranes, but glycolipids and cholesterol are also present. **Glycolipids** are lipids enriched with a carbohydrate group, are mainly derived from sphingosine, and are generally situated on the extemal sheet of the membrane (bilayer). Glycolipids are the determinant of human blood groups, which are characterized by the presence of a specific protein on the blood cell. Glycolipids mediate interactions with other cells or signaling. When glycolipids are not synthetized or metabolized in the correct way, problems at the level ofthe organism can arise, and when sphingolipids are not synthetized in the right way, they accumulate in lysosomes which undergo rupture (lysosomal storage disorder). 

**Cholesterol** is important because it modulates membrane **fluidity:** being short and rigid, it fills the spaces between neighboring phospholipid molecules, thus stiffening the bilayer. Cholesterol decreases the permeability of the membrane and stiffens it. 

Proteins in the cell membrane are responsible for carrying out specific membrane function and in 1972 Jonathan **Singer** and Garth **Nicolson** proposed the **fluid membrane structure,** where membranes are viewed as two-dimensional fluids in which proteins are inserted into lipid bilayers. 

Among membrane characteristics, the following are included: 

- **Fluidity:** lipids and proteins can move and change place inside the membrane. All elements can move but at the same time they maintain the **integrity** of the membrane. Lipids can perf orm flipflop movements from one half of the bilayer to the other (very rare event carried out by enzymes). Lipids which change places with their neighbor in the same monolayer, performing lateral diffusion. Molecules can rotate around their axes (rotation) and lipids can bend their hydrocarbon tails. All these ensures the performance of different functions in the cell. Membrane fluidity allows cell signaling and an even composition along the cell perimeter. 

- **Flexibility:** the membrane can resist to flexion without rupturing. 

- **Discontinuity:** its elements can move within it. 

- **Selectivity:** it has a certain degree of permeability to the passage of molecules. 

Fluidity depends on the nature of the hydrocarbon tails ( on their lengths and on the number of double bonds ). The longer the tails the more hydrophobic the interaction: thus, the membrane is more viscous and less fluid ( as with shorter tails ). Single bonds enable the formati on of a very clean and ordered bilayer and a compact membrane. When there are double bonds, there are bends in the fatty acids, so there is the 

9 

formation of gaps within the chain and the membrane becomes more fluid. Phospholipids are important to ensure a certain level of fluidity. 

At low temperatures, phospholipids tend to cluster together, and cholesterol disrupts their intermolecular interactions and increases fluidity. At high temperatures, instead, phospholipids are more fluid, but cholesterol pulls them together, increasing intermolecular forces and decreasing fluidity. 

### _Membrane proteins_ 

Proteins make all the work in the cell and carry out mostly all the processes within it. They account for about 50% of the mass of most plasma cells and each type of cell membrane contains a different set of proteins, reflecting the specialized functions of the particular membrane. Proteins are involved in many different functions: 

- **Transporters,** such as the Na+ pump, which actively pumps Na+ out of cells and K+ in. 

- **lon channels,** such as K+ leak channel, which allows K+ ions to leave cells, thereby having a major influence on cell excitability. 

- **Anchors,** such as integrins, which link intracellular actin filaments to extracellular matrix proteins. 

- **Receptors,** such as platelet-derived growth factor (PDGF) receptor, which binds extracellular PGDF and, as a consequence, generates intracellular signals that cause the cell to grow and divide. 

- **Enzymes,** such as adenylyl cyclase, which catalyzes the production of the small intracellular signaling molecule cyclic AMP in response to extracellular signals. 

Among integral proteins there are **transmembrane proteins,** which can cross the membrane, having both hydrophobic and hydrophilic regions ( or domains, such as cytoplasm or phospholipid bilayer). Proteins can be associated with the membrane by attached lipid groups. 

Among **peripheral proteins** there are proteins which can be held in place by interactions with other membrane proteins. 

To study proteins it is necessary to destroy the lipid bilayer by disrupting hydrophobic associations using detergents, whose small, amphipathic, lipid-like molecules differ from membrane phospholipids in that they have only a single hydrophobic tail. When mixed in great excess with membranes, the hydrophobic ends of detergent molecules internet with the membrane-spanning hydrophobic regions of the transmembrane proteins and phospholipids, thereby disrupting the lipid bilayer and separating the proteins. 

The membrane interacts with other components of the cell: it not only protects the internal part of the cell, but also maintain its **shape** and to do so it needs interactions with other components. Most cell membranes are strengthened and supported by a framework of proteins: in red blood cell the transmembrane proteins internet with another protein called **spectrin.** Animal cell membranes are stabilized by a meshwork of fibrous proteins, called the **celi cortex.** 

The cell surface is enriched by **glycolipids** and **glycoproteins;** indeed, some lipids and most of the proteins in the plasma membrane have short chains of sugars linked to them. The carbohydrate part is located on the outside of the membrane, where it forms a sugar coating called **glycocalyx.** It forms a 

10 

barrier, mediates cell-cell interactions, and protects the membrane from the direct action of physical forces. The slimy surface of cells prevents them from piling up; additionally, glycocalyx is important also for cell-cell recognition and adhesion. 

Lectins are proteins that recognize particular oligosaccharides side chains. Tue layer is a sort of distinctive clothing, it is characteristic of each cell type and is recognized by other cell types. 

### _Transport across membrane_ 

Cells need to **exchange nutrients** with extemal environment and depending on the type of the chemical and physical characteristics of the molecules that it needs to move, there is a different **permeability** of the membrane. The hydrophobic interi or of the bilayer creates a barrier to the passage of most hydrophilic molecules. Specialized membrane transport proteins help the passage of polar and charged molecules and they include: 

- **Channels,** which discriminate mainly on the basis of size and electric charge: when the channel is open, any ion or molecule that is small enough and carries the appropriate charge can pass through. 

- **Transporters,** which bind their solutes with great specificity and selectivity, transferring only those molecules that fit into specific binding sites on the protein. 

In **passive transportation,** molecules spontaneously flow from a region ofhigh concentration to a region of low concentration, without the need for energy. In active transportation the movement of molecules occurs against concentration gradient, and it requires energy. 

Water molecules can diffuse directly across the lipid bilayer, although slowly. Some cells contain specialized channels, called **aquaporins** that facilitate the movement. The movement of water down its concentration gradientfrom an area oflow solute concentration (high water concentration) to an area ofhigh solute concentration (low water concentration}-is called **osmosis.** 

11 

### **Prokaryotes and eukaryotes: structure and function of cells** 

The cellular space is delimited by membrane compartments, specialized in different functions and collectively called **endomembrane system.** 

### _The endoplasmic reticulum (ER)_ 

The endoplasmic reticulum (ER) is a system of interconnected **sacs and tu bes** of membrane that often extends throughout most of the cell; it can be much more present in terms of volume that it occupi es in specific cells which are implied in detoxification. Its membrane content is 50% of the total membrane volume of a cell, and it is in continuity with the nuclear envelope. There are two types of ER: 

- **Rough endoplasmic reticulum** (RER), with ribosomes attached to the cytosolic surface, and involved in protein synthesis. 

- **Smooth endoplasmic reticulum** (SER), abundant is some specialized cells, and involved in different functions, such as synthesis and metabolism of lipids, steroid hormone synthesis, biogenesis of membranes, detoxification processes, storage and release of Ca<sup>2</sup> + ions, carbohydrate metabolism. 

The **hepatocyte** is the main cellular type present in the liver: it has an abundant SER, involved in the production of lipoprotein particles, whose role is to transport lipids throughout blood to other regions of the body. The hepatic SER is also involved in several detoxification processes; in the liver, SER is involved in glucose metabolism by regulating glycogen storage. 

The ER is also the main site for the **synthesis** of membrane **lipids;** however, the biosynthesis is limited to a monolayer, known as **cytosol side.** To make an even distribution of lipids in the synthetized membrane, there are enzymes, known as **scramblase,** which transfer random phospholipids from one monolayer to the other. 

The ER is also involved in calcium storage. SER lumen contains proteins able to bind Ca<sup>2</sup> + ions, which are pumped directly into the lumen by specific proteins and are released from the ER upon cell signaling pathways. An example of specialized cell is the muscle cell, where the ER ( called **sarcoplasmic reticulum)** is mainly devoted to this function. 

Several ribosomes are bound to the outer layer of the membrane of the RER, which is abundant in cells undergoing high protein synthesis. The most important function of the RER is **protein synthesis:** proteins synthesized in the RER are **"export proteins",** meaning that they are destined to the extracellular space, the endomembrane system, or the cell membrane. The mechanism is co-translational, which means that the polypeptide is transferred into the ER lumen during the synthesis. 

Other RER functions include: 

- glycosylation ( additi on of sugar chains to proteins ). 

- protein folding. 

- assembly of multimeric proteins. 

- quality control of proteins: if the processes of synthesis, folding, modifications and assembly don't succeed, proteins are degraded. 

12 

### _The Golgi Apparatus_ 

The Golgi apparatus is composed of stacks of flattened, membrane-enclosed sacs, called **cisternae.** Its role is to modify and package molecules made in the ER, that are destined to be either secreted from the cell or transported to another cell compartment. 

This cell compartment was named after the Italian scientist Camilla **Golgi,** the first to describe this structure in 1898. While studying the nervous system, he observed thread-like networks inside the cells using a particular staining reaction based on osmium dichromate solution. However, most scientists disputed his discovery as a staining artefact, since no known structure could justify his observation. Only in 1950, his discovery was demonstrated thanks to electronic microscopes. The Nobel Prize in Physiology was awarded jointly to Camilla Golgi and Santiago Ramon y Cajal in 1906 "in recognition of their work on the structure of the nervous system". 

The Golgi apparatus is a very dynamic structure, surrounded by transport vesicles and with different sides: 

1. The **CIS face,** which is oriented towards the ER and receives vesicles budding from the ER. 

2. The **TRANS face,** apposite to the CIS side: vesicles that bud from here are directed to other destinations within the celi. 

3. **MEDIAL cisternae,** located between the CIS and the TRANS sides, where the maturation of proteins and lipids occurs. 

The outermost cisterna at each face is connected to a network of interconnected membranous tubes and vesicles. Expansion and number of the Golgi cistemae depend on the celi activity, and CIS, TRANS, and mediai parts of the Golgi apparatus are biochemically and functionally different, and are involved in: 

- **Glycosylation** of molecules ( additi on of sugar chains to the top of molecules ). 

- **Proteolysis** (cutting) ofpeptides into active forms. 

- **Sorting** of macromolecules. 

The Golgi apparatus is responsible for the determination of asymmetric membranes distribution: the two halves of the bilayer often include strikingly different sets of phospholipids. But if membranes emerge from the ER with an evenly assorted set of phospholipids **(scramblases),** where does this asymmetry arise? **Flippases** remove specific phospholipids from one side of the bilayer and flip them into the other monolayer and their action (similar to that of enzymes in the plasma membrane) initiates and maintains the asymmetric arrangement of phospholipids that is characteristic of the membranes of animai cells. 

As a consequence, all cell membranes have distinct "inside" and "outside" faces: this conservation of orientation applies not only to the phospholipids that make up the membrane, but to any protein that might be inserted in the membrane. Among lipids, those that show the most dramatically distribution in celi membranes are glycolipids, which are located mainly in the plasma membrane, and only in the noncytosolic half of the bilayer. Other lipid molecules show different types of asymmetric distributions, which relate to their specific functions (inositol phospholipids ). 

Proteins and membranes synthetized on the ER must be transported to their final destinations: 

- Endoplasmic reticulum. 

13 

- Golgi apparatus. 

- Endosomes and lysosomes. 

- Cell membrane. 

- Extracellular space. 

Vesicles bub from the ER and reach the Golgi apparatus, where they are sorted to different areas of the cell. 

### _Endocytosis_ 

The ER is particularly important in protein and lipid synthesis and to sort them in the different compartrnents within the cell. The cell is characterized by intense trafficking of vesicles and materials, from the ER to the Golgi apparatus and then to the rest of the cell. Once proteins and lipids have been synthetized, they are sorted to their destinations. 

The cell is also able to take up materials (molecules and fluids) from the outside through **endocytosis:** random particles can be intemalized, but on the surface of the cells there are receptors which induce intemalization of specific substances in the cell. During **phagocytosis** the cell intemalizes big particles, while in **pinocytosis,** the celi indiscriminately intemalizes fluids. Receptor-mediated endocytosis uses receptors in order to intemalize specific materials. 

## Overall, endocytosis is a process through which cells take up fluids and molecules. 

In phagocytosis animals also intemalize pathogens, forming a vesicle around them which will be destroyed with its content by lysosomes. Phagocytosis is a form of endocytosis linked to big molecules and is continuously carried out by the celi: an invagination forms on the inner surface of the celi, then a vesicle buds from the cell and is intemalized. A macrophage swallows 25% of its own volume of fluid each hour, which means that it removes 3 % of its plasma membrane each minute. Endocytosis is balanced by the apposite process, **exocytosis,** which is the process of secretion by the cell of other molecules within the extracellular environrnent. 

Receptor-mediated endocytosis is mediated by **receptors,** which are protein complexes localized in the plasma membrane able to bind specific target molecules. Their binding stimulates the formation of a vesicle which takes up the receptor and its cargo. 

Cholesterol is transported in the bloodstream bound to protein in the form of particles called **low-density lipoprotein** (LDL): LDLs bind to receptors located on the celi surface causing the receptor-LDL complexes to be ingested by receptor-mediated endocytosis and delivered to endosomes (sorting platforms). LDL cholesterol is transported into lysosomes, where its components are digested: there is the generation of free cholesterol and peptides which can be recycled again. Levels of LDL-cholesterol also depend on genetics: this pathway for cholesterol uptake is disrupted in individuals who inherit a defective gene encoding the LDL receptor protein; in some cases, the receptors are missing, while in others, they are present but non-functional. The receptor is encoded in a specific gene, and if there is a mutation the receptor may either be present on the surface but not work as it should be or even be absent. The accumulati on of LDL cholesterol in the blood causes the formati on of **atherosclerotic plaques,** that can obstruct blood flow in vessels. The clogging ofthe coronary arteries that supply the heart muscle can 

14 

cause heart attacks. Indeed, particles accumulate in veins and are too numerous for macrophages to be eliminated. 

Receptor-mediated endocytosis is also used to take up many other essential metabolites, such as vitamin B 12 and iron, both required for the synthesis of hemoglobin. 

Receptor-mediated endocytosis can also be exploited by viruses: the influenza virus, which causes the flu, gains entry into cells in this way. By themselves, viruses are not able to perform any metabolic pathway, but they can find their way to a host cell by binding to receptors on the cell membrane and by exploiting this receptor interaction: thus, they are intemalized by the cell, and once inside they can be replicated and can form viral particles which infect other cells in the body or other hosts. 

### _Endosomes and lysosomes_ 

**Endosomes** are a set of connected membranes tubes and vesicles, whose function is to receive all the vesicles budding from the membrane and materials to be destroyed or recycled and sort them. Two sets of endosomes exist: 

1. **Early endosomes,** just beneath the plasma membrane, which gradually mature into endosomes. 2. **Late endosomes,** closer to the nucleus. 

Endosomes are characterized by slight acidic pH, maintained by **ATP-driven proton pumps** that move H+ into the endosome lumen from the cytosol. This movement of protons happens against the concentration gradient, since the cell needs to accumulate them; energy is thus needed, and it is in the form of ATP. The pH can also induce a modulati on in the shape of the protein. 

The endosomal compartment acts as the main sorting station in the inward endocytic pathway by: 

- **Recycling:** most receptors are retumed to the same part of the plasma membrane. 

- **Degrading:** receptors are transported to lysosomes, where they are degraded. 

- Performing **transcytosis:** receptors proceed to a different domain ofthe plasma membrane. 

If cargo proteins remain bound to their receptors, they share their fate, but if cargo dissociates from receptors in the endosomes (because of the pH), it is doomed to destruction in lysosomes. 

The most important destination for the vesicles and their material are **lysosomes,** which are a compartment discovered in 1955 by the biochemist Christian **De Duve.** In 1949 he was studying how insulin acted on liver cells ( on mice) and trying to determine the locati on of the enzyme glucose-6phosphatase (important in the metabolism of glucose) inside the cells. Scientists were using an enzyme called acid phosphatase as a control for their experiments and a centrifuge which allowed to separate the components of the cellular extract (from livers) by density and dimension, observing the activity of the control enzyme. However, they had a very hard time purifying the protein from the extract. They tried a gentler approach using differential centrifugation: by centrifuging at low speed, they were able to isolate (pellet = precipitate) the heavier molecules (nuclei, for example), and then they centrifuged again at higher speed smaller organelles that did not precipitate previously. They repeated the process several times and were able to separate different fractions of the cell. When they measured the activity of the control enzyme, they observed that it was about 10% of what they were able to measure on cell extract. One day a student forgot a sample in the fridge and by measuring its activity they discovered a much 

15 

higher activity than that in the previous experiments: the only difference was the time spent in the fridge, which in this case was of 4/5 days. They hypothesized that a membrane-like barrier limited the accessibility of the enzyme to its substrate (when measuring the activity immediately they did not find any simply because the enzyme could not reach the substrate), and they described that membrane-like barrier as a "saclike structure surrounded by a membrane and containing acid phosphatase". By giving the enzyme enough time, when taken out of the cell, these compartments are disrupted, and the enzyme can move out and interact with the substrate. By 1955, additional enzymes were discovered in these structures, and De Duve named them "lysosomes" to reflect their lytic nature (able to destroy macromolecules). That same year, Alex **Novikoffvisited** de Duve's laboratory and was able to obtain the first electron micrographs ofthe new organelle. De Duve was awarded the Nobel Prize in Physiology in 1974 for "discoveries concerning the structural and functional organization of the celi". 

Lysosomes are organelles containing **lytic enzymes** able to degrade all the main classes of biologica! macromolecules, including lipids, carbohydrates, nucleic acids, and proteins. Lysosomes represents the **digestive apparatus** of the celi and possess different types of enzymes able to degrade various types of macromolecules. They carry out the controlled intracellular digesti on of both extracellular materials and worn-out organelles. They contain about forty types ofhydrolytic enzymes, optimally active in the acidic conditions (pH~5) maintained within lysosomes. Lysosome's membrane normally keeps these destructive enzymes out ofthe cytosol. The key to lysosomal activity is to internalize H+ ions to decrease the pH. 

The lysosomal membrane contains transporters that allow the final products of the digestion of macromolecules to be transferred to the cytosol, where they can be either excreted or utilized by the celi. The membrane also contains an ATP-driven H+ pump, which maintains its contents at an acidic pH. Most of the lysosomal membrane proteins are unusually **highly glycosylated;** the sugars, which cover much of the protein surfaces facing the lumen, protect the proteins from digesti on by the lysosomal proteases. 

The cell knows that when macromolecules must be degraded, they must be taken to lysosomes, which take care of them all and work in an acidic environment (high compartmentalization). If the enzymes are released in the cytosol (whose pH is about 7) they are not able to work because the environment is not acidic, and this is a way to protect the celi. 

Lysosomes receive extracellular materiai derived from: 

- Phagocytosis, when phagosomes fuse with lysosomes. 

- Pinocytosis/receptor-mediated endocytosis, with the materiai delivered to lysosomes via endosomes. 

**Autophagy** is a process used to degrade obsolete organelles and parts of the cell: a target organelle is enclosed by a double membrane, creating an **autophagosome,** which then fuses with a lysosome, therefore inducing the degradation of the vesicle and its content. All the macromolecules that were forming that organelle will be degraded and the building blocks will be recycled. On the membrane of lysosomes there are receptors which enable the transport of these building blocks: the amino acids generated by this cannibalistic form of digestion can then be recycled to allow continued protein synthesis. 

16 

Lysosomal storage diseases **(LSDs)** are a group of over 70 diseases that are characterized by lysosomal dysfunction. These disorders are individually rare but collectively affect 1 in 5.000 live births. LSDs, typically present in infancy and childhood, have a progressive neurodegenerative clinica! course, although symptoms in other organ systems are frequent. Defects that impair lysosome functions cause the accumulation ofundigested or partially digested macromolecules in lysosomes (that is, 'storage') or impair the transport of molecules, which can result in cellular damage. Severa! LSDs can be treated with approved, disease-specific therapies that are mostly based on enzyme replacement: it is possible to supply patients with extemal enzymes so that the activity of this particular pathway can be restored. 

**Pompe disease** is a disorder of glycogenolysis resulting in glycogen accumulation in lysosomes. Glycogen degradation takes piace both in the cytoplasm and inside the lysosomes. Here, the breakdown of glycogen is accomplished by the enzyme acid a-glucosidase. Rupture of engorged lysosomes leads to tissue damage primarily involving skeletal and cardiac muscle. Patients with infantile-onset Pompe disease present in the first few months of life with weakness, hypotonia, developmental delay, feeding difficulties, and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Enzyme replacement therapy has changed the natural history of Pompe disease, allowing it to evolve from a fatai cardiac disease into a chronic neuromuscular disorder. 

### _Peroxisomes_ 

Peroxisomes are a class of small organelles bounded by a single membrane. In mammals they are particularly abundant in the **kidney** and **liver.** They contain severa! **oxidases** - enzymes that use molecular oxygen to oxidize organic substances, in the process forming hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), a corrosive substance. Peroxisomes also contain copious amounts ofthe enzyme **catalase,** which degrades hydrogen peroxide to yield water and oxygen: 

Inactivation of peroxides that are produced in oxidation reactions may cause injury to proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids, leading to cell death: 

1. Protein oxidation can impair a wide variety of enzymatic processes resulting in marked cellular dysfunction. 

2. Lipid peroxidation has been linked to celi death through effects on cellular phospholipids (major cell membrane components) and the release of ceramide, which activates a program of celi death ( apoptosis ). 

3. Nucleic acid oxidation has been linked with physiologic and premature aging as well as DNA strand breaks, leading to necrosis and/or apoptosis. 

Peroxisomes also contribute to the ~-oxidation of fatty acids; moreover, this pathway is important for fatty acid catabolism and for detoxification of toxic substances, such as methanol, ethanol, phenol. 

### _Nucleus_ 

The nucleus stores the **genetic information** ofthe organism, and within it multiple processes are carried out, such as DNA replication, DNA transcription into RNA, RNA processing, assembly of ribosoma! subunits. The nucleus has a double membrane, which is a double bilayer of lipids: the inner membrane 

17 

contains some proteins that bind to chromosomes and other proteins that anchor the **nuclear lamina,** a two-dimensional meshwork of protein filaments that lines the inner face of the membrane and provides structural support. The outer nuclear membrane is in continuity with the ER. 

The nuclear lamina is part of the cytoskeleton and in particular of the **intermediate filaments,** and it disassembles and reforms at each cell division: the nuclear membrane disassembles to allow chromosomes to be captured and segregated into daughter cells; in the end of the process a new nuclear lamina is formed. 

Materials have to flow across the membrane, so there are specific structures **(nuclear pores,** composed of different proteins), which punctuate each nuclear membrane and allow the passage of molecules. Nuclear pores are large, elaborate structure composed of a complex of about thirty different proteins. Specialized transporters recognized nuclear proteins, target them and mediate their movement. 

Inside the nucleus there are chromosomes and depending on the species their number may vary. DNA is a very long complicated molecule whose building blocks are **nucleotides.** There are three billion nucleotides distributed in our chromosomes, which are organized in a specific way and whose level of packaging vari es depending on the cell cycle moment. U sually, chromosomes must be compacted to fit in the nucleus, but the level of compaction is modified depending on the state ofthe cell life. In interphase the level of compaction is much lower, and as we proceed in cellular division, the compaction increases and chromosomes become more visible. 

Within the nucleus there are other compartments, not separated by membranes: they are functional domains where specific processes take place. 

Chromosomes are spatially organized: each of them tends to occupy a particular regi on or territory of the nucleus, and this loose organization prevents interphase chromosomes from becoming extensively entangled. Some chromosomal regions are physically attached to particular sites on the nuclear envelope, helping chromosomes remain within their distinct territories. This is important from a functional point of view: the **nucleolus** is the most prominent domain within the nucleus which can be visualized through electromicroscopy; it is the site of rRNA and ribosomes assembly (ribosomes are involved in protein synthesis and are composed of rRNA and proteins). By looking at how chromosomes are organized within the nucleolus we see that it is formed of particular regions of chromosomes 13, 14, 15, 21, 22 (known as **acrocentric chromosomes),** whose tips contain genes which code for ribosoma! RNA. 

Other processes are localized to distinct regions of the nucleus, called **domains:** for example, the **splicing** process, which is part ofthe mRNAmaturation, occurs within discrete domains, called **nuclear speckles.** The splicing allows mRNA molecules to mature and takes place within this specific domain. 

Molecules are moved across the nuclear membrane: proteins are needed to enter the nucleus (protein translation occurs outside the nucleus) and all enzymes responsible for carrying out transcription and maturati on of RNA molecules and proteins are required to organize genetic material **(histones** ), which is packaged. Ribosoma! proteins - produced in the cytoplasm - need to get in, since they are produced in the cytoplasm but assembled in the nucleolus. Materials also need to move from the nucleus to cytoplasm: an example is mRNA, since it is the message translated by ribosomes. 

18 

### _Mitochondria_ 

Mitochondria are present in essentially all eukaryotic cells, and they are among the most conspicuous organelles in the cytoplasm. They are tubular-shaped and enclosed in two separate membranes, with the inner membrane formed into folds that project into the interior of the organelle. They are the **powerhouse** of the cell and are the key organelles of the **apoptotic process** ( cell death by suicide): when the cell is damaged, it is better for the organism to get rid of the cell and the apoptotic process is activated. 

There are many biochemical pathways taking piace in the mitochondria, especially what generally called the **cellular respiration process.** Mitochondria are specialized in the **production of ATP,** which is used as energy to perform biochemical 

reactions that need energetic input to be carried out. ATP stands for adenosine triphosphate, and it is a nucleotide composed of three main parts: nitrogen-containing rings (adenine), ribose and three phosphate groups. The energy derives from phosphate groups, which are highly negatively charged and whose charges tend to repulse each other but are kept together by covalent bonds. Whenever they are broken, a phosphate group and a high quantity of energy is released. ADP stands for adenosine diphosphate, since one phosphate group has been released. When ATP is hydrolyzed (the most extemal phosphate group is released), ADP and energy are obtained, but the phosphate group can be attached back. Mitochondria are important because it is possible to continuously reassemble ATP. 

Mitochondria hamess the energy from the oxidation of food molecules to produce ATP. Since mitochondria consume oxygen and release carbon dioxide the entire process is called cellular respiration, (breathing on a cellular level). Mitochondria give the cell the possibility to harvest energy from the oxidation of organic molecules. 

Mitochondria are very peculiar and different from the other compartments: they are adaptable and can adjust their location, shape and number to suit the needs of the celi. There are some types of cells that need more mitochondria than others: **muscle cells** have a very high energy request because they must perform muscle contraction, which needs energy in the form of ATP, so they are rich in mitochondria. In muscle cells, mitochondria localize within **myosin** and **actine** fibers, so the site of production of ATP is the same site where it is mostly needed. 

Even though mitochondria can be flexible and adaptable depending on the cell type, their basic internal structure remains the same. Mitochondria can fuse to form dynamic tubular networks, diffusely distributed through the cytoplasm. 

Mitochondria are tubular shaped and contain a double membrane (outer and inner) and the inner one forms invaginations which increase the surface of the membrane: this increases the number of enzymes which can be present, and the number of ATP molecules produced. There is an intermembrane space between the two membranes and a matrix comprised within the inner membrane; the outer membrane contains porins that make it permeable to small molecules and the intermembrane space has a 

19 

composition analogous to that ofthe cytoplasm. Biggermolecules are transported by **TOM** (Translocase of the Outer Membrane) complexes. 

The Inner membrane contains the main enzymes Involved In oxidative phosphorylation and a variety of transporters that allow the entering of selective molecules in the matrix. Proteins associated to it have three main functions: 

1. Electron transport ( **cytochromes)** 

2. ATP synthesis **(ATP synthase complex)** 

3. Transport of **metabolites** (pyruvate, ATP, ADP) across the membrane (TIM complex) 

In the matrix there are hundreds of enzymes carrying out biologica! reactions such as those required far the oxidation of pyruvate and fatty acids and far the ci tric acid cycle. 

Mitochondria possess their own DNA and ribosomes, so they produce their own RNA, and have this peculiarity since they are thought to derive from bacteria ( **endosymbiosis theory):** in the past they were bacteria able to produce energy and to undergo oxidative phosphorylation, but then the precursor of the cell ingested the precursor of mitochondria. Over time they lost important characteristics that would enable them to survive outside the cell: their DNA is really small (16.000 nucleotides, few compared to the 3 billion of the nuclear DNA) and can code far few proteins, involved in mitochondrial functions, rRNA and tRNA; however, far their survival, mitochondria also need proteins coded in the nuclear DNA. 

Mitochondrial DNA is **circular,** resembling prokaryotic DNA and mitochondria do not have any other organization apart from the double membrane. Mitochondrial DNA has **materna! inheritance** in humans, so the contribution of mitochondria in the new organism only comes from the egg and this is because in humans, during the development in the sperm cell, paternal mitochondria are already tagged by **ubiquitin,** a protein which signals that the tagged cell needs to be destroyed. Therefare, the zygote gets rid of paternal mitochondria. 

## Cytoskeleton 

The cytoskeleton is an intricate network **ofprotein filaments** that extends throughout the cytoplasm (and in the case of intermediate filaments also in the nucleus ). It is a highly **dynamic** structure that is continuously reorganized as a cell changes shape, divides, and responds to its environment. It allows the cell to change its shape and to respond to different inputs from the outside. The cytoskeleton: 

- provides cell **shape.** 

- drives **vesicle trafficking** and determines **organelle position.** 

- supports the **plasma membrane.** 

- is involved in **celi division.** 

- is involved in **celi motility.** 

There are three structures in the cytoskeleton, with different distribution, organization and functions: 

1. **actin filaments** (smallest). 

2. **intermediate filaments.** 

3. **microtubules** (biggest). 

20 

### _Intermediate filaments_ 

Intermediate filaments provide **resistance** to mechanical stress which occurs when cells are stressed. They can be of different types depending on the type 

of cell. They are anchored at plasma membrane by cell-to-cell junctions **(desmosomes),** and they are also located within the nucleus, where they form a meshwork called nuclear lamina. An intermediate filament is like a rope, in which many long strands are twisted together to provide **tensile strength,** which is the ability to withstand tension without breaking. Intermediate filaments are organized as monomers, and two monomers are coiled to form twisted dimers, which internet with non-covalent 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
INTERMEDIATE FILAMENTS<br>NUCLEAR<br>•<br>vimentin and<br>keratin filaments  vimentin-related  neurofilaments  nuclear lamins<br>filaments<br>'<br>in epithelial cells  in connective- in nerve cells  •  in all<br>tissue cells,  animai cells<br>muscle cells,<br>and glial cells<br><!-- End of picture text -->

interactions to form tetramers, which are then added via lateral, non-covalent interactions one after the other to form filaments. 

Intermediate filaments are prominent in the cytoplasm of cells subjected to mechanical stress, such as **nerve cells** (characterized by very long axons), **muscle cells,** and **epithelial cells.** 

Filaments can be **cytoplasmic** or **nuclear:** in epithelial cells there are keratin filaments ( cytoplasmic) and those in the interior of epithelial cells form a cabling ofhigh tensile strength that distributes the stress that occurs when the skin is stretched. The importance of this function is evident in a human genetic disease known as **epidermolysis bollosa simplex** (EBS), in which mutations interfere with the formation of keratin filaments in the epidermis: in this case skin cells do not resist to the stress they are subjected to, and they break, so harmful lesions occur. 

Also, nuclear lamina proteins can be subjected to genetic diseases: defects in nuclear lamina are associated with **progeria,** rare disorders that cause affected individuals to age prematurely. Children with progeria have wrinkled skin, lose their teeth and hair, and often develop severe cardiovascular diseases early in age. Mutations at nuclear lamina level make the nucleus change its shape and lose its characteristic conformation; moreover, since the nuclear membrane binds chromosomes, they can also be altered. 

### _Microtubules_ 

Microtubules grow out from a small structure near the nucleus in the e enter of the cell called **centrosome.** They create a system of tubes and tracks within the cell, along which vesicles, organelles, and other cell components can be transported. Their main function is transporting and positioning membrane-enclosed organelles and vesicles within the cell: they are the **highways** on the top of which vesicles are transported. Microtubules have a fundamental role in cell division, since they form the **mitotic spindle,** which segregates the chromosomes into daughter cells; they also form **cilia** and **flagella.** 

Microtubules are built from _a_ and _B_ **tubulin** proteins assembled to form a dimer. Many dimers can internet with each other to form protofilaments. Thirteen protofilaments compose a hallow tube, which is the final microtubule structure. 

In animal cells microtubules are organized by the centrosome, a structure usually located close to the 

21 

nucleus: they radiate from the centrosome outward through the cytoplasm. The centrosome is formed by a pair of centrioles, is surrounded by a matrix of proteins, and contains y-tubulin, which works as nucleation site. In the centrosome tubulin subunits assemble to give rise to the hallow tube. Because of their structure, microtubules have a **polarity:** the minus end of each microtubule is embedded in the centrosome, and growth occurs only at the plus end that extends into the cytoplasm. 

Microtubules are very dynamic and are able to adapt to the cell: this property is given by **dynamic instability,** which means that they can extend and grow within the cell andata certain point they can start to shrink by losing dimers, which are then regenerated and can be used again. After a while they can either completely disappear or start growing again. Sometimes they need to be stabilized (if they have to move vesicles along the axons of neurons far example ), so they can interact with proteins which stabilize them. 

Dynamic instability stems from the ability of tubulin dimers to **hydrolyze GTP** (GTPase activity), similar to ATP, but with a guanine instead of an adenine. They are able to hydrolyze GTP inducing the release of a phosphate: when GTP is hydrolyzed, a GDP is obtained. Tubulin dimers are bound to a GTP molecule and in this condition they are able to interact more tightly with each other: whenever there are interactions between GTP and tubulin dimers there is the grow of the microtubule. GTP is then hydrolyzed to GDP and when tubulin is bounded to GDP, it has less affinity to from dimers and consequently, tubulin dimers start to be lost by the microtube itself (shrinking). Ther rate of addition of new dimers can slow down: GTPase activity becomes faster than the rate at which dimers are added and this causes the shrinking of the microtubule. Dynamic instability is vital in mitosis: at a certain point, chromosomes must be segregated; since they are attached to microtubules, when they shrink, they bring chromosomes with them. 

Microtubules organize the intracellular disposi ti on of organelles and the transport of vesicles; moreover, they interact with motor proteins, that move along microtubules transporting a cargo. There are two types of motor proteins: 

1. **kinesins,** which move towards the plus end of microtubules. 

2. **dyneins,** which move towards the minus end. 

Microtubules can also form **cilia** (hair-like structures, covered by plasma membrane, that extends from the surface of eukaryotic cells) and **flagellum.** Cilia contain a core of stable microtubules, arranged in a bundle, that grow from a cytoplasmic basal body, which serves as an organizing center. Cilia move in a whip-like fashion, either to move fluid aver the surface of a cell orto propel single cells through a fluid. 

Flagella are much like cilia in their internal structure but are usually very much longer. They move the entire cell, rather than moving fluid across the cell surface. Microtubules in cilia and flagella are different from cytoplasmic microtubules: they are arranged in nine doublets around a pair of single microtubules. 

Drugs can affect microtubules' function and organization: **colchicine** binds tightly to free tubulin dimers and prevents their polymerization into microtubules, so the mitotic spindle rapidly disappears, and the cell stalls in the middle of mitosis, unable to partition the chromosomes. This treatment can be used to stop cell proliferation and division in cancer cells. 

22 

Another drug with similar effect but different mechanism is **taxol,** which binds to filaments and prevents their depolymerization, so microtubules cannot be disrupted. It is the apposite mechanism compared to that of colchicine, but the eff ect is the same. 

### _Actin filaments_ 

Actin filaments are polymers made of **globular actin,** present in all eukaryotic cell, involved in cell movement, and interacting with actin-binding proteins that stabilize them. Actin-dependent movements usually require actin's association with a motor protein called **myosin.** 

Each filament is formed of a globular structure ( actin filaments) which has a **polarity** (like microtubules): theoretically, growth can occur at both the plus and minus ends, but it is faster at the former. When the concentration of actin is quite high within the cell, the growth occurs at the plus end. Actine filaments are **dynamic** structures: both growth and shrinkage can occur, and this kind of activity (similar to that of microtubules) is called **treadmilling activity** (the filament is able to grow atone end and shrink at the other). 

Actin is bounded to an ATP molecule (not GTP like in microtubules ): monomers of actin free in the cell are tightly bounded to an ATP molecule and in this conformation, they can interact with each other forming a growing filament, where ATP is hydrolyzed, ADP is formed, and there is the release of a phosphate group. When actin is bounded to ADP there is loss of stability in the unit. By blocking the dynamic activity of actin filaments, all their activities within the cells are blocked. 

When **phalloidin** - and actin-specific drug - binds to actin filaments, it prevents their polymerization, and this molecule is found in some kind of toxic mushrooms which can be deathly if eaten. This drug is used when studying some cellular processes in labs, in order to block specific pathways and observe the specimen in a steady state. 

As for microtubules, there are cases when we want to stabilize actin filaments: microtubules are the highway of the celi on top of which vesicles are transported and proteins are able to stabilize them by connecting their tips to the plasma membrane. In some processes, dynamic instability of actin filaments is important, but sometimes they need to be stabilized, for example in the cell cortex, a layer beneath the plasma membrane enriched with actin filaments which provide support and mechanical strength thanks to interaction of actin filaments with other proteins that anchor the filaments to the plasma membrane. 

Another important activity of actin is being involved in cell movements, but also in **muscular contraction.** Some cells are able to move by physically crawling over surfaces: for example, neutrophils or monocytes, which normally flow in the blood, during inflammation are able to extravasate from the blood to the infected tissue, finding the pathogen and destroying it. They do so thanks to actin treadmilling activity: at the site of infection there is the release of chemicals ( chemokines or cytokines) that attract the white blood cell (receptors on its surface recognize them) and this interaction activates a cascade of events, that causes a rearrangement of actin filaments. There is the formation of specific structures, called **lamellipodia** and **ftlopodia,** enriched in actin filaments. The cell membrane forms a protrusion and new contacts between cell membrane and surface on which the cell is crawling: new points of anchorage are created, together with an interaction between extracellular matrix and celi membrane, and at a certain potin the cell contracts the rear part thanks to interaction with myosin motor proteins, 

**23** 

which move the cell forward. Thanks to this process white blood cells are able to chase the bacteria and engorge them. 

Whenever there is the extension of lamellipodia and filopodia ( exploratory structures that form and retract with great speed, generated by the local growth of actin filaments) the plasma membrane can adhere to surfaces thanks to **transmembrane proteins** (like integrin) that bind to other proteins on the extracellular matrix orto other neighboring cells. 

The movement mediated by actin is possible thanks to mo tor proteins, belonging to the family of myosin proteins, which bind to and hydrolyze ATP, providing the energy for their movement along actin. Myosin II is present in muscle cells and mediates muscular contraction, while myosin I is present in all cells and mediates cellular movement. 

24 

## **Proteins** 

Proteins are the **main building blocks** of cells, being responsible for carrying out most cell functions, even though the information to produce them is encoded in DNA. By considering the chemical content of a cell, about 70% ofvolume is represented by water and 30% by chemicals, 50% ofwhich is formed of proteins, indicating their key role in cell activity. 

### _Function_ 

Proteins' function is related to their shape: indeed, most of the time the ability of a protein to carry out specific functions depends on their tridimensional structure. Proteins are complex and functionally sophisticated, and this can be observed by looking at their structure, which includes **four levels of complexity:** primary, secondary, tertiary and quaternary structure. 

Proteins have many functions: 

- **Enzymes** catalyze chemical reactions and speed them up. DNA polymerase is responsible for forming the phosphodiester bond during DNA replication, while pepsin degrades dietary proteins in the stomach. 

- **Structural proteins** provide mechanical support to cells and tissues: actin forms filaments that support the plasma membrane, while keratin forms fibers that reinforce cells. 

- **Transportation proteins** carry small molecules or ions: hemoglobin carries oxygen in the bloodstream, and glucose carriers shuttle glucose into and out of cells. 

- **Motor proteins** generate movement in cells and tissues: myosin in skeletal muscle cells provides the force to move, while kinesin interacts with microtubules to move organelles around the cell. 

- **Storage proteins** are able to bind specific amino acids and ions, therefore creating a storage available for the cell's need: ferritin stores iron in the liver, while casein in milk is a source of amino acids for baby mammals. 

- **Signaling proteins** carry extracellular signals from cell to cell: insulin controls glucose levels in the blood, while nerve growth factor stimulates some types of nerve cells to grow axons. 

- **Receptor proteins** are able to bind to signal proteins to detect and decode the information contained in the signal molecules: rhodopsin in the retina detects light, while insulin receptor allows cells to respond to the hormone insulin by taking up glucose. 

- **Regulatory proteins** bind to DNA and are able to activate or deactivate transcription of genes (transcription factors bind to DNA and regulate which genes will be transcribed): lactose repressor in bacteria silences the genes for the enzymes that degrade the sugar lactose, while transcription factors control gene expression. 

- **Defensive proteins** participate in the protection of our body: antibodies are produced by B lymphocytes. 

- **Special purpose proteins** have highly variable functions: antifreeze proteins in Artic and Antarctic fish prevent their blood from freezing, while green fluorescent proteins injelly fish emit a green light. 

25 

#### _Structure_ 

Proteins can be classified in **simple** proteins ( composed only of amino acids) or **modified** proteins ( which include not only amino acids but also prosthetic groups ). Some proteins can be modified and can carry sugar groups on top of them: they are known as **glycoproteins.** Proteins can also be modified by the addition oflipids **(lipoproteins).** Different kinds of modifications can occur: hemoproteins carry a heme group, found in hemoglobin proteins, where the presence of the heme group is very important for the interaction with oxygen. 

Proteins are polymers of **amino acids,** which are their main constituents. Amino acids are linked to each other through **peptide bonds.** Proteins can be variable in size, structure, and function: they reach between 30 and 10.000 amino acids (but generally 200). 

An amino acid has a simple structure: it is always possible to recognize an **amino terminal** (with an amino group) a **carboxyl terminal** (with a carboxyl group) and an a-carbon to which a side chain is bound. The nature of the side chain distinguishes amino acids because it gives them physical and chemical properties, which are specific to each amino acid. Amino acids can be visualized with different models: 

- **Ball-and-stick** model: each amino acid is represented as a ball and stick; atoms are balls and bonds are sticks, and each atom is colored differently (Carbon is black; Oxygen is red; Hydrogen is white, Nitrogen is blue). This model provides an idea of the tridimensional orientati on of amino acids within the space. 

- **Space filling** model. 

The a-carbon is **chiral:** there are four different molecules bound to it and each amino acid can be present in two forms, which are mirror images of each other and are not superimposable. These are called stereoisomers andare L and D-isomers. All enzymes able to work on proteins recognize the L isomer and not the D isomer, so we are not able to perform reactions using D isomers.
