There are 20 amino acids which can be Tyrosine Tyr y uncharged polar Cysteine Cys e nonpolar described with a three-letter code or by POLAR AMINO ACIDS L___ NONPOLAR AMINO ACIDS _J a single letter (alanine =ala= A). 

There are two additional amino acids, which can be synthetized in specific conditions: selenocysteine (Sec) and pyrrolysine (Pyl), present only in some proteins. 

26 

Amino acids are linked together by peptide bonds, which have a partial double-bond structure: the length ofthe band (distance between Carbon and Nitrogen) is in between the values expected far a single band and a double band. This means that the band between Carbon and Nitrogen is quite **rigid** and localized within a **planar** piane, so the molecule is not able to rotate along its axis, and Carbon and Nitrogen are localized within the same piane. The piane of the peptide band is fixed, but there may be rotation of atoms linked to a-Carbon: the rotation angles are called phi or psi and are important when describing the structure of the protein. 

### _Levels of organization_ 

Proteins are very complicated from a structural point of view: we can define a primary, secondary, tertiary and quatemary structure, each characterized by different features. 

The **primary structure** of a protein is the sequence of amino acids and if we consider that we can have 20 different amino acids, the possible combinations of amino acids and therefare the possible sequences are enormous (20n, where n is the length of the protein). However, not all combinations occur, since proteins have been selected to carry out specific functions: ofthe whole possible combinations, there are just few which have been selected by evolution. Even though the primary structure defines the sequence of amino acids, the major structure of a protein may differ because it can be modified during the maturation process. 

Far example, the insulin protein is synthesized as pre-pro-insulin which is further modified befare being secreted in the extemal environment: the protein carries a signal peptide which directs it to a compartment within the cell, where it undergoes further modification that enables it to obtain the actual farm of insulin. Insulin is produced by the isle of Langerhans and in particular by B-cells: once it is synthesized by the translation process, it undergoes further modification in the Golgi apparatus, to give rise to the major farm of insulin, released extracellularly. 

A certain **degree of freedom** in proteins is allowed: the sequence of DNA in each human being contains many differences, localized in different parts of the genome, even in **genes** (structures read by the transcription and translation machines ). **Polymorphism** in genomes allow far these differences, which usually do not impact protein activity, but sometimes can be pathogenic: some mutations within DNA sequences can lead to the substitution of a particular amino acid at the protein level, and the protein may not be able to perfarm its function. Insulin is important in the metabolism of glucose and if does not work well diabetes can be developed: sometimes insulin is produced but is not recognized by its receptors, while sometimes it is not 100% functional. 

The amino acid sequence also determines the **tridimensional structure** of the protein (final confarmation), which is strictly connected to its function. When a protein fails to get a specific shape, it will not be able to perform its function. A protein is able to fald by itself. 

We can describe a protein on the basis of its **secondary structure,** characterized by the presence of regular pattern confarmations, stabilized by Hydrogen bonds 

27 

formed within the backbone of amino acids (between the amino group and the carboxyl group ). There are two main types of secondary structures: 

- **P-strands:** Hydrogen bonds form between the amino group and the carboxyl group of an adjacent strand. There can be a conformation where the B-strands can either run parallel to each other or have an antiparallel conformation. The structure is stabilized by the formation of Hydrogen bonds between adjacent B-strands. 

- **a-helix:** the carboxyl group of a certain amino acid forms a Hydrogen bond with the amino group of an amino acid which is four positions away. This interaction does not involve side chains. a-helixes are enriched with proteins able to span the membrane (transmembrane proteins): the hydrophilic part of polypeptide backbone forms hydrogen bonds at the interior of the helix, while the hydrophobic side chains of amino acids are exposed to the phospholipid tails. About twenty amino acids are required to span a membrane. Sometimes two or more _a_ helix wrap around each other ( at the presence of hydrophobic chains, so that they are localized within the interior) and forma coiledcoil structure. 

**hydrophobic amino acid sidechain** 

Secondary structure elements can combine in specific geometrie phospholipid in lipid bilayer arrangements known as **motifs** or super secondary structures: they include helix-turn-helix, B-turn (hairpin), Greek key, and _B-a-B._ They can be found in proteins different from each other, but which perform the same function. 

Some protein regions lack any definite structure. These intrinsically disordered sequences are often found as short stretches linking domains in otherwise highly ordered proteins. Other proteins instead are almost entirely without secondary structure and exist as unfolded polypeptide chains in the cytosol. The **unstructured sequences** have several functions within the cell: 

- they can wrap around target proteins like a scarf, binding with both high specificity and low affinity. 

- they provide **flexibility** while increasing the frequency of encounters between the domains. 

- they can help **scaffold proteins** bring together proteins in an intracellular signaling pathway, facilitating interactions. 

- they also give proteins like elastin the ability to form rubberlike fibers, allowing our tendons and skin to recoil after being stretched. 

When looking at proteins, it is possible to visualize them in different ways, through: 

- the backbone model, with just the backbone of polypeptide chains. 

- the ribbon model, with only the secondary structure 

28 

- the wire model, where side chains are depicted too. 

- the space-filling model, showing which part of the protein is exposed to the surface, and represents it as a solid sphere. 

The **tertiary structure** is the final shape that the protein gets and is determined by a series ofweak noncovalent bonds that form within the protein and can involve the backbone, the side chain or both. There are several types ofweak interactions: 

- hydrogen bonds. 

- electrostatic interactions, when dealing with charged amino acids. 

- hydrophobic interactions, which tend to cluster hydrophobic amino acids within an environment that is the least possible exposed to water. Polar or charged amino acids are instead localized on the surface to interact with water. 

- Van der Wall attractions. 

- disulfide bonds, established between sulfur atoms and formed during the processing of the protein: enzymes in the ER perform the formati on of this particular covalent bond, important for the maintenance of the protein structure. 

The tertiary structure corresponds to the 3D shape of the protein, and it is connected to its function: only when it reaches its final conformation, the protein is able to function and if it is not folded correctly, it may not function or damage the cell, since badly folded proteins cluster together and precipitate in the cytoplasm and kill the cell. Sometimes protein aggregate when they are not able to reach their final conformation, and this can contribute to neurogenerative diseases, such as **Alzheimer's** disease and **Huntington's** disease. 

It is the primary structure the main determinant of the protein shape: by considering a protein shaped in its 3D conformation and denaturing it, it loses its fmal conformation, but when the denaturant agents are taken off, the protein is able to fold back. This proves that the information needed to reach this conformation only depends on the sequence of amino acids. The final conformation is characterized by the best energetic considerations, and it is the one that minimizes the energy. 

In the cells there are helpers for protein folding, called **chaperone proteins,** which help them find their conformation: they are important players because the cell is filled with molecules, so the cell needs help to be sure not to interact with other molecules before time. Chaperone proteins assist the proteins to reach the tridimensional conformation. 

The **quaternary structure** is characteristic of those proteins formed of two or more peptides, called subunits. Tue hemoglobin protein, important for the transport of Oxygen in cells, is a tetramer, which means that it is composed of four subunits (two _a_ and two ~) which all participate in the function of the final hemoglobin protein. 

Each polypeptide can be defined starting from a primary structure (the sequence of amino acids we read going from the N-terminal to the C-terminal), a secondary structure, a tertiary structure (the 3D conformation), and- only for some proteins - a quatemary structure. 

Protein **domains** are stable units of the protein structure that can fold autonomously. Whitin a protein there are different parts with different functions: transcription factors, for example, interact with DNA 

29 

molecules on one side and with RNA polymerase on the other. These domains can fold by themselves autonomously and are characterized by different functions. Each domain forms a compact 3D structure (globular element) within a protein and has a variable size (25-50 amino acids ); each protein is composed of one or more domains which have **structural autonomy** (they might keep their structure when separated from the rest ofthe protein, for example by proteolysis) and **functional autonomy** (the domain by itself is sufficient to play specific functions ). 

The system evolved so that there are different proteins which share the same domains. 

30 

### **Nucleic Acids** 

The discovery ofDNA does not date back too much: the first experiment of DNA (which at the time was **"nuclein")** was performed in the 19<sup>th</sup> century. From its first discovery in 1869 (by Johann Friedrich **Miescher,** who isolated DNA from pus), almost a century passed before the acceptance that DNA was actually the genetic material. Scientists were having a hard time believing it because from a chemical point of view, DNA is a very simple molecule, so they tended to believe that proteins were the genetic material. Many experiments were carried out to demonstrate beyond any doubt that DNA is the genetic material. 

## _Johann Friedrich Miescher_ 

Miescher was the first one to isolate nuclein from the nucleus ofwhite blood cells: while carrying out all tests to identify the characteristics of the molecule, he found out that it was unlike any other protein, since it contained phosphorus, absent in proteins. 

After his discovery, other scientists took interest in his studies. Albrecht **Kossel** discovered that nuclein is composed of sugar and Nitrogen-containing rings he called bases. Moreover, Eduard **Zacharias** discovered that nuclein was key part of chromosomes, which were discovered to be composed of proteins too, and was the first to combine the concept of chromatin with nuclein. Proteins have a more structural role in compacting DNA, but at the time this was not clear, and it was hypothesized that proteins were the key to heredity. 

## _Frederick Griffith_ 

Frederick **Griffith** was interested in studying some kid of bacteria _(Streptococcus pneumoniae)_ responsible for pneumonia: two different strains of this bacterium exist, one benign I and one highly virulent and deathly (S). The R strain does not have a capsule and is benign because the immune system gets rid of it, whereas the S strain has a capsule, which helps the bacterium escape the immune system. Gri:ffith started by analyzing the S strains: he grew them and inoculated them in mice, which died. He then repeated the experiment with R strains and observed that the infection led to no particular consequences and mice survived. 

He heated the S strain and killed the bacteria contained in the culture, so when he injected the dead bacteria in mice, they lived. He then repeated the same experiment and mixed the dead virulent bacterium with the live benign one, he grew the mixture, and injected it, observing that mice died. When he extracted the bacteria from dead mice, he saw that they belonged to the virulent type, which in the beginning was dead. These experiments showed that somehow there was a specific substance that allowed the transformation of the alive benign bacteria into the virulent one, and this transformation was permanent. Griffith's experiment showed that a **transformation** can happen, is **permanent, inheritable,** but still he didn't know what the transforming principle able to change the genetic material ofthe benign strain was. We now know that the virulent factors are transported in **plasmids** ( accessory chromosomes which are circular molecules ), which can be transmitted from a bacterium to another. 

## _Avery, MacLeod & McCarty_ 

**Avery, MacLeod and McCarty** in 1944 fractioned S strains extract into different molecules (RNA, proteins, DNA, carbohydrates and lipids), aiming at testing their ability to transform R non-virulent 

31 

bacteria into S virulent ones. They observed that the test subjects always survived unless they were treated with nucleic acids, which meant that the molecule responsible for transformation is the **DNA molecule.** However, the scientific community was skeptical about this discovery. 

### _Hershey and Chase_ 

The **blender experiment** performed by **Hershey** and **Chase** in 1952, finally proved that DNA is the genetic material. They started by studying the T2 phage, a virus which has bacteria as targets and is composed ofjust proteins and DNA: it has a very peculiar structure and can adhere on the surface ofthe target cell and act as a molecular syringe, injecting genetic material into the host cell, while everything else just remains on its surface. Hershey and Chase exploited the chemical differences between proteins and DNA: DNA is highly enriched with phosphates, while phosphorus is absent in proteins, which instead can contain sulfur. By using radioactive sulfur and phosphorus, they grew viruses and infected the bacterium with either the virus tagged with radioactive sulfur or radioactive phosphorus; with a blender they sheared off the empty capsids (phage's heads) from the surface of the bacterium. After that, the samples were centrifuged at high speed and since bacteria are heavier than viral particles, they can be separated by centrifugation: the bacteria pellet at the bottom of the container, while the phage's head remains in the supematant. When they analyzed the batch where proteins were radioactively tagged, they found their traces in the supematant (phage's head), while when they analyzed the batch where DNA was radioactively tagged, they found its traces in the bacteria, indicating that DNA was what it was injected into the bacteria by the virus. 

### _Nucleotides_ 

Nucleic acids are polymers of **nucleotides,** which are characterized by the presence of a sugar, a base and one or more phosphate groups. In DNA the sugar is **deoxyribose,** a pentose sugar in which each carbon is numbered with a prime. The sugar is linked to a base (Nitrogen-containing ring) by a glycosidic bond and to one or more phosphate groups by an ester bond. 

In RNA the structure of the nucleotide is the same, but the pentose sugar is **ribose,** which carries a hydroxide group in position 2', while deoxyribose carries a hydrogen in the same position. 

## There are two main classes ofbases: 

- **pyrimidines,** characterized by the presence of a single ring, and to which Thymine (present only in DNA), Uracil (present only in RNA) and Cytosine (present in both DNA and RNA) belong. Thymine has a methyl group: the addition of a methyl group to a base is very expensive from an energetic point of view, so the cell uses Thymine only for long storage molecules. Uracil is identica! to Thymine but lacks the methyl group: therefore, Uracil is cheaper to produce and can be used in many RNA molecules, which are **transient** (last in the cytoplasm only a few minutes, so it does not make sense to use so much energy to produce something that will be destroyed soon after). Cytosine undergoes **spontaneous deamination** and when it loses its amino group it is transformed into Uracil. DNA should be stable, since it must remain the same throughout the life of the cell, so if spontaneous deamination occurred without limitations, we would end up with many Cytosines being transformed into Uracil. This means that ifDNA contained Uracil instead of Thymine, we could end up introducing many mutations in our genetic material, and indeed, 

32 

the use ofThymine is a way to avoid it: whenever Cytosine undergoes spontaneous deamination the cell has surveillance systems that recognize the presence of Uracil instead of Cytosine, and since Uracil is nota DNA base, the nucleotide is removed and substituted with a new Cytosine. 

- **purines,** characterized by a double ring. They include Adenine and Guanine, both present in RNA andDNA. 

Nucleotides are linked to each other by **phosphodiester bonds,** created by enzymes. The bond is established in a specific orientation: the phosphate of one nucleotide is connected to the sugar of another nucleotide and this linkage is called 5' to 3' because the 5' Carbon of the first nucleotide is connected to the 3' Carbon of the second nucleotide. The formation of this linkage determines the formation of a polynucleotide chain, with a constant backbone made of sugar and bases. Phosphates are negatively charged, and this makes both RNA and DNA negatively charged too. 

lt was Erwin **Chargaff** the first one to note that the amount of Adenine and Guanine (A +G) and that of Cytosine and Thymine (C+T) was constant in a species (human cells coming from different tissues have this constant sum). Moreover, the quantity of Adenine is equal to that of Thymine (A=T) and the quantity of Cytosine is equal to that of Guanine (C=G). These observations were important in determining the structure of DNA molecule. 

_Watson and Crick: the discovery of DNA s structure_ 

In 1963 **Watson and Crick** managed to identify the structure of DNA as a **double helix** and this discovery marked the beginning of the era of molecular biology. Watson and Crick were studying at Cambridge University, trying to understand the structure of DNA and to put together a model. The work of Rosalind **Franklin** was fundamental to accomplish this, since she performed the **crystallography** studies which enabled Watson and Crick to create their model, but died at the age of thirty-seven for cancer and was not awarded the Nobel prize, which instead was given to Watson, Crick, and Wilkins. 

X rays are used to analyze crystal samples: X rays hit the atoms of the sample, which scatters the rays, giving rise to a specific pattern, detected by photographic or electronic devices. The diffraction 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
DNA double helix<br>3'<br>sugar-phosphate<br>backbone --<br>5'<br>3'<br><!-- End of picture text -->

pattern can be analyzed because every spot gives information about the position of the atoms within the molecule. In this way, it was possible to understand that DNA is structured as a double helix and to determine the distance between the bases. Starting from a picture it was possible to understand important characteristics of DNA. 

The observations made by Chargaffwere very helpful too: since DNA is a double helix, the two strands are kept together by interactions (hydrogen bonds) between the bases. Cytosine and Guanine are held together by three hydrogen bonds, while Adenine and Thymine by two, so Cytosine and Guanine are kept together more tightly. 

33 

The double helix is charncterized by the fact that the strands run **antiparallel** to each other, so they run with an opposite orientation. The backbone of DNA, composed of sugars and phosphates, represents the external part of the helix, while bases are situated on the inside allowing the two strands to tightly internet with each other. Tue double helix is **asymmetrical,** so it is possible to distinguish a major and a minor groove, which are the sides where proteins internet with DNA (mainly on the major groove). 

The structure of 'NA implicates a specific mechanism for its replication. 

## _RNAvsDNA_ 

In the RNA molecule there is just a **single strand,** but this does not mean that the molecule is linear: bases can pair within the same RNA molecule forming a peculiar tridimensional shape. Because of this, the RNA molecule can be folded in a specific structure which reminds to the tridimensional structure of proteins, and actually there are some RNA molecules which play the same role of proteins, by performing: 

- **Catalytic** functions, like **ribozymes,** which play an important role in trnnslation. 

- **Structural** functions. 

- **Regulatory** functions, and in this case, it is of great importance that RNA molecules get a specific shape to regulate various processes. 

Sometimes RNA molecules can internet by forming non-canonica! internctions, with more points of contact. 

The RNA world includes not only the **messenger RNA** (intermediate between genome and proteins), but also different types of RNA molecules, known as **non-coding RNAs,** which are the majority of RNA molecules found within the cell. Non-coding RNAs are involved in processes such as **translation** (rRNA, tRNA), or **regulatory** and **structural** functions. RNA has a possible role in the development of life: indeed, it is thought that the first molecule with real importance for the development of life was RNA. Before the appearance ofthe first cell with DNA, the world transitioned through a phase ofRNA, which had the function ofboth storing genetic information and catalyzing biochemical reactions. Nucleic acids are fundamental for protein synthesis, but proteins are required for the synthesis of nucleic acids: it is thus an interdependent world which makes it difficult to say which carne first. However, RNAs are molecules which in ancient world could play both roles: they had the ability to reproduce themselves, to internet with each other so that a final aim could be reached, and to compete with other systems for the same rnw materials (whenever deprived ofthese rnw materials, the system would decay to death). 

RNA molecules appeared before DNA ones since the deoxyribose in DNA is harder to be synthesized and is produced starting from ribose. When DNA was produced, it became the **permanent repository** of **genetic information,** since being a double helix, it is more stable and easier to fix without losing any information. The catalytic role was taken up by proteins, charncterized by great diversity and heterogeneity. RNA is now an **intermediate** between DNA and proteins, but it also has a structural and regulatory role. RNA is also able to act as a **template** for the formation of another strand: indeed, the base paring can be used as a model to replicate nucleic acids. Moreover, RNA molecules also catalyze the synthesis of other RNA molecules. 

34 

### **Chromatin and chromosomes** 

The genetic materiai is huge compared to the size of the cell, since we need a large amount of DNA to encode all the proteins required for the cell to work. In humans, DNA extends for about **two meters,** but it is allocated in a very small piace, since the nucleus of an average cell is about 5-8 micrometers. Specialized proteins are able to bind and fold DNA, generating a very compact structure. The main difference between eukaryotes and prokaryotes is that the former have a nucleus while the other lack it and the organization of the **genome** is different. 

### _Prokaryotic cells_ 

Prokaryotes do not have a nucleus, but the genetic materiai is concentrated in a region called **nucleoid,** which is not enclosed by any membrane. In bacterial cells there are two main kinds ofmolecules: 

- The **genomic DNA:** in prokaryotes the DNA is a **circular** molecule, quite small compared to that of humans, and made of about 5 million bases. 

- The **plasmid DNA,** consisting of circular DNAmolecules, but smaller (100-200k nucleotides). 

The genomic DNA is always present and contains genes **vital** for the life ofthe cell (rRNA genes, genes for the generai metabolism, and housekeeping genes ), whereas plasmid DNA can be absent and variable: indeed, it consists of genes that encode for particular, non-essential features, which can grant an **advantage** in particular situations, like drug resistance, toxic compounds resistance, additional metabolic abilities. Plasmids can be transmitted **horizontally** (within the same colony, environment), and this is a way for plasmids to spread. **Fertility plasmids** encode for proteins able to forma bridge **(pilus)** between two bacteria, which allows the exchange of plasmids; **killer plasmids** contain genes that synthetize toxins which kill other bacteria; **degradative plasmids** encode for enzymes important for the metabolism of unusual molecules; **virulence plasmids** increase the pathogenicity ofthe bacterium. 

Plasmids are important tools in molecular biology and are widely used in labs because they are easy to modify and engineer: they can be used to convince the cell to express the gene they carry, in arder to analyze in more details a certain protein. Nowadays some plasmids - small, carrying the gene of interest - can be created in the lab and cut using restriction enzymes that allow a cut-and-paste strategy. 

**p Uc18** consists of a gene that encodes for **antibiotic resistance,** a region called **multicloning side** (where the gene of interest will be put), a marker for the selection of the colony, and a point of origin of replication. A DNA of interest is produced, amplified through molecular biology techniques, and then put within the plasmid, by using a specific enzyme **(ligase):** a **recombinant vector** is obtained, which is a plasmid containing the gene of interest. The recombinant vector is then produced in great quantities by inserting it in bacteria and making them reproduce. Afterwards, the plasmid can be introduced in a mammalian cell and studied; different types of analysis can be used to visualize and study the function of the protein, under specific cellular conditions which can be replicated in labs. 

Even though the main genome of prokaryotes is smaller than that of eukaryotic cells, there is stilla **fitting problem:** the genome of E. Coli has a circumference of 1.6 mm, but the cell is only 1-2 micrometers. Prokaryotes fix the problem in two ways: through interactions with **specialized proteins,** and by exploiting the mechanism of **supercoiling,** which occurs when the number of tums in the double helix is modified. Imagine having a rope, composed of two strands twisted around each other: by opening up the 

35 

rope on one side, and keeping the other side closed, the end of the rope is shortened, and tortional stress is introduced. lf the other end was let free, no supercoiling would form, since the tortional stress would be released. When tortional stress in DNA is introduced, it cannot be released, unless the molecule is broken; two enzymes are specialized in this activity **(DNA gyrase** and **Topoisomerase,** and no materiai is lost), since the double helix remains wound, and can be repristinated by ligase in case of breaks. The molecule ls then attached to a protein core, from which severa! supercoiling radiate into the cell, forming loops that resemble a flower. Each loop contains about 100 kilo bases and is a structural domain of the bacterial genome. The protein core comprises DNA gyrase, topoisomerase I, and other proteins (HU) that are involved in the packaging, acting in a way similar to histones. 

By introducing a **single strand break,** also called **"nick",** it is possible to pass from a supercoiling structure to a more relaxed one, and by doing it on a loop of the molecule we observe that only the loop where the nick has been introduced is relaxed, while the rest of the loop remains supercoiled. This happens because DNA interacts with proteins and is not really free to move around: proteins in the middle block the DNA molecules, so the relaxation movement is limited to the loop where the nick is present. This is important for the transcription process: if DNA is in a packed conformation, it is not accessible by the transcription machinery, so some loops must be relaxed to perform transcription.

<!-- Start of picture text -->

This organization resolves the fitting problem and is **dynamic,** since it is possible to pass from the supercoiled conformation to the relaxed one allowing transcription and then translation. DNA is attached to proteins which restrict its ability to relax: to demonstrate it we can treat the genomic sample with proteases ( enzymes able to destroy proteins) and introduce a nick. We would observe that the whole molecule relaxes, while, when we do not use proteases, only one loop is relaxed. The domain structure is important to combine two apposite needs: fitting the genome in the cell and being able to relax certain domains when we need to perform transcription. 

### _Eukaryotic cell_ 

In eukaryotic cells the situation is more complicated: the length of the DNA molecule is much bigger compared to the prokaryotic genome, and in humans the DNA contains three billion nucleotides, divided into **23** or **24** different types of **chromosomes.** The human body contains on average 50 trillion cells, so there are 100 trilli on meters of DNA per human. 

36 

In the nucleus of eukaryotes, the DNA is distributed among a set of different chromosomes, each consisting of a single, enormously long, linear DNA molecule associated with proteins. The complex of DNA and proteins is called **chromatin.** 

A **karyotype** is a description of the number and the appearance (in terms of size, shape) of chromosomes in the nucleus of the eukaryotic cell and is distinctive for each species: in humans there are **46 chromosomes,** organized in 23 pairs ofhomologous chromosomes, where 22 ofthem are equal in both males and female and then there is the **sex couple** which differs according to the gender. 

The DNA molecule interacts with a series of proteins, especially **histone proteins.** 

When talking about chromatin, it is possible to recognize: 

- **Euchromatin,** which is loosely packed (it has lower level of packaging), is lightly stained (with Giemsa staining) in the karyotype ( since transcription is continuously occurring) and contains transcriptionally active ( expressed) genes. 

- **Heterochromatin,** which is characterized by higher degree of packaging, is darkly stained (with Giemsa staining) in the karyotype and contains transcriptionally inactive genes (not expressed, they do not internet with RNA polymerase ). It can be subdivided into: 

   - a. **Constitutive heterochromatin** (regions always in a compact form, usually the centromere, corresponding to centromeric and telomeric DNA) 

   - b. **Facoltative heterochromatin** ( only in specific tissues, corresponding to genes expressed in certain cell types). 

Each tissue has a specific transcriptomic signature: genes are always present in the DNA of every cell, but not all of them are expressed. 

The basic structural unit of chromatin is represented by the nucleus, firstly described in the 70s; two observations can be made: 

- The first result that led to the discovery of nucleosome was the observation that if we treat chromosomes with nucleases (which degrade DNA molecule but not proteins), fragments of specific lengths are obtained. Chromatin ( complex of DNA and proteins) is treated with nucleases, which theoretically chop the DNA in a random way; after the chromatin is digested with nucleases, it is possible to extract DNA and run it on gel **electrophoresis,** a generai biology technique which allows to separate DNA fragments on the bases oftheir length. A gel matrix of **agarose** is created, containing pores through which DNA molecules are able to pass when the matrix is put in an electric field. DNA - being negatively charged - tends to run towards the positive pale and we can discriminate the length of DNA fragments, since the smaller ones can go through pores with no problems, while bigger fragments more slowly. By performing this kind of experiment, we observe that fragments migrate with a specific pattern, where each band visible in the gel corresponds to DNA fragments of a specific size, more or less multiple of 200 nucleotides. This is peculiar, because by treating naked DNA (not complexed with proteins ), with nucleases, we would expect it to be cut randomly, resulting in a smeared appearance; instead, a **pattern** is observed, and by letting the chromatin be digested for more time, we end up with obtaining only one band of 200 nucleotides inside. 

37 

- By looking at chromatin through **electromicroscopy,** we observe beads and stretches connecting these beads: when the structure is digested with nuclease, nuclease cuts easily the liker DNA (the part that links two beads with each other). By letting nuclease digest fora long time, we could give enough time to the enzyme to cut where every linker part is present. 

These two observations (structure and digestion of chromatin) made scientists hypothesize that beads are the part of DNA interncting with proteins, while strings link one bead with the following. Nowadays, beads are called **histone octamers** (protein structures around which the DNA is bound) and strings are charncterized by **naked DNA** (which does not internet with proteins) which connects histone octamers. Ifthe structure is this, it is easier for the nuclease to access the linker part than to access the part ofDNA coupled with proteins, and this is why we were always observing fragments ofDNA ofthe same length. 

The first level of packaging of DNA Is represented by **nucleosome,** composed of eight histone proteins interncting with stretch of double-strnnded DNA, 147 nucleotide pairs long, that winds around this histone octamer. A nucleosome 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
core histones  ~<br>of nucleosome  histone  147-nucleotide-pair<br>octamer  DNA double helix<br>*<br>"beads-on-a-string" nucleosome includes  DISSOCIATION<br>form of chromatin  -200 nucleotide<br>,  pairs of DNA<br>H2A  H2B  H3  H4<br><!-- End of picture text -->

particle consists of a complex 

of eight histone proteins (octamer): H2A, H2B, H3, H4, each present in a copy. By decomposing the octamer, we would obtain two subunits of H2A, H2B, H3, H4. 

The union of octamers and DNA double helix gives rise to the nucleosome. By considering the length of DNA wrnpped around the histones and the length on avernge of a linker DNA, we end up with a 200nucleotide stretched DNA. 

The nucleosome structure enables to compact the DNA molecule to about 1/3 of its initial length. Histones are quite small and enriched with positively charged amino acids: this is a fundamental charncteristic, since DNA is negatively charged, and the best way to make it wrap around proteins is to use proteins with high content of positively charged amino acids. Each histone is charncterized by **tails,** unstructured regions that extend out of the nucleosome pores, and are important to form more complex structures and to compact the double helix even more. Tails are indeed subjected to many kinds of chemical modifications: groups can be added to the tail in certain positions (methyl groups and acetyl groups, for example) and specific combinations have a functional meaning to the cell (histone code), since they are recognized by enzymes able to modify the chromatin structure. **Methylation** of a specific position of a certain histone tail recruits modifying enzymes that silence the transcription, while **acetylation** recruits enzymes which internet with RNA polymernse, making it easier for the region to be trnnscribed ( opposite effect). Once a region is established with a specific signature, it spreads to the nearby chromosomes, since this modification recruits modifying enzymes that induce a more complex packaging of a specific region. Some sequences form an isolated sequence, which makes the spreading ofheterochromatin stop, restricting it to a specific area. 

Nucleosomes can be further packed on top of each other to form the more compacted **chromatin fiber:** This additional packing depends on a fifth histone, histone Hl, which is thought to pull adjacent 

38 

nucleosomes together into a regular repeating array. It bends the DNA double helix in a specific way that allows histones to bend on top of each other, forming a more complex structure. 

Histones are **conserved among species,** so it possible to find almost the exact sequence of histones in the same species, and this shows the high significance of these proteins, which have a fundamental function within the cell. 

DNA bends of the top of histone pores, making about 1.65 turns around the histone octamer. There are some nucleotides which are most likely to be positioned within the DNA fragment wrapped around the nucleosome: segments enriched in Adenine and Thymine are more likely to be present in the minor groove (in the part of the double helix which tightly interacts with the histone octamers ). 

It is possible to pass from the naked molecule to a chromosome structure, and thanks to the presence of histone Hl and core histone tails, a **solenoid** structure of **30 nm** can be formed (more complicated), which allows to reach a condensation of about 100 folds. There can be several degrees of compaction which enable the DNA to reach a more compact form: the 30 nm structure can be further condensed, and the most condensed form of chromatin can be found during mitosis, to enable the correct distribution of genetic materiai into the daughter cell. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
30  Solenoid structure<br>nm<br>......  "'<br><!-- End of picture text -->



Specialized non-histone chromosomal proteins ( **clamp proteins)** fold the chromatin into a seri es of loops, which can be further compacted to produce the chromatin state visible during most of the celi life. This compact string of loops is thought to undergo at least one more leve I of packing to form the mitotic chromosome. 

The more the chromatin is packaged, the more difficult it is for the transcription materiai to access it. The level of packaging should be a compromise between the need to fit DNA in the nucleus and the need for the transcription complex to transcribe it. 

Apart from the housekeeping genes - those needed for the correct function of the cell - the others are specific for each cell type and are expressed only in certain conditions. 

39 

Depending on the cellular state, the DNA is packaged differently: at mitosis, it is possible to identify chromosomes, already duplicated (state of sister chromatids), while in interphase, chromosomes cannot be distinguished ( only heterochromatin - denser - can be visualized). 

Sometimes the chromatin structure must be remodeled, and some parts of DNA must be more accessible than others: if there is an extemal stimulus that induces the expression of a certain gene, chromatin must be remodeled so that the part of DNA encoding for that gene can be accessible to the transcription machine. This is possible thanks to **chromatin-remodeling complexes,** which (by using energy of ATP hydrolysis) change the position of DNA wrapped around nucleosomes, making certain parts more available than others. Chromatin's structure is dynamic, and the nucleus can be moved along the structure. Histones' tails are unstructured regions which can be further modified to harbor different kind of chemical modifications, and act as docking sites for a variety of regulatory proteins, which can promote chromatin condensation or decondensation.

**40** 

### **DNA replication** 

DNA replication occurs in the **nucleus** ( once the cell has divided) and is a very complex mechanism. _Theories_ 

Watson and Crick already had in mind a possible copy mechanism of the genetic material based on **base complementarity** and proposed a **semiconservative replication model:** by replicating the double helix, two strands are obtained, one of parental originandone of new synthesis. At the time (1950s), however, this model was not universally accepted, and other theories were formulated: the geneticist Max **Delbruck** believed that DNA was too long and too complicated and thought that a mechanism in which the two strands would unwind and be copied was impossible (physically unlikely and too inelegant to be efficient). Instead, he thought that the daughter molecules were a **patchwork** of DNA of new synthesis and of parental origin, and that the DNA was cut in small pieces (few nucleotides), which were copied, and which wouldjoin to others **(dispersive replication model).** 

According to the **conservative replication model,** when replicating DNA, we would end up with two daughter molecules, one completely of parental origin and one completely of new origin. 

### _Mese/son and Stahl_ 

**Meselson** and **Stahl** performed a very straightforward and clean experiment ("the most beautiful experiment in biology'') that proved that the replication of DNA was **semiconservative.** Meselson was studying a way to differentiate proteins of new and old synthesis, but talking with Stahl he thought the method he was using could somehow be adapted to nucleic acids. This method was based on a technique known as **density gradient centrifugation:** inside a test tube a density gradient of a specific substance (for example Cesium chloride - CsCl -) is created, and this means that it is possible to lay down the solution with an increasing density, then distribute the sample on top of the gradient, and apply centrifugation force. When the solution reaches equilibrium, molecules migrate to the region of gradient where their density matches that of the gradient. Meselson and Stahl exploited density gradient centrifugation coupled with the use of isotopes: they knew DNA molecule's bases are enriched with Nitrogen and thought of growing bacteria in two different conditions: in a medium containing<sup>14</sup> N, and in a medium containing<sup>15</sup> N (the isotope of<sup>14</sup> N), which has higher density. When opening the tube, isolating the DNA, and applying density gravity centrifugation, they were able to physically distinguish the DNA coming from the<sup>15</sup> N culture from that coming from the<sup>14</sup> N culture, because oftheir difference in density, which is visible since the band formed in the tube and corresponding to the regions where molecules can be found has different positions.<sup>15</sup> N is the heaviest form and is therefore at the bottom of the tube, while<sup>14</sup> N, which is lightest, is near the top. 

Then, bacteria grown for a few days in the heavy medium were transferred in the light medium, allowing one additional replication cycle: this means that when bacteria are transferred from the<sup>15</sup> N to the<sup>14</sup> N, the nucleic acid of new synthesis will be lighter compared to that of parental origin. According to the semiconservative theory, after applying the centrifugation, the DNA molecules should be half-way between the heavy and the light bands, since they are a mixture of parental and new origin molecule; this is what was expected also according to the dispersive model (since the daughter molecules are a mixture of new synthesis and parental origin). Meselson and Stahl actually got a band of intermediate weight, 

**41** 

which allowed them to rule out the conservative model, since according to it they would expect two bands, one corresponding to the parental DNA and one to the new synthesis DNA (lower band). 

They now had to figure out which of the two models (semiconservative or dispersive) was correct and used the **denaturation** technique, by heating the system and physically separating the two strands of the double helix. At this point, they repeated the experiment: after denaturation, they loaded DNA on the tubes and expected different results far the dispersive and semiconservative model. According to the latter two bands were expected, since one strand is of parental origin and one of new synthesis, and the scientists actually observed a band of higher density and one oflower density. According to the dispersive theory, instead, they should have seen a band of intermediate weight, since the new DNA molecule should be a patchwork between that of patemal origin and that of new synthesis. 

This experiment demonstrated that DNA replication is semiconservative. 

_DNA replication_ 

Each strand that composes the double helix can act as a template far the synthesis ofthe complementary strand: the double helix can be opened up and each strand can be copied, thus synthetizing a new strand respecting the base pairing. The replication is fast and accurate and in about eight hours billions of nucleotides can be copied. The farmation of **phosphodiester bonds** must be induced, and an enzyme must catalyze them by joining two nucleotides together. 

DNA replication involves different **enzymes** and **accessory proteins,** since it must be very precise to avoid the introduction of mutations. In arder to be copied, the double helix must be opened up and the strands must be separated thanks to interaction with **initiator proteins,** which recognize specific sequences ( called **replication origins)** enriched in Adenine and Thymine ( since they have less hydrogen bonds to be destroyed). Initiator proteins bind to the sequences, mediate the dissociation between the strands and the farmation of a **replication fork,** a region of DNA that replicates from a single origin. Bacteria and prokaryotes have only one replication origin, which is enough to copy their DNA in a reasonable amount oftime. Human genome instead has 10.000 replication origins (more or less 200 per chromosomes), which make it possible to diminish the time needed to replicate it. 

The replication fark expands bidirectionally: the DNA Is simultaneously replicated In both directions, so two replication farks are farmed at each replication origin, one moving to the left and one to the right. 

Once the two strands are separated, a mechanism to continuously separate the strands is needed and it is performed by an enzyme called **DNA helicase,** which is loaded onta the DNA, and which exploits the energy derived from ATP hydrolysis to move down the DNA and open it. Helicase has a ring shape and is able to grasp the DNA tightly and continuously unwind it. To maintain the two strands separated without interacting, **single strand binging proteins** can be used: they bind to the DNA backbone, without covering the bases, which remain exposed and can be used as a template far the new DNA. Therefare, SSBP allow the exposure ofthe bases and keep the strands apart. 

However, a **winding problem** occurs: when DNA helicase works its way through the DNA double helix, there is the farmation of **supercoiling,** since the tortional stress introduced while opening up the helix is not released, because chromosomes are very big and long and the molecule rotates tao slowly. This tension must be released, otherwise DNA helicase would not be able to proceed further, and therefare 

42 

there are other enzymes **(topoisomerase)** able to introduce single strand breaks (nicks) in the DNA backbone, allowing the rotation of the cleaved DNA strand along the DNA double helix, thus releasing the supercoiling, and allowing helicase to proceed.

The copying of the generic materiai Is performed by **DNA polymerase,** able to add nucleotides to a growing chain ( one of the problems connected to DNA synthesis, since it is not able to start it). There are different types of polymerases, involved in different processes, both for prokaryotes and eukaryotes. The adding of new nucleotides occurs with specific directionality: new nucleotides must be added to the 3' end of DNA (3' position not engaged in any bond), and the growing direction is **from 5'** (phosphate group not engaged in any bond) **to 3'.** This reaction requires energy, which is provided by the hydrolysis of the phosphoanhydride bonds (most energetic bond that keeps together the phosphate groups ): phosphate groups are hydrolyzed, one phosphate is maintained in the nucleotide, while pyrophosphate is let free, and the formati on of the phosphodiester bond can be catalyzed. 

DNA polymerase resembles a hand, with palm, fingers, and thumb involved in the grasping of DNA. This enzyme uses a single active site to catalyze the addition of all four deoxynucleotides triphosphate, and it can recognize the identica! geometry that characterizes the base pairs **A** + **T** and **G** + **C.** To understand if the right base paring occurs and to catalyze the phosphodiester bond, DNA polymerase verifies the steric hindrance, which derives from the base pairing. If there is not the right base pairing, the mismatched nucleotide cannot be positioned in the right way in the pocket of the enzyme and the reaction does not take piace. If the correct base pairing occurs, the double helix has the right conformation, and the phosphodiester reaction occurs. DNA polymerase is a highly **processive** enzyme, which means it can catalyze the addition of many nucleotides without releasing the substrate (it performs consecutive reactions without releasing the substrate ), so the replication can proceed fast. DNA polymerase can remain attached to its substrate (DNA) because it is helped by **sliding clams,** which are ring-shaped proteins able to keep the DNA polymerase tightly attached to its template. 

DNA polymerase is very good at adding the right nucleotide and at avoiding the adding of a ribonucleotide ( only deoxyribonucleotide can be positioned correctly in the enzyme pocket) and has a **proof-reading** activity, which is the ability to check if the previously added nucleotide is correctly base paired to the template strand. This makes DNA polymerase even more precise, and it commits an error 

43 

every 1 O million nucleotide: if the wrong nucleotide is added, there is a distorti on of the double helix, but DNA polymerase is able to detect this anomaly, clipping off the mispaired nucleotide and substituting it. 

The proof-reading activity explains the directionality of DNA polymerase: only adding nucleotides in a 5' to 3' fashion allows the proof-reading activity to take piace. Once the wrong nucleotide is removed, the synthesis can go on, but if the direction was the apposite ( chain growing in a 3' to 5' fashion), the chain 5' would finish with a nucleotide that still has a free phosphate and the addition of a new nucleotide would depend on the hydrolysis of that pyrophosphate in the growing chain. Moreover, after removing the wrong nucleotide, there would be a 5' chain which cannot be extended anymore, since the energy that derives from the activation of this position lacks.
