### _Problems connected to DNA replication_ 

There are some problems with the way the system develops, especially with the beginning of the DNA synthesis and its directionality. DNA polymerase can add new nucleotides to the chain but is not able to start it from scratch: indeed, it is only able to start adding nucleotides when it has a **primer,** and the strand has already been started. The solution is to use another enzyme, **RNA primase,** which will synthesize a short **oligo nucleotide** that is the beginning of the new strand; the primer is made of RNA, and RNA primase is able to synthesize a 1 O-nucleotide long RNA which will be used by DNA polymerase as a starting point to synthetize the new strand. 

**44** 

Whenever there is a replication origin, both strands are replicated in both directions, but DNA polymerase can synthetize only in a 5' to 3' fashion: when the helix is opened up, a bubble is created where the strands are separated, and the helix opens up as the two forks move in both directions. lt is possible to distinguish: 

- a **leading strand,** synthetized without any problems in direction 5' to 3 '. 

- a **lagging strand,** where the situation is more complicated: little fragments are synthetized as the fork moves in a backstitching technique that causes a delay in its synthesis. In the lagging strand, RNA primers must be synthetized for every piece, called **Okazaki fragment,** not linked to each other by phosphodiester bonds. When the replication terminates the lagging strand is characterized by many fragments not linked to each other. 

Once the synthesis has finished, primers must be removed, and Okazaki fragments must be linked: a nuclease specifically attacks and degrades the RNA primers, and a repair polymerase replaces the pieces where RNA was present using Okazaki fragments as primers. Then DNA **ligase** - thanks to the use of ATP - catalyzes the formati on of a phosphodiester bond between a 3' OH group and a 5' phosphate group at the ends oftwo adjacent DNA fragments 

**Nucleases** can hydrolyze phosphodiester bonds, are specific for DNA and RNA, andare divided into: 

- **Exonucleases,** which degrade the polynucleotide chain starting from its end, and therefore are able to destroy only linear DNA molecules and not circular ones. 

- **Endonucleases,** which are able to cut internal parts of the molecule. 

The participation of RNA primase is needed to synthetize a short RNA fragment responsible for the starting of the replicati on process, and in the end of the process, we have to get rid ofRNA primers. DNA polymerase is able to fill the gaps with DNA nucleotides, but a problem arises at the **end of chromosomes,** since it is not able to replace the very last one. At the end of the molecule, DNA is no able to replace the primers, so at the end of each round of replication the chromosome shortens, and this phenomenon is very known in biology and is associated to aging. Chromosomes shorten until they reach a certain length that is no more compatible with replication, so they enter a phase which will bring them to death. 

This is the **telomere** problem and was solved by three scientists who won the Nobel prize in 2009. The end of chromosomes does not bring genes but only tandem repeats of a hexamer (GGGTTA). Cells have mechanisms to allow telomers to be efficiently replicated, but is not activated in all types of cells, but only in cells with great replicative potential. There is an enzyme **(telomerase RNA)** able to replicate telomers: telomerase is a protein enzyme carrying an RNA strand which is used as a template to elongate the telomers. Telomerase elongates the leading strand using the RNA it carries as a template: the complex pairs with the existing end ofthe chromosome, which gets elongated. Then, DNA polymerase finished the duplication ofthe lagging strand and in the end the complete molecule and the end of the chromosome is duplicated. Ends of chromosomes tend to form a circular structure which make it possible to recognize the end from accidental breaks that could be generated in the chromosome itself. 

Telomerase is a **ribonucleoparticle,** so it is made by both a protein and an RNA part which acts as a template for the DNA molecule. What telomerase does is **reverse transcription** since it is producing 

45 

DNA from an RNA molecule, but it is usually inactive in somatic cells, which means that telomers shorten about 50-100 nucleotides per cycle and when they are too short to ensure replication, the celi exits the replication cycle and enters a senescent program which will end with its death. When telomers get short, DNA becomes unstable, and ifthere was the reactivation oftelomers, it would increase the risk for the celi to develop cancer. 

If there is a reactivation of telomerase, malignant **cancer cells** could develop: most cancer cells are characterized by the reactivation of telomerase activity, while germ-line cells are able to reactivate telomerase in arder to generate cells with intact chromosome ends. 

The **Hayflick limit,** or Hayflick phenomenon, is the number of times a normai somatic, differentiated human celi population will divide before celi division stops. 

46 

### **RNA transcription** 

Messenger RNA constitutes just a very small part of the RNA world: we can estimate that only 2-4% of RNA molecules are coding, while the majority of them (96-98%) are non-coding RNAs but are very important for structural and regulatory functions. tRNA, rRNA, small non-coding RNA, long non-coding RNA are some of non-coding RNAs. mRNAs represent just a small part of RNA molecules, and most RNA found in cells include ribosoma! RNA, which is a big molecule, and tRNA, much smaller and very frequent in cells. 

The **centrai dogma** of molecular biology describes the flow of genetic information and says that it flows from DNA to RNA to proteins: pieces of DNA are transcribed to RNA which is then translated into proteins. However, not all RNAs have a coding potential andare transformed into proteins; most ofthem have regulatory functions in the transcriptomic universe and in the gene expression, which is the level at which a specific gene is transcribed in an RNA molecule. 

### _Why is mRNA needed?_ 

- The **organization** of the cell and of the genetic material is quite clever: RNA molecules allow to store genetic material in a secure place (nucleus in eukaryotes ); the genetic information is qui te big, so it is handy to produce smaller molecules which can be later translated. 

- Thanks to RNA intermediates, DNA can be used a **template** to produce many mRNA molecules, therefore amplifying the synthetic output. 

### _Genes_ 

Different genes can be transcribed at different rates: not all of them are transcribed simultaneously and the rate at which we produce RNA and proteins varies depending on the gene considered. **Genes** are pieces ofDNA with variable length, from few hundreds to thousands to nucleotide and most of eukaryotic genome is actually represented by genes that are not active. There are about 20 thousand protein-coding genes: however, not all of them are transcribed at the same rate. **Housekeeping genes** produce proteins fundamental for cell structure and are highly transcribed to give many proteins. Other genes are more finely regulated, code for specific enzymes (involved for example in cell-to-cell communication), must be activated in specific conditions and only a few copies are needed. 

### _Prokaryotes vs eukaryotes_ 

In prokaryotes a single mRNA is produced in the cytoplasm, and it is translated to give different proteins: this structure is **poly-cistronic (polygenic** ), which means that there is a messenger RNA, and sequences that correspond to genes and will be translated into proteins. Eukaryotic RNA is **mono-cistronic,** meaning that there is one RNA for one gene, which will be then translated into a protein. 

In prokaryotes translation starts while mRNA is still being transcribed, while this cannot happen in eukaryotes since mRNA needs to be physically transported to the cytoplasm before translation can take place. In eukaryotes, mRNA undergoes **major modifications** before being transported to the cytoplasm and start translation; in prokaryotes, instead, mRNA undergoes little modifications. Moreover, in eukaryotes DNA is stored in the nucleus and both transcription and mRNA modification occur there; after that, mRNA is transported into the cytoplasm, where translation takes place. 

47 

In both cases there is still a **polymerization** reaction where ribonucleotides are link.ed together using a DNA strand as a template: RNA polymerase, a DNA template, four ribonucleotides triphosphate and transcription factors (accessory proteins that help transcription to occur) are needed. 

### _RNA transcription_ 

**Transcription** works in a similar way compared to DNA replication: the two strands of DNA can act as a template for the synthesis ofRNA and enzymes are involved. The most important is **RNA polymerase,** able to form an RNA molecule starting from a DNA template: it catalyzes phosphodiester bonds between nucleotides using a base-pairing mechanism. RNA polymerase can start without a primer but does not have a proof-reading activity, so it makes one error every 10 thousand nucleotides; when an RNA molecule is produced, it is very **labile,** lasts only few minutes, and is continuously replaced, so not much effort is required in its production. 

Many molecules of RNA polymerase can transcribe the same gene at the same time. At the same time transcription takes place, there is also the assembly of ribosoma! subunits, which are then loaded on rRNA molecules. 

RNA transcription takes place in three phases: 

- **Initiation:** RNA polymerase recognizes the unit of DNA to transcribe (gene) thanks to the interaction with a specific region **(promoter).** 

- **Elongation:** transcription starts, and by using DNA as a template, a new RNA molecule can be produced. 

- **Termination:** when the gene ends, the RNA polymerase stops transcription, and the RNA molecule is released. 

RNA polymerase can transcribe only from 5' to 3': RNA molecules are single-stranded, so when DNA is opened up only one of the two strands can be used as a template, depending on the directionality of the gene. The direction is understood because RNA polymerase recognizes a specific region on the DNA template, the **promoter,** whose orientation determines the directionality ofthe gene. 

### _Prokaryotic promoter_ 

A promoter is a region of DNA localized upstream of the gene, which is a region ofDNA encoding fora specific RNA molecule. The structure of promoters is different from prokaryotes and eukaryotes, but they are recognized either by RNA polymerase or transcription factors, so by other proteins involved in the replication process. 

In prokaryotes, the position indicated as + 1 is the starting site of transcription: upstream of it there are two regions localized in position at -10 and -35 nucleotides from the starting position, which are important recognition points for RNA polymerase. These two regions are characterized by **consensus sequences:** they are specific sequences found in majority of cases and which derive from comparison of more than 3 thousand genes. These sequences are very similar in different promoters, and by looking at the regions we can derive a consensus sequence, which is the most frequent combinati on of nucleotides localized in a specific genomic region. A consensus sequence tells the frequency of each nucleotide from 

48 

the -10 to the -35 position; however, as always happens in biologica! systems, a certain degree of flexibility is allowed, so we can find sequences with slight variations compared to the consensus ones. 

Apart from the -10 and -35 regions, in some promoters we can find another element, the **upstream/up element,** which provides an additional interaction site between RNA polymerase and DNA template. 

RNA polymerase is a qui te complicated enzyme composed of many different subunits ( _a,_ ~, _a ... )_ and different parts ofthe polymerase can recognize different elements: the e-terminal domain recognizes the upstream element, while the _a_ subunit recognizes the elements at -35 and at -10 with two specific subdomains. The interaction between RNA polymerase and these elements is fundamental for transcription to take piace. 

In prokaryotes RNA polymerase recognizes itself the promotor elements (with the e-terminal domain and the _a_ subunits) and when recognition happens there is the formation of the **close promotor complex.** At this point, the RNA polymerase (which has a helicase activity) starts to unwind the DNA around the initiation site (because it uses just one strand as a template) and there is the formation of the **open promotor complex.** When the DNA starts being unwound, transcription can begin; the _a_ subunit, important for the recognition of promotor regions, is release, and RNA polymerase starts working along the DNA molecule. 

### _Termination in prokaryotes_ 

After the initiation and elongation steps which give rise to the RNA molecule, transcription has to stop. In prokaryotes, transcription terminates in two different ways: 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
D A 5'<br>3'<br>5'<br>3'<br>RNA 5'  r onn,uion  ol >1.em k>op<br>Dl»ocl.1t1on OI RNA lf0HI<br>j  O A templ,1t<:-<br>I)  "5'  3'<br><!-- End of picture text -->

- **Rho independent mechanisms:** actively O A templ,1t<:transcribed genes are characterized by a termination signal, which is an inverted repeat of a Ge-rich sequence followed by A reduce. When the region is transcribed, it tends to form a **hair-pin** structure or **stem-loop** structure, which is characterized by a centrai regi on composed of Gand e that internet with I) "5' 3' each other and a loop at the top. This structure, formed by complementary base pairing, stimulates the dissociation of the transcription complex from DNA, thus inducing the terminati on of the process. 

- **Rho dependent mechanisms:** there is the participation of enzyme **RHO,** able (through a mechanism still not well characterized) to be loaded on top of the RNA molecule being actively transcribed. The RHO enzyme moves along the RNA molecule and reaches some RHO recognition elements: the interactions between the RHO molecules and the RHO recognition elements stimulate the stopping oftranscription and the release ofRNA from the DNA template. 

In prokaryotes the mechanism is quite simple and straightforward: everything is done by RNA polymerase and there is only one type of RNA polymerase that transcribes all genes. 

**49** 

### _Eukaryotic promoter_ 

In Eukaryotes the term "promoter" indicates **ali** the **sequences** important to control the transcription of a gene. Therefore, Eukaryotic promoters are extremely diverse and are difficult to characterize. 

In eukaryotes the process is more complicated: promoters in eukaryotic genes are very much diverse and can have sequences diff erent from each other, making it difficult for a researcher to identify and characterize the promoter. Promoter elements are not recognized by RNA polymerase itself, but by accessory proteins ( **transcription factors)** that recruit RNA polymerase to the promoter of a gene. Within eukaryotic genes we can define the **basai (core) promoter,** containing sequences fundamental for transcription to happen; however, transcription can be sped up by the presence of additional **upstream regulatory elements** ( cell-specific, tissue-specific, or present only in some genes ), recognized by a large variety of accessory proteins (when these regulatory elements are absent transcription is highly inefficient). 

In eukaryotes there are three types of RNA polymerase, each dedicated to specific types of genes: 

- **RNA polymerase** II transcribes all protein-coding genes and some small non-coding RNAs. 

- **RNA polymerase** I is dedicated to rRNA genes. There are four types of genes of rRNA, which are 5.8S, 18S, 28S (transcribed by RNA polymerase I) and 5S (transcribed by RNA polymerase III. 

- **RNA polymerase III** transcribes gene 5S, together with some snRNA genes and genes for other small sRNAs. 

There are four main elements within the promoter, known as **generai transcription factors** (always present): the **initiator point** (transcription start site), upstream of which the **TATA box** is (localized at 30 nucleotides upstream of the transcription start site), and the **TFIIB recognition element** (near the TATA box); sometimes there is a **downstream promoter element** (localized downstream of the transcription start site). 

N ot all elements are always present, and apart from these core promoter elements, there are usually other recognition sites for transcription factors specific for certain cells and tissues. These main elements are not recognized by RNA polymerase itself, but by transcription factors (accessory proteins) and the most important is the **TATA binding protein** ( or TBP) which binds the TATA box. Transcription factors IIB and IID recognize the other core promoter elements. 

There are other specific transcription factors found only in specific cell types and tissue: they stimulate transcription by binding to the promoter. Transcription factors can be divided into: 

- **Constitutive or generai transcription factors,** which act on all genes. 

- **Specific transcription factors,** which act on few genes in a very regulating way. 

The affinity of RNA polymerase for the promoter is very low, so it needs transcription factors to efficiently recognize the promoter and start transcription. The first generai transcription factor involved in the contact with the promoter is TFIID, composed of the TATA binding protein and of twelve associated factors (TATA binding protein associated factors ). This complex recognizes the TATA box when present. 

50 

These transcription factors generally belong to the class of DNA binding proteins, which have specific domains specialized in taking contact with the DNA molecule. The TATA binding protein is the subunit responsible for the recognition of the TATA box: the binding between DNA and the protein causes a specific **bending** of the DNA molecule, creating a platform for other transcription factors to land on and participate to the transcription process. 

TFIID takes the first contact with DNA, and the TATA binding protein recognizes the TATA box; once TFIID lands on the promoter, it starts to attract other transcription factors, such as TFIIB, TFIIE and TFIIH. In particular, TFIIH is very important because it has both helicase activity and it phosphorylates the e- terminal domain of RNA polymerase IL The helicase activity is vital to unwind the DNA molecule, and in this case RNA polymerase relies on this transcription factor to perform it. 

**Phosphorylation** of the tail is important to transcription because it causes a conformational change of the complex, the release of general transcription factors and the start of the elongation process of transcription. 

For RNA polymerase to be recruited to the promoter and start transcription, in eukaryotes transcription factors are needed: TFIID, TFIIB, TFIIF, TFIIH participate to the assembly and TFIIH is important for its helicase activity and for the phosphorylation of the e-terminal domains, an event fundamental for the transition from the initiation to the elongation phase. 

Once RNA polymerase is recruited to the starting site of transcription, it starts an abortive transcription in which it synthesizes short RNA transcripts without leaving the promoter. It's only after the phosphorylation of the e-terminal domain of the tail that the generai transcription factors can be released; RNA polymerase II can now leave the promoter (performing what is called **promoter clearance)** and start the elongation phase of transcription. 

RNA polymerase in eukaryotes is not very possessive, meaning that it needs the involvement of other protein factors to effectively bind to the template and carry out transcription. We described the structure of the **promoter,** a region localized upstream of a gene onto which the RNA polymerase binds, and the main elements situated within it. The promoter is an important site for transcription factors to bind, in order to promote the binding of RNA polymerase and the transcription process. We also described the elements ofthe **basai promoter,** which is immediately upstream ofthe gene region. 

Apart from these, we can have other elements, which are more distant, and which bind to transcription factors and participate in promoting the transcription process. It is easier to understand how the nearby regions influence the activity of RNA polymerase, while it is quite difficult to understand how regions far away from the gene can actually be involved in transcription. 

51 

### **How transcription factors promote transcription** 

RNA polymerase can internet with transcription factors that are nearby, therefore directly inducing the transcription process, or transcription factors can also bind in a place distant from the gene itself and make contact with the RNA polymerase through mediator proteins, which act as a **bridge** between RNA polymerase and the transcription factors. Moreover, we can have a direct contact between transcription factors and RNA polymerase, that is mediated by a process of **DNA bending.** 

There are different mechanisms through which the RNA polymerase's work can be induced, and most of the time, these processes are not mutually exclusive, but they are all present and participate in the determination of the transcription level of a gene. In the example here, we have transcription factors binding to the promoter region, that isjust upstream ofthe gene; other transcription factors internet with the so-called **enhancer** regions, that are far away from the promoter, and with the RNA polymerase itself, by inducing the bending of DNA. 

We could imagine that the transcription factory works in this way, where RNA polymerase is for sure the most important enzyme involved in the transcription process, but there are also other transcription factors **(generai transcription factors** are always present, while **specific transcription factors** are specific for a celi type or a tissue and are thus ~ 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
~<br>•=~;:,  ~<br>ractors (pink)<br>Mediato,<br>complexes<br>(purp1e)<br>easar  transcription<br>complex (blue)<br><!-- End of picture text -->

produced in a limited way). These •=~;:, factors can either directly recruit ractors (pink) RNA polymerase or internet with it Mediato, **complexes** by inducing the bending of the DNA (purp1e) itself, through **mediator proteins** easar (bridges that allow them to internet **complex (blue)** with the RNApolymerase). 

These enhancer regions are important because usually, when transcription factors bind to them, there is a higher amount of transcription of a gene: as we said last time, different genes can be transcribed at different rates, meaning that not all genes produce the same amount of RNA molecules. This depends on the need of the cell: for example, it needs different proteins in different amounts depending on the type of protein and the type of activity the protein is supposed to play, thus the number of RNA molecules produced will be different. A way to regulate the amount of RNA produced by the celi is to use **basai promoters** (so enhancer regions) to induce a **boost** in the production ofRNA molecules. 

### _Transcription activators_ 

Transcription factors are proteins which usually act as **activators,** inducing transcription and promoting the production of RNA molecules. Most of the time we have transcription activators, and these transcription factors are actual proteins, mainly composed of two domains: 

- the **DNA binding domain,** which allows transcription factors to physically land on the DNA molecule and to internet with the promoter. 

- the **activation domain,** which fixes contacts with the transcriptional machinery and increases the rate of transcription. 

52 

We have many different trnnscription activators encoded in our genome and this tells us the importance that gene regulation has for the cell activity: we have **more than 2500 gene transcriptional activators** that are encoded, and therefore this shows the effort the cell takes to regulate gene expression. 

### _DNA binding domain_ 

Activators are able to take contact with DNA through the **DNA binding domain:** what happens is that they usually recognize a specific sequence of DNA on which they have to land. This sort of reading activity derives from the fact that the DNA binding protein ( our trnnscription factor) is able to take contact with the bases by accessing the major groove of DNA. This contact genernlly consists in **Hydrogen bonds** established between protein residues and the bases that compose DNA. 

Trnnscription regulators - through DNA binding domain - can access the DNA sequence by using specific conformations of the protein itself. Examples of the structures these proteins can acquire to access the DNA molecule include: 1<sup>11</sup> •&"" 

1 z,nc<sup>11</sup> •&"" 

- **Zinc finger conformation,** with loop regions (~-sheets and a- helices ). This particular conformation contains repeats of **cysteine** and **histidine** able to bind zinc ions and fold into structures that resemble the fingers of a hand and can "grab" the DNA molecule. 

- **Helix-turn-helix,** where the residues that make contact with DNA are mainly contained in one helix, whereas the others stabilize the internction. 

**(8) t-tehx~turn-hehx** 

### _Activators can recruit chromatin-modifying complexes_ 

We saw the function of DNA binding domain, where the protein can physically internet with the DNA sequence. What about the activation domains, which induce a boost in the transcription process? Genernlly, they are not so well charncterized and can either internet with other genernl transcription factors, or with **mediator proteins,** or they can induce a **modification of the chromatin structure** therefore opening up the double helix to allow RNA polymerase to have a better access to a specific regi on of the genome. 

The binding of the trnnscription regulator could recruit other modifying enzymes such as **chromatin remodeling complexes,** that can remodel the structure of chromatin, opening it up by physically moving the position of nucleosomes, and therefore allowing DNA to be accessed by RNA polymernse itself. 

### _Transcription repressors_ 

Finally, there are also some **repressors,** which are proteins that bind to the DNA and instead of inducing and boosting trnnscription, they induce **inhibition.** These repressors can either: 

- **interfere** with the binding of other trnnscription factors: they bind to the DNA molecule, therefore avoiding the binding of the activators in the same spot, so they could have only one domain (the DNA binding domain) that physically occludes and avoids the binding of the activators. 

- have both a DNA binding domain and an inhibition domain which can inhibit transcription by protein-protein internctions. 

53 

### _Eukaryotes vs prokaryotes_ 

To conclude, transcription in prokaryotes and eukaryotes is similar in the way is carried out but usually in eukaryotes transcripts are much longer and the process is much more rapid. Apart from RNA polymerase, eukaryotes have several factors which participate to the transcription process. Some generai transcription factors take care ofthe start of transcription, and during elongation (when the new chain is produced), elongation factors take the RNA polymerase along the DNA template and allow transcription. The most striking diff erence between prokaryotes and eukaryotes is that in **eukaryotes** there is a **massive modification of mRNA,** which needs to be change and modified to be translation-ready. 

### _Eukaryotic elongation factors_ 

**Elongation factors** are needed in eukaryotes to assist RNA polymerase during the polymerization process (the synthesis of the new RNA chain). These factors are needed to **suppress the pausing** ofRNA polymerase: the DNA double helix is opened up while RNA polymerase proceeds, but sometimes messes may form, and as a consequence RNA polymerase pauses. If it pauses for too long, there is a higher chance that it will detach from its template and transcription will finish. Thus, elongation factors have the function to help prevent this pausing and to ensure that transcription goes on. 

Elongation factors are also responsible for **modifying chromatin** in order to assist elongation and to allow the regions of DNA to be fully accessible to RNA polymerase. 

### _Termination in eukaryotes_ 

RNA polymerase III and RNA polymerase I terminate transcription by recognizing specific sequences, and they require additional auxiliary factors. RNA polymerase II termination does not occur at a conserved site or at a constant distance from the 3 '-end of mature RNAs. 

The mechanism of **termination** in eukaryotes is much more complex than that in prokaryotes and is coupled with the processing of mRNA. 

54 

## **RNA processing and gene expression** 

We can now talk about the destiny of mRNA; in particular, we will describe how mRNA is processed and we will talk about three modifications: 

- **polyadenylation.** 

- **capping.** 

- **splicing.** 

We will see not only how they are carried out, but why they are carried out. 

All these modifications induce a very **profound change** in the structure of mRNA, and they occur in eukaryotes, whereas prokaryotic mRNAs are pretty much ready for translation as soon as they detach from the transcription complex. 

Modifications occur everywhere in the molecule, both at 5' end, at the 3' end, and in the internal part of the molecule. **Capping** consists in the addition of the so-called cap - modified nucleotides - to one end of the molecule. **Tailing** is the addition of a **poly-A tail** to the other end (the 3' end). Tue **splicing process** is the removal of specific sequences (called **introns)** from mRNA molecules. 

The enzymes responsible for all these modifications ride on the phosphorylated tail of RNA polymerase, and this means that **mRNA modifications occur co-transcriptionally:** as soon as the molecule emerges from the transcription complex, it is modified ( capped, spliced and polyadenylated). There are capping factors, splicing factors, and polyadenylating factors which are physically transported by the tail ofRNA polymerase. 

Let's see in more detail each ofthese modifications, starting from the capping at 5' side ofthe molecule. 

### _Capping_ 

Capping consists in the **attachment of a modified Guanine nucleotide,** that is connected to the last nucleotide of mRNA by a 5'-5' bridge. This means that the 

5' position of the last nucleotide of mRNA is connected to the modified nucleotide through a bridge formed by three phosphates connected to the 5' position in both nucleotides that are involved. 

Capping is involved in protein **synthesis initiation** because it is identified and is bound by factors involved in the translation process. Both the capping and the 3' poly-A tail have this function: **recognition** that this is a mRNA and that it must be translated. 

Capping also has a **protective function:** especially in cytoplasm there are many **RNAases,** which are enzymes involved in the degradation of mRNA molecules. We want mRNA to be not so stable in our cells because gene expression must be **adaptable:** depending on the condition of the cell, on extemal environmental stimuli, the cell needs to be able to change its metabolic activity. Changing the metabolic activity also involves changing gene expression and proteins that are produced. Consequently, mRNAs 

55 

have a **limited half-life** in cells, and they will be degraded shortly after being synthetized (sometimes in a few minutes ); this dynamic activity of the celi allows it to be responsive to changes. 

F or example, when a grow signal comes to the cell, the cell has to start to produce proteins that are involved in cell division. This signal lasts for a limited amount of time, so after it comes there is the activation of a cascade of events, and some of these events lead to the activation of the transcription of genes that produce proteins involved in celi division. However, those proteins should not be produced forever from that moment, but only for a certain peri od of time, and the way to regulate this is to degrade mRNAs as soon as they are not needed anymore. 

A **balance** must be found between the degradati on of mRNAs and the possibility to transiate them in the right amount of proteins. A way to do that is to produce mRNAs having enzymes which will degrade them, but also trying to protect these molecules by putting a capping and a poly-A tail to them, so that degradation will take longer (but it will still happen). 

What is added during capping is a Guanine nucleotide, added by an enzyme called **guanylyl transf erase.** Then, the nucleotide is further modified by the addition of a methyl group, obtaining a **7-methylguanosine.** 

Capping is the most straightforward modification: the modified nucleotide protects mRNA, is important for its exportation from the nucleus to the cytoplasm, and will be recognized by the ribosoma! translational machinery, thus indicating that the molecule has to be translated. 

### _Polyadenylation_ 

Let's analyze what happens at the other end of the molecule (3' end): there is the **addition of a poli-A tail,** but before talking about this let's take a step backward and let's go back to the concept of terminati on of transcription in eukaryotes. 

**Termination** in eukaryotes is a more complicated pathway than what happens in prokaryotes because we have a completely different mechanism, where termination is actually strictly associated with polyadenylation. For the transcription to end, we must have polyadenylation to be carried out. Indeed, RNA polymerase always transcribes a fragment that is longer compared to the mature mRNA, and the last portion of mRNA that is produced by RNA polymerase is usually eliminated, cut, and degraded, whereas a poli-A tail will be attached to the mRNA molecule. 

The message that is important for translation and for protein production will be retained in the mRNA molecule. We are just cutting out a part that is non-coding and we are adding a **tail** that is composed of just **A nucleotides.** Polyadenylation is important for **translation,** because the poli-A tail will be recognized by the translational machinery. Moreover, it is connected to the **stability** of mRNA; indeed, it protects the message contained in mRNA from the damaging activity ofRNAases, which are enzymes (nucleases) that degrade the RNA molecule in the cytoplasm. 

To sum up, transcription produces a mRNA longer than the actual one, and for transcription to terminate and for polyadenylation to occur, we need to physically cut the mRNA and add a tail. 

How can this process be carried out and how can the celi know which the right point is to cut mRNA without inducing any damage or loss of the message? 

56 

The answer, as usually happens when talking about nucleic acids, is contained in the sequence that we are analyzing. There are sequences that are **specific signals** far polyadenylation and that are recognized by specific factors. In particular, there is what is called the **poli-A signal,** where there are A and U, and which can be found along the mRNA molecule. After the poli-A signal, there is another important element, more or less 1 O to 30 nucleotides downstream, which is the **point of cleavage** where CA can be found. After this, there is another downstream element enriched in U or GU nucleotides. These sequences are important, but also the distance between each other is vital, because factors have to be able to interact with each other so that polyadenylation can be carried out. Therefore, there are specific factors that recognize the poli-A signal, the downstream element, and the poli-A site. 

### _Consensus nucleotide sequences directing polyadenylation_ 

As happens most of the time and as was previously discussed concerning the promoter region, these sequences are consensus sequences: they are the sequences that can be generally found in these specific spots, even though a certain degree of flexibility is allowed (we can have slight variations). There are two main protein factors that are involved, called **CPSF** (Cleavage and Polyadenylation Specificity Factor) and **CstF** ( cleavage stimulation factor F). They ride along the tail of RNA polymerase as the capping factor does. When the polyadenylation signal is recognized by CPSF, the polyadenylation process starts to happen: there is the recognition of the site, the physical cut of the mRNA molecule ( **cleavage)** by CstF, and at this point there is the recruitment of a very special RNA polymerase, called **poly A polymerase (PAP).** This polymerase is special because it is able to synthesize a tail of nucleotides and attach it to mRNA; however, it is able to include in the tail only A nucleotides, forming a poli-A tail. The poli-A polymerase **does not need a template,** and this is another important characteristic: this means that the poli-A tail is not physically encoded in our DNA molecule, but it is something that is added after the RNA synthesis. 

RNA polymerase is synthetizing a certain mRNA and at a certain point the factors that ride on the tail recognize the poli-A sequence and mediate the cleavage ofmRNA, thus releasing from mRNA the poliA polymerase, that is at the same time continuously synthesizing RNA molecules. 

At this point, poli-A polymerase synthetizes a tail of only **eight nucleotides** and poli-A binding proteins bind to the tail, stabilizing it and stretching it out. 

### _RNA processing_ 

mRNA has therefore been polyadenylated and is good to go concerning this modification, but what happens to the rest of the molecule? RNA polymerase continues to synthesize the RNA molecules, but at a certain point it **releases its grip** on the template and transcription terminates. It is not really clear what the trigger of the release of the grip is, but the idea is that factors involved in RNA modification are transferred to the template ( cap factors, splice factors and poli-A factors are released from the tail because they start to work on the mRNA molecule ). This modification of the interaction between the proteins causes a **conformational change** in RNA polymerase, which loses its ability to grip effectively to the DNA template, and therefore at a certain point, after polyadenylation takes place, it will release the template and the RNA molecule that has been synthetized after the cutting. This piece of RNA will be directly degraded in the nucleus. 

57 

These modifications are important for the **stability** of mRNA and for the translation process, because they are features that are recognized by translation factors (translation machinery). They help identify the molecule as a mRNA molecule. 

### _Splicing_ 

Now let's goto the last modification on the mRNA, but first we have to introduce one more concept about genes, and in particular about eukaryotic genes. 

Usually in bacteria, mRNAs are characterized by uninterrupted stretches of DNA sequences which are directly transcribed and translated without further processing. However, eukaryotic genes are different, and we can distinguish: 

- **exons,** which contain coding sequences important for the translation into protein. 

- **introns,** which are long intervening noncoding sequences and interrupt exons. They do not contain any information useful for protein synthesis. 

So, ifwe imagine processing messenger RNA into a form that is ready for translation, we should expect to carry out some kind of process that enables us to eliminate introns and stitch together exons. 

Introns are removed through **splicing:** when comparing exons and introns, exons are quite short (few hundred nucleotides long) while introns can be very long (in the order of kilo bases) reaching even 10 thousand nucleotides. Depending on the gene and organism the distribution of lengths can also be different. 

Actually, there are some exons which contain sequences that are not translated but that are present in mature mRN A and have a **regulatory** function. These regions are localized at the 5' and at the 3' ends of the molecules and are called **UTRs** or untranslated regions. When we get rid of introns and end up only with exons, exon 1 won't be coding starting from the first nucleotide, but there will be a region at the beginning of the nucleotide called **5' UTR;** the same is true for the end of the nucleotide, where the last exon or part of it is not coding **(3' UTR).** These regions regulate the stability of mRNA, its half-life and can interact with other RNA species (microRNAs) which can regulate the stability of mRNA itself. 

Splicing was discovered because scientists realized that mRNA was somehow **shorter** compared to the genomi e versi on of the gene: by taking the mRNA of a protein of interest and hybridizing it with a DNA template, we observe that only part of the DNA is able to hybridize with mRNA. DNA will form **unpaired** S' loops because there will be regions unable to pair with mRNA, and they , ~·· correspond to introns (parts removed by the splicing process ). This Two transesterification observation was first done on adenoviruses and soon it was reactions demonstrated that it was a **widespread mechanism** for eukaryotic genes. Moreover, introns consti tute more than 90% of the length of the S' 3' average human gene, so they must have an important function.

The splicing mechanism relies on specific factors able to recognize specific sequences; by analyzing the structure of RNA, during splicing there is for example the union of exon 1 and exon 2 and the removal of 

58 

the intron in between. We can define specific regions important far the recognition of exon-intron boundaries that are localized at the beginning **(5' splice site or donor splice site)** and at the end **(3' splice site or acceptor splice site)** of the intron. A regi on near the end of the intron itself **(branch site)** carries other important nucleotides. 

During the splicing reaction, there is the formation of a structure called **lariat,** where the branch site is the physical place along the intron where there is the formati on of an unusual band between the 5' end of the intron and the 2' hydroxyl group of the A nucleotide at the branch site. Instead of the donar and acceptor sites linked together after getting rid of the intron, the donar site is linked to the branch site, forming a branched structure. The final aim of the splicing process is to get rid ofthe intron and to stitch together the two exons. Thus, mRNA is 5' lltJnch po,nt 3' cleaved at the donar splice site, which 5<sup>. e:='=°'='</sup> '~n:z::==============~s::=====MJ·==L22 **=** i 3. °'=' attaches to the branch site, forming an unusual 5'-2' donar. Finally, there is a third step, where also the 3' part is cut and there is the release of the lariat (the intron is not linear anymore but has a very peculiar structure ). The **intronic lariat** is then directly degraded into the nucleus. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
5'  lltJnch po,nt  3'<br>5 . e:='=°'='  '~n:z::==============~s::=====MJ·==L22 = i 3. °'='<br><!-- End of picture text -->

Considering sequences, the situation is quite flexible: there are consensus sequences, so we can tel1 what the most present ones are. **R** means that there is an equal probability to find either G or A; **Y** means that there is an equal probability to have find C or U; **N** means that any nucleotide can be present. The first nucleotide of the intron is always G, and the second nucleotide is always U, but in any case, a certain degree of freedom is allowed. 

### _The spliceosome_ 

The molecular machinery in charge of the splicing process is the **spliceosome:** it is a very complicated complex, composed of both proteins and RNAs; there are about 150 proteins and five small nuclear RNAs (snRNAs). There are five main complexes (Ul, U2, U4, U5, U6), collectively called **snRNP** (small nuclear ribonucleoproteins). 

The splicing process is carried out largely by RNA molecules instead of proteins, which mostly stabilize the structure. RNAs have a catalytic role, recognize intron-exon boundaries and participate in the chemical of the splicing. The spliceosome is a very dynamic component and is assembled in a dynamic way: there are parts of the complex that continuously enter and exit the system. 

Small nuclear RNAs are able to recognize splicing sequences by direct base pairing and **Ul** is able to recognize the **5' splice site** of mRNA through base paring. **U2** recognizes the **branch point** (still by direct base pairing) and at this point there is the introduction of a huge **complex** consisting **of U4/U6 and U5,** 

59 

that binds to 5' splice site. All snRNA molecules, together with proteins, carry out the chemical part of the process by physically cutting the 5' site, forming an unusual bond with the branched site and finally removing the intron by cutting also the 3' site. In the end there is the removal of the intron and the formation of **mature mRNA.** 

### _Alternative splicing_ 

lntrons represent a clever system to produce **diff erent versions** of the same proteins. Apart from the splicing mechanism in which we get rid of introns and stitch exons all together, another process exists: it is the **alternative splicing,** which allows to stitch together exons in a **different order.** 

This means that we can have the same gene localized on the DNA, which is transcribed to produce a primary RNA transcript (the first version of mRNA) with both exons and introns. At this point splicing occurs: with "traditional" splicing, we get rid of introns and stitch exons all together, but thanks to alternative splicing, we can **shuffle** the **exon cassette** (we can imagine exons as separated functional units, which are then shuflled). Starting from the same gene and the same primary transcript we can get another version of the mRNA, which- when translated- will give rise to a different version of the same protein. It is quite convenient for the cell to produce different versions ofthe same protein: let's say that the first mRNA produces a receptor localized on the celi membrane, and we are interested in producing the same receptor, but intracellularly. Thus, we can produce the same protein, lacking the domain inserted in the cell membrane so that the receptor will function in a diff erent way. 

Alternative splicing can be seen as a way to **regolate gene expression,** since exon combinations are not always translated into functional proteins, and it is also a way to change the balance between mRNA and protein production. Imagine you want to produce a lower amount of a receptor: you have to change the splicing of the transcript to produce another transcript that is not able to be translated into a functional protein, so that the receptor is produced in a smaller amount by simply changing the splicing of the transcript. This will cause the receptor to be produced in a lower amount by simply changing the splicing of the transcript. This is a very clever way that the cell has to regulate protein forms and gene expression. Moreover, alternative splicing allows the celi to produce many **diverse proteins** starting from the same gene, thus extending the **diversity of the proteome** without extending the diversity of the genome, which is a very powerful process. 

### _Backsplicing_ 

In another mechanism, called **backsplicing,** there is a circular transcript: a strange order of exons is obtained, because when performing it, a closed loop is formed (some exons follow another molecular mechanism). 

### _Export from the nucleus_ 

Now mRNA is exportation-ready and can be physically moved from the nucleus to the cytoplasm. The transportation is necessary because **translation** takes piace in the **cytoplasm** or in the outer part of the rough endoplasmic reticulum. However, only those RNAs carrying specific signatures can be transported out of the nucleus, and these are represented by the presence of **several proteins** along the mRNA: cap binding proteins bind to the cap, poli-A binding proteins bind to the poli-A tail, and exon junction 

60 

complexes are deposited on exon junctions during splicing. At this point mRNA is transported from the nucleus to the cytoplasm and translation can take place. 

mRNA are quite **labile** molecules, having a reduced half-life. In particular, in bacteria they have a lifetime of about three minutes so the process must be carried out very rapidly. In eukaryotic cells we can have longer half-lives, but in any case, mRNAs are **degraded** in the end. 

61 

### **Translation** 

Transcription from DNA molecules to RNA ones is kind of an intuitive process because it goes from a 4-letter alphabet to an almost identica! 4-letter alphabet. Instead, in translation, the RNA code is translated into proteins, so from a 4-letter alphabet it goes to a **20-letter alphabet,** which is different. The rules goveming this transition are collectively called **"genetic code".** If each nucleotide encoded fora single aminoacid, there would be only four possible combinations, which would not be enough; the same problem would arise if two nucleotides coded for one aminoacid, since there would be sixteen possible combinations. Instead, ifa triple of nucleotides codes for one aminoacid, there are enough combinations **(64)** to cover all the twenty aminoacids. 

### _Historical background_ 

Experiments were carried out by **Crick** and **Brenner** in 1961 to study the genetic code: they introduced mutations in T4 phages modifying their virai potential and infected bacteria with them. The mutation caused the phage to form abnormally large plaques in infected bacteria, which could be clearly distinguished from those formed by wild-type phage. The scientists were able to insert or delete a base pair in the interest sequence: owing to the addition or deletion of nucleotides, the gene becomes nonfunctional, except for the addition or deletion of three base pairs, which instead did not affect the functionality ofthe gene. This proved that a **codon** is a sequence ofthree nucleotides coding fora specific amino acid. 

Still in 1961, **Nirenberg** and **Matthaei** performed **_in-vitro_ translation** and were able to decode the first word of the genetic code. They used high speed centrifugation on bacterial cells, so that the heaviest molecules would sediment in the pellet, while the lightest ones, soluble in the cytoplasm, would float. The scientist already knew that all components necessary for protein synthesis were present in the cytoplasm, and they synthetized an RNA composed solely of Uracil, using enzymes able to randomly join nucleotides together. Then, they set up twenty reaction tubes, each containing a different amino acid, and by adding a single type of radioactively labeled amino acid at a time to the mix, they found out that poly U directed the synthesis of a polypeptide containing only **phenylalanine.** In this way, Nirenberg and Matthaei were able to decipher the first word of the genetic code. 

Subsequently, they were able to crack the entire code, and this was made possible by an additional piece of discovery: RNA fragments that were only three nucleotides in length (size of a single **codon)** could bind to a ribosome and attract the appropriate amino-acid-containing tRNA molecule to the proteinmaking machinery. Once produced all 64 possible codons, researchers were able to work out the complete genetic code within one year. 

### _Characteristics of the genetic code_ 

The genetic code: 

- is **redundant,** meaning that more codons give rise to the same amino acids. 

- Is **non-overlapping,** meaning that two consequent codons need to be written separately, without overlapping between the two triplets. 

- has **no punctuation,** so there are no signs between the end of a codon and the beginning of the following. 

62 

- is **universal,** so it is written in the same way every time. 

- **non-ambiguous,** meaning that one codon gives rise to a umque aminoacid. The starting aminoacid **(methionine)** is coded solely by the codon **AUG.** 

Code redundancy minimizes the impact of mutations on a protein level: many times, a DNA mutation can lead to the production of the same protein as it would if the DNA was correct. Instead, if there were only 20 triplets codifying for the 20 amino acids and the remaining 44 codons were non-coding, this would result in either a **missense** for each mutation - meaning that there would be an amino acid different from the expected one - or translation would stop if the mutation led to the formation of a **stop codon** (UAA, UAG or UGA). AUG deriving from a mutation is called **"alternative start site".**

AGA<br>AGG<br>GCA  CGA  GGA<br>mRNA  GCG  CGG  GGG  AUA<br>Codons  GCU  CGU  GAU  AAU  UGU  GAA  CAA  GGU  CAU  AUU<br>GCC  CGC  GAC  AAC  UGC  GAG  CAG  GGC  CAC  AUC<br>Amino acids<br><!-- End of picture text -->



<!-- Start of picture text -->
CCA  ACA  GUA<br>AAA  ..  uuu  CCG  ccu  ACG ACU  UAU  GUG GUU  UAG UAA<br>AAG  AUG  uuc  ccc  ACC  UGG  UAC  GUC  UGA<br><!-- End of picture text -->

The degeneration of the genetic code is not random: amino acids which have similarities in terms of structure and function are encoded by similar codons, so that **point mutations** (mutations of a single nucleotide) would not impact the protein too much. 

Sometimes, the third position of a codon is tolerant for mutations: it is glycine case, for example, since it is encoded by GGA, GGG, GGU, GGC. We can see that the amino acid that is produced will always be glycine, independently of the third nucleotide, assuming that the first two nucleotides are both Guanine. 

**Transitions** are the most common types of mutations in DNA and involve the switch from Adenine to Guanine and from Cytosine to Thymine and vice versa in the third position; amino acids corresponding to only two codons are resistant to transitions in the third position. 

### _tRNAs_ 

tRNAs are **adaptors** that allow translation of mRNA to polypeptide chain. Indeed, codons do not directly recognize the amino acids for which they are coding, but they need adaptors that recognize them and bind the amino acids on the other end. These adaptors are tRNAs: they are single stranded molecules which fold as a **cloverleaf** and present an **anticodon** loop to which they bind the mRNA that is being translated. Instead, at the 3' end they bear an amino acid corresponding to the codon complementary to the sequence on the anticodon. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
attached amino<br>acid (Phe)<br>icodon  I<br>p<br><!-- End of picture text -->

63 

Mature cytosolic t-RNAs average 75-80 nucleotides in length andare produced from large precursors (pre-tRNAs) synthetized by RNA polymerase III in the cytoplasm. Cleavage and base modification occur during processing of all pre-tRNAs: 5' sequence of variable length is removed by a ribonuclease and about 10% of the bases are modified during the process. Uracil residues at the 3' end are replaced by a **CCA** sequence in all tRNAs. Some pre-tRNAs contain one or more introns that are spliced out during processmg. 

### _Wobbles_ 

Different codons can codify for the same amino acid, so there can be either more than one tRNA for many of the amino acids or some tRNA molecules can base-pair with more than one codon. 

Some tRNAs require a perfect base-pairing only in the first two positions, owing to genetic code redundancy and because the third position **(wobble)** can tolerate some mismatch at the third position. Wobble base-pairing allows the translation of the 61 codons (we rule out the three stop codons) into the 20 amino acids with as few as 31 kinds oftRNA; without wobbles, each ofthe 61 codons would need a specific tRNA, but thanks to the tolerance of the third position, the number of tRNA that is needed is reduced to 31. Humans have nearly 500 tRNA genes, but only have 48 different anticodons. 

Moreover, this wobble base-pairing explains why so many of the alternative codons for an amino acid **differ only in their third nucleotide.** 

### _Aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases_ 

At the 3' end - where the amino acid is attached - there is always a CCA sequence, so why the amino acids that tRNA bring are different from each other? The answer is that the anticodon must be in some way reflected in the amino acid that tRNA carries and to do so, an adaptor is needed: **aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases** are enzymes which covalently couple an amino acid to its appropriate set of tRNA molecules. Since each enzyme binds a specific amino

How is an amino acid loaded onto the tRNA? Within aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, the amino acid is adenylated, so the carboxyl group is linked directly to 

an AMP moiety thanks to energy coming from ATP. The carboxyl end of the amino acid thus forms an ester bond to ribose, and since the hydrolysis of this ester bond is **lncorrect** associated with a large favorable change in free energy, an amino acid held **aminoacid ----willbe** in this way is said to be **activated. removed**

Some aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, like DNA polymerase, have a **proofreading activity.** Some amino acids are actually quite similar to each other, and this could lead to mistakes: ifthe incorrect amino acid is bound to the 3' end of tRNA, **steric hindrance** would push the amino acid to an editing side where the wrong amino acid would be removed. RNA synthetases remove their own coupling errors through hydrolytic editing of incorrectly attached amino acids. 

64 

### _Ribosomes and snoRNAs_ 

Ribosomes are composed of a **large** and a **small subunit,** both made of **rRNA** and **proteins,** and they slightly differ from eukaryotic (80S) and prokaryotic cells (70S). Like tRNA and mRNA, rRNAs are transcribed as a precursor (pre-rRNA), and many times pre-rRNAs contain different final rRNAs separated by **spacer regions,** which then get degraded. 

Other modifications occurring in rRNA include the addition of **methyl groups** to the 2 '-OH group on nucleotide sugars and the **conversion of uridine to pseudouridine.** These chemical

modifications involve snoRNAs (small nucleolar RNAs), which are present only in eukaryotes (located in the nucleolus) andare mostly encoded in the introns of other genes. snoRNAs bind to specific regions of pre-rRNAs, are necessary to introduce chemical modifications on specific residues of rRNAs, participate in methylation and pseudouridylation of rRNAs. More than 200 snoRNAs have been identified. 

### _Protein synthesis_ 

Translation takes place on **ribosomes** free in the cytoplasm or associated to the endoplasmic reticulum's outer membrane. The carrier of information in translation is mRNA; the machinery consists of ribosomes and of initiation, elongation and termination factors; reagents include ATP, tRNA, amino acids, aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, aminoacyl-tRNA and GTP. 

The translation process involves three phases: 

- **Initiation,** when ribosomes bind to the mRNA at start codon. 

- **Elongation,** when actual translation takes place, and the polypeptide elongates by successively addition of amino acids. 

- **Termination,** when a stop codon is reached, the polypeptide is released, and the ribosome dissociates. 

### _Initiation: prokaryotes vs eukaryotes_ 

During the initiation phase, the ribosoma! subunits must recognize the mRNA and the correct **reading frame** (where the codon starts) must be chosen, which corresponds to the firstAUG triple, so everything before it is a 5' UTR. 

## Prokaryotes 

Prokaryotes present the so-called **Shine-Delgarno sequence,** which is slightly upstream of the start codon (so it is situated in the 5' UTR) ,is rich in purines ( often AGGAGG), recognizes a pyrimidine-rich sequence in the 16S rRNA, and favors the correct positioning of the mRNA to identify the start codon (AUG), thus the correct reading frame. Not every AUG possesses a Shine-Delgarno sequence, since a Shine-Delgarno sequence signals the fact that the AUG is the start codon and not the fact that it codifies fora methionine. 

65 

In prokaryotes, initiation factors first bind to the small ribosoma! unit; then, mRNA and the initiator tRNA join the complex. A 50S subunit binds to the complex, triggering the hydrolysis of bound GTP, followed by the release of initiation factors. Initiation factors can also be recruited in the middle of mRNA ifa Shine-Delgamo sequence is present. 

### Eukaryotes 

In eukaryotes, instead there is no Shine-Delgamo sequence, but there are other sequences that affect the efficiency of initiation, since sometimes the first AUG in the mRNA is skipped and translation initiates at a downstream AUG. 

A **preinitiation complex** is formed by association of the 40S subunit, the initiator tRNA, and initiation factors. Then, mRNA is recognized and brought to the ribosome by other initiation factors, which recognize both the 5' and 3' ends ofmRNAs. The 40S ribosoma! subunit, in association with the bound methionyl tRNA and initiation factors, then scans the mRNA to identify the AUG initiation codon. 

At this time, we only have the small subunit scanning mRNA and when AUG is found, GTP is hydrolyzed, and initiation factors are released. Then, the large 60S ribosoma! subunit binds to the 40S subunit to form the 80S initiation complex of eukaryotic cells, and elongation can start. 

To sum up, we can say that in prokaryotes the initiation complex is directly formed at the start codon, while in eukaryotes at least 12 initiation factors are required, and the initiation complex is formed at the 5' end of the mRNA. Afterwards, it moves along the mRNA in the **5' to 3' direction** until it finds the first AUGcodon. 

## _Elongation_ 

The elongation phase is similar in prokaryotes and eukaryotes and requires **elongation factors.** There is the formation of a peptide bond that links the carboxyl group at the end of a growing polypeptide chain growing polypeptide chain to the free amino group of an incoming aminoacid. A STEP 1 / newly bound 2 charged protein is thus synthetized **from its N-terminal end to its C-terminal end.** H,N 1 - 3 **4** !RNA 



Ribosomes present three sites, important for the elongation phase of translation. When a new tRNA arrives, it goes to the **A site** of the ribosome, which is the place where a charged tRNA is recruited. 



The carboxyl end ofthe polypeptide chain is uncoupled from the tRNA at the **P site** and j oined by a peptide bond to the free amino group of the amino acid linked to the tRNA at the A site. The polypeptide chain is 

held at the P site, and the tRNA sitting there gets attached to the growing polypeptide. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
mP 3<br><!-- End of picture text -->



A shift of the large subunit relative to the small subunit moves the two bound tRNAs into the **E** and P **sites** of the large subunit, and the uncharged tRNA finds itself in the E site, while the tRNA that has the whole chain attached is at the P site. 



66 

The small subunit moves exactly three nucleotides along the mRNA molecule, bringing it back to its originai position relative to the large subunit. This movement ejects the spent tRNA and resets the ribosome with an empty A site. 

The mRNA is translated in the **5' to 3' direction,** and the N-terminal end of a protein is made first. 

### _Ribozyme_ 

Ribosoma! proteins seem to have a **structural** and **folding role,** while the enzymatic activity seems to be held by rRNA itself and that is why the structure is called **ribozyme.** The catalytic site in this rRNA (which is a **peptidyl transferase)** is similar in many respects to that found in some protein enzymes: it is a highly structured pocket that precisely orients the two reactants, which are the elongating polypeptide and the charged tRNA. 

### _Termination_

After the adding of several amino acids, if the codon in the mRNA hosted in the A site is a **stop codon,** which has no tRNA recognizing it, termination occurs. A **release factor** is recruited, which binds the stop codon and allows the catalytic site to add a water molecule instead of a new nucleotide (the ester bond gets hydrolyzed). The peptide is released with a free C terminus, and the ribosome also releases the mRNA and dissociates into its two separate subunits. 

### _Polyribosomes_ 

Many times, multiple ribosomes bind to each mRNA that is being translated, therefore mRNAmolecules being translated are usually found in the form of **polyribosomes,** also known as **polysomes,** which operate in both bacteria and eukaryotes. 

Ribosomes will typically attach to the free end of a bacterial mRNA molecule and start translating it even before the transcription of that RNA is complete; this is not possible in eukaryotic cells, since transcription occurs in the nucleus, while translation takes place in the cytoplasm. 

Diff erences between eukaryotic and **_I_ a.** prokaryotic cells have been exploited for the production of **antibiotics,** which inhibit RNA and protein synthesis in bacteria but not in eukaryotes.

### **The cell cycle** 

The cell cycle is a complex assemble of processes that allows to grow and **duplicate DNA, segregate DNA** and **cytosolic materiai,** and **partition** it into daughter cells. It is a process that is quite variable depending on the cell that is considered, and indeed it ranges from minutes to hours or days. 

### _Celi cycle_ 

The cell cycle is divided into **four phases,** with the actual cell division occurring in the M phase. The two main processes occurring are duplication of the genetic material and divisi on ofthe nucleus and of the cytoplasm **(cytokinesis).** The DNA is replicated at the S phase, while **mitosis** (nuclear division) and cytokinesis (cytoplasmic division) occur in the M phase. The two phases are separated by the **Gl** and the **G2 phases** (gap phases ), in which it seems nothing is happening, but actually cells are monitoring the cycle and deciding whether to proceed or not. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
,----__  M  PHASE  Only 1 hour!<br>: mitosis<br>, (nuclear<br>: division)<br>(DNA replication)<br><!-- End of picture text -->

There is an additional phase, known as **GO phase,** during which the cell cycle arrests, and cells stop dividing: this occurs in **nerve cells,** for example, which cease cell division altogether, do not go through it anymore, and enter a terminal GO phase. 

Other cells, instead, divide only occasionally, as they need to replace cells that have been lost because of injury or cell death **(liver, fibroblasts),** and for the rest of the time they are in the GO phase. When the surrounding situation and environment changes and they get the impulse to start dividing, they will resume cell cycle and go on. 

### _Interphase_ 

In **interphase** the cell replicates its DNA **(S phase),** grows **(Gl** and **G2** phases) and monitors the internal and extemal environment to decide whether to proceed to the next phase **(checkpoints).** 

What would happen if the interphase - apparently uneventful - lasted just long enough for DNA replicati on and nothing more than that? A group of cells which get smaller and smaller would be obtained, since they do not have time to grow and expand, so every time they divide, their dimension diminishes. Instead, if the cell is given enough time to grow, at the end of the process daughter cells will have the same size at the mother, so growth is needed to maintain cell cycle. 

There are actually some situations, for example during **embryogenesis,** in which the gap phases are not present, and cells do not grow but just divide as quickly as possible. This is due to the fact that the egg cell is quite big and needs to be divided into smaller cells. 

During the G 1 phase, the cell is metabolically active, and duplicates its organelles and cytosolic components. In the S phase, DNA is replicated: each chromosome replicates to form two sister chromatids held at the centromere. During the G2 phase, enzyme and protein synthesis occur, ATP is produced in large amounts, and centrioles replicate. 

68

Let's considera group of cells and stain their DNA with a fluorescent dye. Then, they are sorted through a machine which can analyze the level of fluorescence for each single cell. A graph is obtained, where the intensity of fluorescence is converted into the amount ofDNA in the cell, so that it is possible to determine in which phase of the cell cycle the cells are . 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
E  G2/M<br>::, e:  B<br><!-- End of picture text -->

Each portion of the graph corresponds to a phase of the cell cycle: cells in G2 and M phases have **twice** the amount of **DNA** than cells in G 1 phase. 

## _Discovery of MPF (maturation promotingfactor) and studies on yeasts_ 

A key contribution to the definiti on of cell cycle was the discovery of the **maturation promoting factor** (MPF), using oocytes from frogs, arrested in G2 phase until a hormonal stimulation triggers them to enter the M phase of meiosis. Scientists injected the cytoplasm of another cell in M phase into the oocyte, which was released from the G2 phase and entered the M phase; instead, if they inj ected the cytoplasm of a cell in interphase, the oocyte would not enter the M phase. They interpreted these results by hypothesizing the existence of a soluble factor - the maturation promoting factor - that was inducing cells to get to the following step of the cycle. 

During the 60s and the 70s, many other studies were carried out using **yeasts,** organisms in which all the different phases of the cell cycle were clearly visible under the microscope. _Saccharomyces cerevisiae_ was implied: it is a **budding** yeast, meaning that it divides by budding, so it grows a bud that then enlarges, and at the end of M phase becomes almost as big as the mother cell. The size of the bud is proportional to the phase of the cell cycle, and thanks to this property, it was possible to study mutants, which would stop at specific phases of the cycle. Scientists were able to identify an important **mutant,** the **Cdc28,** which caused the cell cycle to arrest at the G 1 to S transition: especially in yeasts, it seemed that when cells passed this transition, they committed to complete the full cycle. The same gene was identified in another kind ofyeast, and it was discovered that it encoded fora **kinase.** 

Another important study was carried out on **sea urchin embryos,** which go through a series of rapid cell divisions. Scientist found out that the entry in M phase required new protein synthesis for these embryos, and they managed to identify two proteins, called **cyclins,** that had a periodic pattern of accumulation and degradation, and were involved in the induction of the M phase. 

Finally, in the 80s, the maturation promoting factor was purified from frog eggs and it was discovered of being composed of two key subunits, a **cyclin** Band **Cdc2** (analogue to Cdc28). Scientists also found out that cyclins are **regulatory** subunits required for the catalytic activity of the Cdc2 protein kinase. These discoveries were consistent with the notion that the maturation promoting factor activity is controlled by the periodic accumulation and degradation of cyclin B during cell cycle progression. 

69 

### _How is the cell cycle controlled?_ 

The cell cycle control system guarantees that the events of the cell cycle occur in a set sequence and that each process has been completed before the next one begins. It employs molecular breaks or **checkpoints,** used to pause the cycle at certain transition points to gather **feedback** from inside and outside the cell. The control system does not trigger the next step in the cycle unless the cell is properly prepared. 

To enter the S phase, the environment must be favorable, and before the entrance in the mitotic phase, all DNA must have been replicated and possible DNA damage must have been repaired. In the M phase, sister chromatids can be pulled apart only ifthey are properly attached to the mitotic spindle. 

The cell cycle is composed of a series of molecular switches that operate in a defined sequence and orchestrate the main events of the cycle, including DNA replication and the segregation of sister chromatids. The cell-cycle control system govems the cell-cycle machinery by cyclically activating and then inactivating the **key proteins** involved in DNA replication, mitosis, and cytokinesis, by **phosphorylation** and **dephosphorylation. Kinases** perform phosphorylation, which is the addition of a phosphate group (P04), while dephosphorylation (removal of a phosphate group) is carried out by **phosphatases.** 

Kinases catalyze the transfer of the phosphate group of ATP to a serine, a threonine, or a tyrosine (they all have a hydroxyl group that makes the addition ofthe phosphate group possible) on the protein. Cells contain many different kinases and phosphatases; some of these are highly specific for one or a few proteins, whereas others act on a broad range of target proteins. Phosphorylation can either activate or inactivate a protein and phosphorylation and dephosphorylation cycles allow proteins to switch rapidly from one state to another. 

### _Cyclins-Cdk complexes_ 

Different **cyclin-Cdk complexes** trigger different steps of the cell cycle: some of them act exactly in correspondence of checkpoints, while others act at other transition points that are not strictly checkpoints. What was previously called MPF is called M-Cdk, and it allows the transition from the G to the M phase. 

These complexes help the transition from one phase to the other andare themselves regulated on multiple levels: 

- one level of regulation involves a **cyclic pattern** of cyclins, which is achieved because cyclins **accumulate** and then get **degraded.** Cdks are always present, but they are inactive in absence of cyclins, which are the regulatory subunits, whose levels vary during the cell cycle owing to their regulated synthesis and degradation. In regulation by cyclin concentration, a complex of **APC** (anaphase-promoting complex, which is a ubiquitin ligase) ubiquinates cyclin and sends it to the proteasome for degradation. Thus, cyclin gets disrupted and the cyclin-Cdk complex becomes inactive. This kind of degradation is responsible for the oscillation of cyclin levels during the cell cycle. 

- another level of regulation is the modulation of the activity of the complexes through **phosphorylation** or **dephosphorylation.** In modulation by phosphorylation, the M-Cdk complex (MPF) is inhibited by phosphorylation: when it gets phosphorylated, even if there is a cyclin 

70 

   - bound to it, the complex is inactive. A phosphatase (which in this case is **phosphatase Cdc25)** is needed to remove the phosphate group from the kinase to allow the complex to become active. 

- an additional way is though **Cdk inhibitors.** Inhibitory proteins, like p27, block the assembly or the activity of the cyclin-Cdk complex. 

A delay in progress through the cycle can be induced at various transition points, and a combination of the previously described mechanisms is used to be sure that before a transition point is passed, all conditions needed for the cell to successfully complete the following phase are satisfied. Thus, these mechanisms allow the cell to make decisions on whether to progress or not in the cell cycle. 

### _Checkpoints in the celi cycle_ 

A **checkpoint** is a moment in the cycle when the cell stops and asks itself if the conditions are sufficient to proceed. If not, the cell has to determine if it can wait and fix the problem, and if the answer is negative, in most cases the cell will descend to death through **apoptosis.** Instead, ifthe issue can be fixed, the cell can fix what was not done in the proper way and proceed to the following phase. 

There are three main checkpoints in the cell cycle: 

- **START,** also called **restriction point,** which occurs late in G 1: it is the decisi on of the cell whether to divide or not. 

- **G2 to M checkpoint,** in which the cell checks if it has properly duplicated DNA without damages and if everything is in place to proceed with the division into daughter cells. 

- **Metaphase-anaphase transition:** metaphase and anaphase are two mitotic phases, during which sister chromatids are separated into daughter cells. In order for this to happen, chromatids must be properly aligned and attached to the spindle, which is a structure of microtubules that pulls sister chromatids apart and allows them to travel to the daughter cells. 

## _Contro/ of_ G _1/S checkpoint_ 

In G **1** phase, Cdks are inactive, and in order to proceed to the S phase the presence of **growth factors** is required, and the DNA must not be damaged. If these conditions are satisfied, the cell **commits to cell division,** activates the cyclin/Cdk complex and proceeds to the S phase. 

During the G 1 phase, the cell monitors the presence of growth factors, which induce the production of cyclins, required to pass the START (restriction) point. **Mitogens** (growth factors) act by switching on cell signaling pathways that stimulate the synthesis of G 1 cyclins, G 1/S cyclins, and other proteins involved in DNA synthesis and chromosome duplication ( everything that is needed for the S phase ). 

Cells present a **negative regulator** in the **retino blastoma** (Rb) protein, which actively prevents the activation of genes required for cell proliferation. After mitogens have induced intracellular signaling pathways and cyclins have been produced, the activation of cyclin/Cdk complexes triggers the phosphorylation of the retinoblastoma protein that therefore becomes inactive, so the transcription of genes required for the S phase can occur. 

The **origin recognition complex** (ORC) sits on the origins of replication throughout the cell cycle, and in order for the cell to enter the S phase, the ORC recruits **Cdc6** (right at the beginning ofthe G 1 phase) and together they recruit DNA helicase and load it on top of the DNA in correspondence to the origin of 

71 

replication. Once the transition point has been passed, **S-Cdk** activates helicase and DNA replication starts; S-Cdk also prevents re-replication of DNA by inducing the degradation of Cdc6, otherwise the genetic materiai would be replicated twice. 

- Ifthe DNA is damaged, **protein p53** which is a transcription factor itself- is activated, and blocks the celi cycle, by inducing the transcription of a **Cdk inhibitor (p21),** which represses the cyclin/Cdk complex. If the DNA damage cannot be fixed, p53 induces the celi to undergo apoptosis. 

_Contro/ ofG2 to M checkpoint_ 

To enter the M phase from the G2 phase, the DNA must be fully replicated and intact, without any damage. If so, it is possible to proceed to the next phase by the activation of mitotic cyclin/Cdk and the entering of the cell in mitosis.
