In G2, the **M-Cdk** complex is kept inactive by phosphorylation, but if the checkpoint is passed, **phosphatase Cdc25** steps in and dephosphorylates the M-Cdk complex, making it active and allowing the celi to enter mitosis. 

If, instead, conditions are not good to pass from the G2 to the M phase, DNA damage is perceived by **ATM or ATR** protein **kinases,** which respectively recognized **double-strand breaks** and **singlestranded or unreplicated DNA.** When these proteins get activated, they phosphorylate checkpoint kinases **CHKl** and **CHK2,** which phosphorylate and inhibit Cdc25 phosphatase. As long as Cdc25 phosphatase is phosphorylated, it stays inactive, hence the cyclin/Cdk complex is inactive as well and the cell cycle is arrested. The cell gets stalled before mitosis until the double-strand/single-strand breaks or unreplicated DNA are fixed. 

## _Contro/ to metaphase to anaphase transition_ 

The control of **metaphase to anaphase transition** is the last checkpoint. The M-Cdk complex is active, because the cell is undergoing **mitosis,** until it reaches metaphase, a mitotic stage at which chromosomes should be attached to the spindle ( a system of microtubules that allows the pulling of sister chromatids to the daughter cells) and properly aligned (to ensure proper distribution and segregation ofDNA). If so, the cell can proceed to anaphase, where sister chromatids are pulled, and their separation can take place. 

An essential event far metaphase to anaphase transition is the activation of the **anaphase promoting complex** (APC/C), which is a **ubiquitin ligase.** Ubiquitin is a sort of flag put on proteins that need to be degraded, so a ubiquitin ligase is a protein that attaches ubiquitin to other proteins to signal that they have to be degraded. The APC complex marks proteins that have to be disrupted, which in this case include **securin** and **cyclins,** which will undergo **proteasome-mediated degradation.** 

The APC complex Is activated by M-Cdk, but a second level of contrai is present, since some sensors are present, and their function is to control if chromosomes are properly attached and aligned: 

- if they are not, they **inhibit Cdc20.** 

- when instead everything is fine, Cdc20 is activated, released from these sensors, and can go to complete the activation ofthe APC complex. 

72 

Indeed, the APC complex is kept inactive as long as there are **mis-alignments** and **mis-attachment** of chromosomes. When activated, the APC complex marks cyclins and securin for degradation. Securin is a **separase inhibitor,** meaning that it is a protein that sequesters another protein called separase: as long as the two proteins are bound to each other, the sister chromatids stay together, and anaphase cannot go on. By degrading securin, the APC complex allows separase to perform its job, which involves the **removal** ( cleavage) of **cohesins** holding together sister chromatids, which are finally pulled to the daughter cells. 

73 

### **Celi division** 

### _Prokaryotic replication_ 

Prokaryotes divide by **binary fission,** which implies expansion of the cell, duplication of the genome driven by ORI points (separating the new DNAs, that are pulled to the opposite sites of the expanding cell), and cytokinesis (mediated by bacterial complexes). 

### _Mitosis and meiosis: differences_ 

**Mitosis** is a pathway exploited for asexual reproduction in unicellular eukaryotes and for growth, homeostasis, and tissue regeneration in multicellular organisms. **Meiosis,** instead, is used for sexual reproduction in both unicellular and multicellular organisms. Cells in the lowest layer of the skin continuously reproduce in order to replenish cells in the upper layer, which progressively die, and this is an example of mitosis. The same occurs in the liver, whose cells do not usually proliferate much, but if some tissue is lost (for example it is taken off in surgery), mitosis is activated to replenish it. 

## Mitosis 

Mitosis is the process of nucleus division, occurring in the **M phase,** and slightly overlapping with **cytokinesis,** which is the real pinching of the plasma membrane that generates the daughter cells. 

### _Preparation far the M phase_ 

In preparation for the M phase, DNA has been replicated (in S phase) and its two copies **(sister chromatids)** remain tightly bounded to each other thanks to the **cohesin complex,** assembled along the entire length of the chromatids. This is qui te important for their alignment and for keeping track of sister chromatids in the cell without losing them; moreover, the cohesion between sister chromatids is vital for their segregation in the daughter celi. 

Another important structure is the **centrosome,** a region in the DNA that has a particular sequence attracting **kinetochore proteins,** which enable chromosomes to be attached to the **spindle,** a structure that allows the pulling of chromatids to the two daughter cells. Before the M phase starts, the centrosome must be duplicated as well, since daughter cells must inherit one copy of it; at the beginning, the two copies will stay together on one side of the nucleus, and only after they will start to move apart. The centrosome contains a pair of **centrioles,** whose function is nucleating microtubules, formed of **tubulin heterodimers,** which assemble together in **protofilaments,** thirteen of which form a **hollow tube.** The essential feature of microtubules is their **dynamic instability** which allows them to grow and shrink to perform their task: indeed, the affinity of tubulin dimers for the surrounding ones is higher when they are bound to GTP and lower when bound to GDP. When GTP is bound, dimers are added to a growing microtubule to generate a **GTP cap,** which stabilizes the microtubule and helps it keep growing. However, if the addition of new molecules is slower than GTP hydrolysis, the extremity of the microtubule will be GDP-bound (thus less stable), and the subunits will start to detach. 

74 

## _Mphase_ 

M phase starts with the activation of **cyclin M,** a complex that allows mitosis to occur by inducing **chromatin condensation, nucleus envelope breakdown, fragmentation** of the **Golgi apparatus,** and **spindle formation.** Mitosis is divided into different phases. 

## _1. Prophase_ 

During **prophase,** chromosomes condense, and the mitotic spindle assembles. To prepare DNA for mitosis, the cell devotes a great deal of energy to gradually reorganize sister chromatids into distinct structures that can be pulled apart more easily in anaphase. 

Duplicated chromosomes need to condense, otherwise pulling them apart would cause their breaking; by condensing, instead, they become tightly packed, and consequently it is easier to separate them. the chromosome condensation reduces mitotic chromosomes to **compact bodies** that can be more easily segregated. 

There are two processes taking part in this reorganization: first of all, **sister chromatid resolution** occurs, when the two chromatids become separable units, and then the actual **chromosome condensation** takes piace, with DNA getting compacted almost a thousand times. As a result, when the celi reaches metaphase, the sister chromatids appear in the microscope as **compact, rodlike structures,joined** tightly at their centromeric regions, and only loosely along their arms. 

During resolution, the two chromatids need to be decatenated (separated from each other) and this involves the **partial removal** of **cohesins,** even though the chromatids remain tightly joined at the centromere, but are looser at the extremities. Cohesin keeps the sister chromatids together, while **condensins** enwrap single chromatids, allowing them to be compacted in an ordered manner and in a **ring-like** structure. Phosphorylation of condensing subunits by M-Cdk stimulates this coiling activity, providing one mechanism by which M-Cdk may promote chromosome restructuring in early mitosis. 

Moreover, during prophase the mitotic spindle is assembled. Centrosomes have been duplicated before the start of the M phase, and during prophase the two centrosomes be gin to move apart from each other and to nucleate microtubules, in a radiai array known as **aster.** Asters move to opposi te sides of the nucleus, which are called **poles** of the mitotic spindle. In the meanwhile, as mitosis starts, there is an **increase** of the **dynamic instability** of microtubules, which become more dynamic, keeping shrinking and growing, extending and retracting in all directions, until the interactions between microtubules from two different asters (at the opposite pole) become stable. Interacting microtubules from opposite poles become more stable, are called **interpolar microtubules,** and are essential for the formation of the spindle, which is only completed in metaphase. 

## _2. Prometaphase_ 

Two main events occur during prometaphase: 

- the **nuclear envelope breakdown.** The spindle needs to attach to chromosomes to pull them apart and in order to do so the nuclear envelope must be broken down. The nuclear envelope has an internal and external membrane and a nuclear lamina made of a meshwork intermediate filaments. The nuclear envelope is in continuity with the endoplasmic reticulum: RER is mostly composed 

75 

of sheets, some of them making up the nuclear envelope; SER is mostly made of tubes, and is more abundant in the periphery of the cell. When the nuclear envelop breaks down, it is divided into pieces that go back to the endoplasmic reticulum itself and are absorbed by it. The phosphorylation of nuclear envelop proteins and of laminins is what promotes this breakdown. 

- the **chromosome attachment** to the mitotic spindle: once the nuclear envelop has been broken down, microtubules crossing the cytosol in the cell can get in contact with fully condensed chromosomes (and this was not possible before owing to the presence of the nuclear envelope) The centromere is recognized by **kinetochore proteins,** which - together with other connecting proteins - capture microtubules and stick to them. Each duplicated chromosome becomes linked to both spindle poles **(biorientation),** but since the two chromatids mostly face opposite directions, kinetochores are most likely to be attached to microtubules coming from different spindle poles. When this happens, **tension** at the centromere is generated, since the spindle poles pull in opposite directions, and this is a feedback that tells the cell that chromosomes are properly attached to the spindle. 

In a complete spindle, it is possible to find three kinds of microtubules: 

- **astrai microtubules,** which keep growing and shrinking in all directions. 

- **interpolar microtubules,** which stabilize the spindle and hold it in place by interacting with each other. 

- **kinetochore microtubules,** which randomly encounter kinetochores and get attached to them, thus being responsible for the attachment and the pulling of chromosomes. 

## _3. Metaphase_ 

During metaphase, the kinetochore microtubules on each sister chromatid are attached to opposite poles of the spindle and **line up** at the **equator** of the spindle, halfway between the two spindle poles. 

This line up generates the **metaphase plate,** and the forces bringing chromosomes to the equator involve the continuous growth and shrinkage of microtubules and the action of **microtubule motor proteins,** like **kinesin** - which walks towards the plus end-, and dynein - which walks towards the minus end. At the equatorial plane, the forces balance each other and pull the chromosome back and forth with continuous micro-adjustments. 

## _4. Anaphase_ 

In anaphase there is the **degradation** of **cohesins,** which is the driving event for the separation of chromosomes: indeed, once cohesins are degraded, the tension can act and pull chromatids in opposite directions. This process is triggered by the **anaphase promoting complex,** which degrades **securin,** which was sequestering the enzyme separase and keeping it inactive. Once securin is degraded, **separase** can act as a scissor, by degrading cohesins and pulling chromatids apart. 

Two independent and simultaneous processes occur in anaphase and contribute to the movement of chromatids to the poles: 

- **anaphase A,** during which the driving force is the **loss of tubulin** subunits from kinetochore microtubules, which consequently shrink and pull microtubules to the poles. 

76 

- **anaphase B,** during which the driving farce is provided by **motor proteins,** which move the poles themselves towards the two apposite sides of the cell. This is due to the action of both kinesin and dynein in different locations. Dynein acts on astral microtubules that connect the spindle to the plasma membrane, by pulling apart the two spindles towards the plasma membrane in apposite parts. Kinesin, instead, acts on interpolar microtubules, pushing them to slide on each other, in such a way that pulls the two poles closer. 

   - These two forces combine and allow chromosome separation on the two sides of the daughter cells. 

How can kinetochores remain attached to a microtubule that is losing subunits and shrinking? Two hypothesis have been formulated to answer this question: 

- one possibility is that motor proteins acting at kinetochores use the energy from **ATP hydrolysis** to pull chromosomes along the kinetochore microtubules, which depolymerize as a consequence. 

- another possibility is that microtubule depolymerization itself drives the unit, without ATP: the energy driving the movement is thus stored in microtubules and released when they depolymerize (shrinking occurs at the plus end as well, and the minus end is just that attached to the spindle). 

However, both processes make take part to chromosome movements, even though they are not still totally understood. 

### _Telophase_ 

During telophase, sister chromatids are situated at apposite poles, and the **nuclear envelope** that was previously destroyed must be **reassembled,** while the **spindle** must be **broken down.** The nuclear envelope reassembly requires the **dephosphorylation** of nuclear core proteins and laminins, which enables them to reform (patch by patch) the wide sheet covering the genetic material. Then, nuclear poles are reassembled at their place, and mitosis can be declared finished. 

Meanwhile the spindle is disassembled, there is the start of the cytoskeletal assembly of the **contractile ring,** which will drive **cytokinesis** ( division of plasma membrane). The contractile ring is made of actin and myosin filaments, starts to appear at telophase, and is the main player in cytokinesis. 

### _Cytokinesis_ 

During **cytokinesis,** the cytoplasm is split by the contractile ring, which pinches and pulls the plasma membrane until the two cells are separated from each other. The pinching occurs at positions that were previously determined by the spindle: the plane of the spindle drives the position of the **furrowing,** always in a plane perpendicular to the long axis of the spindle. If after the furrow starts to appear, the poles of the spindle are displaced, the furrowing disappears and rearranges again to become perpendicular to the new axis. 

There is a signal coming from interpolar microtubules (which overlap with each other halfway between the two poles), activating and recruiting proteins that signal the contractile ring to form at a position midway between the spindle poles, and that dictated the position of the furrowing. 

77 

_Celi division: shape and size_ 

## Division can be either: 

- a **symmetric division,** with the spindle located centrally in the cell, and daughter cells that are more or less of the same size. It is the most common type of cell division in proliferating cells. 

- an **asymmetric division,** common in embryonic development, with the spindle that is asymmetrically and peripherally located. Cytokinesis occurs in a peripherical region and not in the center, so that daughter cells have different sizes and different inherited molecules, so they inherit different cell types. 

The contractile ring is composed of **actin** and **myosin** filaments and attaches to protein that bind the plasma membrane, so that it is able to pinch and pull it and generate its cleavage. The contractile force pulling the plasma membrane is generated by the sliding on top of each other of the actin and myosin filaments, so that the diameter of the ring gets smaller and smaller, and being attached to the plasma membrane, the latter is invaginated until two cells are generated. The contractile ring is a **transient** structure, and lasts only for cytokinesis to occur. 

During interphase, the cell is very attached to the surrounding environment, but during mitosis it needs to round up to let cell division occur. This dramatic rearrangement in its shape allows the tissue itself to accommodate the new incoming cells. Indeed, many animal cells during cell division change their cell shape and **decrease** the **adherence** of the cell to its neighbors, to the extracellular matrix, or both. This cycle of attachment and detachment allows cells to rearrange their contacts, so that new cells can be accommodated within the tissue. 

### _Organelles distribution to the daughter cells_ 

The endoplasmic reticulum most likely retains its structure as is pinched off together with the plasma membrane in cytokinesis, and it is equally divided between the daughter cells. 

The Golgi apparatus gets fragmented during mitosis in smaller vesicles, which are randomly distributed to the daughter cells by association with spindle microtubules. 

Mitochondria and chloroplasts are doubled in number at every cells cycle, until they reach an amount that enables their fairly even segregation into daughter cells. Mitochondria divide like bacteria, by binary fission. 

Other elements, like endosomes, ribosomes and proteins, are randomly inherited when the cell divides. 

_Drugs affecting microtubules_ 

**Colchicine** inhibits microtubule polymerization, while **taxol** prevents microtubule depolymerization, but both inhibit mitosis and therefore are both used for cancer treatments, since mitosis gets out of control. 

Colchicine binds tightly to free tubulin dimers and prevents their polymerization into microtubules: when a cell in mitosis is exposed to colchicine, the mitotic spindle rapidly disappears, and the cell stalls in the middle of mitosis, unable to partition chromosomes into two groups. Taxol, instead, binds to microtubules preventing their depolymerization: when a cell in mitosis is exposed to taxol, the disassembly of microtubules required for chromosome segregation is blocked, and mitosis is arrested. 

78 

### **Meiosis** 

### _Asexual vs sexual reproduction in eukaryotes_ 

**In asexual** reproduction there is only one parent, so the offspring is its clone, and is genetically identical to it. In **sexual** reproduction, instead, two parents are involved, so the offspring has a novel combination of chromosomes and genes compared to the parents (higher **genetical variability).** 

### _Karyotype_ 

By observing a metaphasic **karyotype,** it is possible to notice that humans have twenty-two pairs of homologs ( of matemal and patemal origin), each composed of two sister chromatids. Chromosome twenty-three, instead, is the sexual chromosome, different in males and females. 

Overall, humans have **forty-six** chromosome, organized in twenty-three pairs, each of them comprised of one chromosome of matemal origin and one of patemal origin. 

When referring to one single copy of each chromosome type (n), in humans, a cell with n = 23 would be called **haploid.** Instead, when referring to both copies of each chromosome type (2n), in humans, a cell bearing 2n = 46 would be called **diploid.** 

During metaphase, four copies of each chromosome are present, so the total number of chromosomes would be 92. 

### _Sexual reproduction_ 

Sexual reproduction involves both haploid and diploid cells. Starting from two diploid parents (2n), each of them with a set of chromosomes, through the process of **meiosis,** gametes can be formed. Gametes are haploid cells, so they have only one set of chromosomes (n), and through the process of fertilization, the two haploid cells fuse with each other to form a diploid celi **(zygote),** that will divide by mitosis to generate a full diploid organism (keeping the 2n stable throughout all the cycle of the organism, except in gametes). 

Meiosis is the process used to produce gametes, also called **gametogenesis,** and is carried out in specialized organs **(gonads)** from specialized cells **(germ-line cells).** Only gametes propagate genetic information to the next generati on, and a crociai implication of this fact is that if there is a mutation in a gamete, it will be passed off the offspring. On the other hand, a mutation in a cell that is not a gamete will not be transmitted to the offspring. 

### _Overall functions of meiosis_ 

- reduce chromosome number from diploid to haploid (from 2n ton). 

- ensure that each daughter celi has one full set of chromosomes. 

- promote **genetic diversity** among the products. 

The first event occurring in meiosis is DNA replications: starting from a 2n celi, owing to chromosome replication, the cell reaches 4n. Then, through a first step of cell division known **as meiosis I,** the 4n cells divides into two 2n daughter cells. Afterwards, a second round of meiosis takes piace **(meiosis 11),** during which the 2n daughter cells give rise to four haploid cells (n). This means that during meiosis there is 

79 

one round of cell replication followed by two rounds of cell division, and between the two consequent rounds of cell division there is not an additional DNA replication that would ensure to obtain diploid cells at the end of the second division. 

## Meiosis I 

During meiosis I there is the separation of homolog chromosomes. Let's consider chromosome I as an example: the cell possesses two copies of chromosome I, one of matemal origin and one of patemal origin. During DNAreplication, each ofthem replicates, leading to two sister chromatids from the mater and two from the father; there four sister chromatids - all chromosome I - together are called **homologs.** 

In meiosis I, the matemal inherited chromosome will be divided from the patemal one: at the end of the process, the daughter cells will be still diploid (2n) but will have half of the chromosomes. 

Similarly to mitosis, also meiosis I can be divided in a series of steps. 

_Prophasel_ 

Prophase I can be in tum divided into five stages: 

- **leptotene,** when chromosome condensation and pairing occurs. 

- **zygotene,** when the **synaptonemal complex** is formed. The synaptonemal complex is a complex that allows the two homologs to be kept together, as they were zippered. 

- **pachytene,** during which crossing over takes place, allowing to further increase genetic variability. 

- **diplotene,** when the synaptonemal complex is almost completely disassembled. 

- **diakinesis,** when the bivalent are ready for metaphase. 

During leptotene, each chromosome pairs with its homolog in the nucleus, according to a mechanism that has not been still understood: apparently, the **telomers** - specific regions of each chromosome - have specific interactions with the nuclear envelope and start to cluster together, allowing the centromere of each chromosome to find its homologous and pair with it. The so-called **bouquet** is formed. Another important process taking place in this phase is the formation of **double-strand breaks:** breaks in the DNA from, and they activate the DNA repair system, which works by finding homologous sequences in order to repair whatever damage as occurred. Thus, by inducing these DNA breaks, the process of pairing homologous sequences is triggered, and homologs are able to find each other. 

Once the pairing occurs, there is the formation **oftetrads** (or **bivalent),** which are pairs oftwo homologs, each comprising two chromatids from the mother and two from the father. This occurs between zygotene and pachytene, and the chromosomes are held together by a protein complex (known as **synaptonemal complex)** which looks like a zipper, and assembles the two homologs, keeping them attached. The sister chromatids are held together by **cohesin,** and are attached to their corresponding homolog by the synaptonemal complex, which is transient, being only present from zygotene to diplotene. Cohesins will still be holding together the sister chromatids, while the synaptonemal complex stitches the homologs together. By the end of prophase I, however, it will be dismantled, but its function is crucial, since it enables the pairing of the two homologs and the crossing over. 

80 

At pachytene, the assembly process started at zygotene is complete, and the homologs are synapsed along their entire lengths. At diplotene, after the crossing aver has finished, the synaptonemal complex will be disassembled. 

At pachytene, **crossing over** takes place: it is a homologous recombination between two non-sister chromatids, one of patemal origin and one of matemal origin. This recombination results in the paternal and matemal chromatids physically swapping their genetic material, thus increasing the genetic variability. Each crossing aver gives rise to an x-shaped structure, called **chiasma,** and numerous chiasmata may happen on the same tetrad. What happens during crossing aver is a double-strand break in one the DNA helix of the chromatids, which triggers nucleases to digest the ends of the helices that have been broken apart. This induces **homology recombination,** in arder to replenish the lost sequences: however, instead of looking far the homologous sequence on the sister chromatids (as happened in a normal mitotic cell), a strand exchange occurs, and the homology is found in the non-sister chromatid in the tetrad. Thus, DNA synthesis takes place, repairing the gaps and generating a crossing of the helices, resolved again by double-strand breaks. A chiasma is generated, since the matemal chromatid is partially combined with the patemal one and vice versa, and on top ofthis, they are also crisscrossed in a structure called chiasma. Crossing aver is vital to generate **diversity** and hold homologs together: indeed, a chiasma is needed since before the end of prophase I ( way before metaphase ), the synaptonemal complex will be dismantled. The chiasma will still hold homologs together even after the disassembly of the synaptonemal complex, and it does so by using cohesins ( exchanged right when DNA was being exchanged). 

Once crossing aver has finished, diplotene can start, so the synaptonemal complex is degraded and there is a partial separation of the non-sister chromatids. The, in diakinesis, which is the transition to metaphase, the nuclear envelop disappears, and homologs attach to the spindle. 

## _Metaphasel_ 

Tetrads are held together by the chiasma, and the two sister chromatids are facing towards the same direction, so when the kinetochore forms, they work as a single unit metaphase and will be attached to microtubules on the same pale. This of meiosis I attachment to apposite poles generates tension between the homologs, but not between the chromatids, since they will be pulled to the same pale. The sister chromatids now are not identica! to each other, but they differ owing to the crossing aver. **a single unit** 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
metaphase<br>of meiosis I<br>a single unit<br>anaphase<br>of meiosis I<br><!-- End of picture text -->

## _Anaphase I_ 

Cohesins that were holding together the two sister chromatids are degraded along the arms, hence resolving the chiasma and freeing the sister chromatids, which however remain associated at the centromere, where cohesin is left. The forced pulling the kinetochore are now allowed to separate the two homolog one from the other. Anaphase I terminates with the sister chromatids of the homologs at apposite poles. 

81 

### _Cytokinesis I_ 

Cytokinesis takes place, and at the end of meiosis I each daughter cell has **half** the number of chromosomes ofthe parent cells. 

### _Meiosis II_ 

During meiosis II, the two chromatids will be separated without previous replication, so that the daughter cells will be **haploid.** It is important to remember that in meiosis II the sister chromatids may not be identica! because of crossing over, and that the number of chromosomes at the equatorial plate is half the number of those in meiosis. The kinetochores of the two sister chromatids are now facing opposite directions (as occurred in mitosis) and the microtubules will pull them to opposite poles. Indeed, the tension is now generated between the sister chromatids, which are pulled in opposite directions towards the daughter cells. 

### _Advantages of meiosis_ 

The greatest advantage of meiosis is that it generates **great genetic variability,** by: 

- the **independent assortment of homologs** into gametes. Indeed, when homologs line up during metaphase I, the matemal and patemal chromosomes are **shuffled randomly,** so that each daughter cell will have some matemal and some patemal chromosomes, and not all from the same parent. Each gamete will contain the matemal version of some chromosomes and the patemal version of others; thus, owing to this process, an individua! could in principle produce **2n** genetically different gametes. In humans, this number is 2<sup>23</sup> , so the potential number of genetically different gametes that one could generate only due to independent assortment of homologous is over a million. 

- **crossing over.** About two or three crossovers occur on average between each pair of human homologs, and crossing over takes place at random sites along the length of a chromosome. In the end, each meiosis produces four sets of entirely novel chromosomes. 

Each homologous chromosome contains the same genes in the same order, but **gene variants** ( called **allei es),** different from mother to father, might exist. Alleles are just as likely to represent an improvement as to change to the worse; however, they become an evolutionary advantage especially in an unpredictably variable environment, where having the possibility to generate a multitude of possible different combinations of genes in the offspring would increase the likelihood of generating at least one individua! able to survive. Moreover, sexual reproduction also speeds up the elimination of deleterious alleles. 

Still, meiosis is not flawless: **nondisjunction events** between homologs may occur, meaning that they might not be able to separate properly, leading to gametes with either one less copy of a chromosome or an extra one. This phenomenon is called **aneuploidy,** and most of the time the embryos forming from gametes which lack a chromosome or have an extra one are compatible with life, but will show genetic syndromes, like **Down's syndrome.** This event of non-disjunction occurs more frequently during oogenesis (is female gametogenesis), increases with age, and represents the most frequent cause of m1scamages. 

**82** 

### _Male and female gametes_ 

The female **egg** is incredibly big with respect to any other human cells; it is very **large, non-motile,** and is a gamete specialized for **nutrient storage.** Instead, the male gamete - the **sperm** - is small, motile, and specialized for movement. Fertilization occurs when the haploid egg and sperm get together, forming a diploid **zygote** that then, through mitosis, will generate the fully diploid organism. 

### _Spermatogenesis_ 

**Spermatogenesis** starts from puberty, when **spermatogonia** begin to proliferate though mitosis. In some spermatogonia, meiosis is periodically activated, and those spermatogonia are referred to as **primary spermatocytes,** which go from a diploid cell (2n) to four haploid cells (n)- called **spermatids** -passing through two **secondary spermatocytes** (2n) during mitosis I. Spermatids need to differentiate to acquire their most important features to move, and once they have gained them, they become mature gametes. From each progenitor, four gametes are obtained in spermatogenesis, which is a continuous process; the entire meiosis is completed in days or weeks at most. 

### _Oocyte meiosis_ 

Oocyte meiosis starts before birth, and the germ-line cells are called **oogonia.** They diff erentiate through mitosis, and only a definite number ofthem starts meiosis, becoming **primary oocytes** (around 400.000, which will not increase ). Before birth, these primary oocytes undergo **diplotene arrest,** remaining in this diplotene phase (prophase I) until puberty. During that period, chromosomes are not fully condensed and can be actively transcribed, so that the oocyte can grow and accumulate the materials it needs to possibly undergo fertilization. 

From puberty to menopause there is a periodica! activation of meiosis I upon hormonal stimulation, which occurs only a few cells at a time: the re-activation of the phase allows the primary oocyte to complete meiosis I, becoming a **secondary oocyte.** What is peculiar about oocyte meiosis I is that it is an **asymmetric division,** where the two daughter cells are one secondary oocyte and one **polar body,** which will eventually die. This happens because the oocyte's purpose is to store nutrients, and to do so the cell must be very big. After meiosis I, there is not the re-formati on of the nuclear envelop nor the decondensation of chromosomes, and again another arrest occurs, now at metaphase II. The secondary oocyte stops in metaphase II until it gets fertilized, when then it will progress to the end of meiosis II. 

From one progenitor, only one gamete is obtained **in** oogenesis, which is a **discontinuous** process that continuously stops and resumes; meiosis II is completed only upon fertilization, so it can possibly be infinite. 

### _F ertilization_ 

The sperm is rapidly attracted to an ovulated cell by signals that are sent by the egg itself and by supporting cells in the ovary. When one or more sperm cells meet the egg, they migrate through a layer (called **zona pellucida)** surrounding the egg, thanks to the presence of specific sperm organelles **(acrosomes)** that contain lytic enzymes that allow them to dig their way to the plasma membrane. Once sperm cells have gotten there, they can bind to it, causing the activation of **calcium waves,** which lead to the **secretion** of **granules** (which render the zona pellucida harder, not allowing a second sperm to 

83 

access the plasma membrane) and the **reactivation of meiosis** in the egg. Moreover, the nucleus of the sperm enters the cytoplasm of the egg, and the zygote cell that is obtained has two haploid nuclei (male and female **pronuclei),** which start to replicate their DNAs (S phase), until they meet, starting M phase and undergoing the first mitotic division. Thus, the two nuclear envelopes break down, and the two sets of chromosomes are merged together and can align on the same spindle, starting regolar mitosis. 

84 

### **Genetics** 

Genetics is the study of **genes,** how they are inherited, and how variation in genes can affect living organisms. A gene is a genomic sequence, corresponding to a unit of inheritance, which is associated with regulatory regions, and it is a transcribed region. 

A unit of a gene is responsible for and is associated with a **phenotype,** which is an observable characteristic or trait of an organism. The phenotype is how the information encoded in the DNA is translated into an aspect that is shown by the organism. 

A common type ofphenotype are diseases themselves, like normal red blood cells and sickle-shaped red blood cells, which have this shape owing to a phenotype associated with the mutated gene. 

A gene encodes for a phenotype, and this is quite obvious today, but in the past, when there was no idea about DNA, chromosomes, mitosis and meiosis, it was much more mysterious. 

### _Mendel s studies_ 

In 1865 a paper, _Experiments in plant hybridization,_ was published, and showed for the first time that: 

- each organism has physical traits that correspond to invisible elements within the cell. 

- these invisible elements (which now are called **genes)** exist in pairs (since most organisms are diploid). 

- only one member of this genetic pair is passed on to each individua! of the progeny. This invisible element is incorporated into a sperm or egg cell (both haploid). 

**Mendel** was the author of this paper, and his discoveries are collectively known as **Mendel's law.** Between 1856 and 1863 he conducted his experiments on **pea plants** in the garden of the St. Thomas monastery. His experiments led to some generalizations on the way by which traits are inherited, which laterbecame knownas Mendel's laws ofinheritance. The importance ofthis laws, however, became clear only after Mendel's death. 

### _Why pea plants?_ 

He chose pea plants because they have a number of varieties in type (many **different phenotypes);** moreover, it was possible to easily operate on them, since both **self-fertilization** and **hetero-fertilization** are allowed. Moreover, pea plants were not expensive, they did not take up much space, had a brieflicecycle, and produced many offspring per mating. 

He decided to study and observe seven specific and **discrete** ( either in one way or in another, with no shade in between), and easily detectable traits of pea plants to conduct his studies (pea shape, pea color, flower color, flower position, pod shape, pod color, plant height). 

Mendel chose to analyze only one trait per experiment and started all his experiments with plants being different only for the chosen trait. He used **true-breeding** pea plants, which produced offspring of the same variety, when allowed to self-fertilize. 

85 

### _Monohybrid crossing and the Law of Dominance_ 

Mendel started by performing **monohybrid crosses:** the experiment was carried out crossing two strains of plants that differ only in one characteristic (like the color of the flower, either white or purple ). 

He took the pollen from one plant and put it in the other flower. Mendel named P the two parents: he took a true-breeding plant showing yellow peas and another one showing green peas. After performing **cross-fertilization,** he observed that all the offspring in the first generation showed yellow peas. He repeated the same for the other traits he was taking into account: what was common in all crossings, was that the offspring were completely uniform, and it seemed that only one parent transmitted the trait. 

All monohybrid crosses produced an F 1 generati on showing a phenotype identica! to just one of the parents. The trait (phenotype) that always showed up was called **dominant trait,** while the one that never showed up was called **recessive trait.** In this way, Mendel formulated the **law of dominance** (third Mendel's law). 

### _Self-fertilization and the Law of Segregation_ 

Then, he allowed the offspring of the first generation to **self-fertilize** (they were not mated with each other), and in the offspring obtained (second generation, F2) he noticed that **75%** ofthe peas showed the **dominant trait,** while in **25%** of them there was the reappearance of the **recessive trait.** Regardless of the trait he was looking at, he found out that the F2 generation had a ratio of **1 to 3.** 

The phenotype absent in the F 1 generati on reappeared in approximately ¼ of the F 2 offspring: Mendel used the term _elementen_ to indicate hereditary particles transmitted unchanged between generations; these particles are now called **genes.** To explain this, Mendel called the hereditary particles F, and considered the two alternative forms **(alleles),** F (yellow peas, dominant) and f (green peas, recessive). Mendel hypothesized that yellow peas could derive from two different combinations of alleles ( either FF or Ff), while green peas could derive from only from one combination of alleles (ff). 

Regarding the phenotypes analyzed by Mendel, one phenotype is determined by one hereditary particle (one gene), which is present in two **alternative forms,** known as alleles. For each gene, an organism inherits two alleles, one from the mother and one from the father. These alleles may be the same or different: an organism having two identica! alleles fora gene is **homozygous** for that gene (what Mendel called "true-breeding", the plants he started from), while an organism having two different alleles fora gene is **heterozygous** for that gene (the plants ofF1). 

Mendel postulated that when sperm and eggs are formed, the two copies of each gene present in the parent separate so that each gamete receives only one allele for each trait. For his pea plants, each egg ( ovum) and each sperm (pollen) receives only one allele for pea color ( either yellow or green), one allele for pea shape (smooth or wrinkled), one allele for flower color (purple or white), and so on. During fertilization, sperm carrying one or the other allele units with an egg carrying one or the other allele, to produce a fertilized egg or zygote with two alleles. During gamete formation, the two alleles for each gene segregate from each other, so that each gamete carries only one allele for each gene. This is the **law of segregation,** or the first Mendel's law. 

86 

### _Law of Independent Assortment_ 

Mendel then considered two different phenotypes, for instance the color of seeds (Yy; yellow dominant and green recessive) and the shape ofthe seed (Rr; round dominant, wrinkled recessive). Mendel wanted to investigate if each pair of alleles **segregated independently** of the other pairs of alleles during gamete formation. He started from pure-breeding pea plants (yellow and round with green and wrinkled), and obtained a F 1 generation characterized by plants all showing the same traits (round and yellow): this proved that each pair of alleles segregated independently, and the possible gametes were YR, Yr, yR, yr. 

He then performed crossed dihybridization, by crossing the possible gametes of F 1, and obtaining four different phenotypes: yellow round seeds, yellow wrinkled seeds, green round seeds, green wrinkled seeds, with a **9:3:3:1 ratio.** 

Mendel counts revealed for all dihybrid crosses a ratio of 9:3 :3: 1, and this showed that during gamete formation, genes for different traits segregate independently, according to the **law of independent assortment,** or second Mendel's law. 

### _The genetic meaning of meiosis_ 

A single meiosis event gives rise to four gametes, which will differ from one another; instead, mitosis produced daughter cells that are exact genetic copies of parent cells. Genetic diversity is determined by: 

- crossing over events **(recombination)** during meiosis I allows the exchange of reciproca! chromosomal segments. 

- a random alignment **(independent assortment)** of matemal and patemal chromosomes on either side of the metaphase plate. This random alignment causes daughter cells to be completely different. 

Mendel's observations work perfectly for genes carried on different chromosomes. However, when genes are on the same chromosome, their distance aff ect the likelihood of their independent segregation (because of crossing over). 

# _Exceptions to Mendel_ s _law: incomplete dominance_ 

**Complete dominance** occurs when the dominant allele completely masks the effect of the recessive allele in the heterozygous condition. For example, the color of pea plant flowers is a trait with complete dominance, and purple masks the white color when both alleles are present (Ff). 

**Incomplete dominance,** instead, is a sort of intermediate inheritance: one allele is not able to completely mask the second allele, so this results in a **third phenotype.** At a molecular level, incomplete dominance is generally caused by a quantitative effect of the number of doses of a wild-type allele **(haploinsufficiency** in diseases, when the phenotype does show an effect, but not as bad as if it was that of the homozygous individua! for the affected allele). 

# _Exceptions to Mendel_ s _law: codominance_ 

**Codominance** occurs when the contributions of both alleles are visible in the phenotype. In humans, the classic example of co-dominant alleles is the **ABO group** (which is also a good example of multiple 

**87** 

alleles ): AB is heterozygous, since both antigens (phenotypes) on the surface of the red blood cells are expressed. 

_Exceptions to Mendel s law: multiple alleles_ 

Mendel proposed that there are two alleles for every gene (the dominant having its phenotype expressed in a heterozygote ). However, a gene can have more than two allelic forms segregating within a population, and these genes are referred to as having **multiple alleles.** Every individuai still possesses only two alleles ( one materna! and one patema!), no matter how many alleles exist in the population. 

The AB0 blood type is controlled by a single gene, the AB0 gene, located on the long arm of chromosome 9, discovered by Karl **Landsteiner** at the beginning ofthe 20<sup>th</sup> century. The AB0 group is controlled by a single gene, which encodes the enzyme **glycosyl-transferase,** responsible for determining the carbohydrate content at the surface of red blood cells **antigens.** 

## The IA and IB alleles are codominant, while i is recessive. 

Our immune system develops antibodies against the carbohydrates that are not present in the organism: this is why group O can donate to everyone, but can receive only from individuals having the O group. 

Beside the AB0 system, also the **Rh blood group system** can affect transfusion compatibility. Five proteins (antigens) D, C, c, E, and e are the most important components of this system. The commonly used terms "Rh positive" and "Rh negative" refer to the D antigen only. An individuai is either positive or negative for the Rh factor. Blood that is Rh- can be transfused into a person who is Rh+, but an Rhindividual creates antibodies for Rh+. 

### _What is the reaction to the wrong transfusion?_ 

Should a wrong transfusion occur, the first symptoms of the reaction appear within a few minutes and may include: 

- a strong feeling that something bad is about to happen. 

- **fever** and **chills.** 

- **breathing difficulties.** 

- **muscle aches.** 

- feeling **nauseous.** 

- **chest** pains, **abdominal** pain, or **back pain.** 

- pain where the transfusion line is inserted. 

- blood in urine **(hematuria).** 

- **jaundice.** 

Ifa mother is Rh- and the fetus for the first pregnancy is Rh+, during delivery, Rh antigens enter the mother's circulation through breaks in the placenta, thus the mother makes anti-Rh antibodies. lt is important to remember that mother and fetus circulations are separate. During delivery, it is possible that a contamination of fetal blood with the mother's circulation occurs. In the second pregnancy, ifthe fetus is again Rh+, the mother's antibodies (already created in the previous pregnancy), cross the placenta and destroy fetal blood cells. Indeed, Rh antibodies are small enough to cross the embryonic chorion and to 

88 

attack fetal erythrocytes. This induces the **hemolytic disease** in the newborn: the materna! antibodies determine the lysis offetal red blood cells, causing **anemia, hypo-albuminemia, jaundice,** and possibly **fetal death.** To prevent this from happening, **Rh immunoglobulins** coat fetal red blood cells having surface D antigen, making antigen-antibody complexes that move across the placenta before the activation of the materna! immune system. 

### _Penetrane e_ 

**Penetrance** is defined as the proportion of individuals carrying a particular allele/genotype that expresses the associated phenotype. In **complete penetrance,** 100% individuals carrying an allele/genotype also express the corresponding phenotype. In **incomplete penetrance,** less than 100% individuals carrying an allele/genotype also express the corresponding phenotype. 

In medicai genetics, this concept is particularly important in the case of disease-causing alleles. For many inherited diseases, the same mutation is not always expressed in all individuals who carry it. Moreover, when the mutation is expressed, it is not always expressed in the same way. 

**Retinoblastoma** is a cancer ofthe retina that primarily affects children, and it is caused by a mutation in the Rb gene. Not all people who carry this mutation suffer from retinoblastoma: for instance, two sisters could inherit the same mutation from their parents, and one might be aff ected by the disease, while the other may not. Other diseases with incomplete penetrance include **Huntington's disease** and breast cancer. A disease shows complete penetrance if all of the individuals who carry the mutation are affected by the diseases. For instance, the inheritance of a specific point mutation in the fibroblast growth factor receptor 3 gene always results in the disorder **achondroplasia,** characterized by abnormal bone growth and dwarfism. 

### _Expressivity and genetic diseases_ 

**Expressivity** measures the extent to which a genotype exhibits its phenotypic expression. For instance, one patient who is suffering from a disease may have more severe symptoms than another patient who carries the same mutated allele. **Neurofibromatosis** is a disease caused by mutations in the neurofibromin gene, which is a negative regulator of Ras, a protein involved in signal transduction cascades. Mutations cause the development of neurofibromas, non-cancerous (benign) tumors that are usually located on or just under the skin. Family members who carry the same mutated gene can exhibit a range of symptoms, with some individuals experiencing much more severe symptoms than others, although they carry the same allele. 

Phenotypes show differences in penetrance and expressivity owing to the role of **modifier genes, environmental factors,** and **complex genetic** and **environmental interactions.** A genetic modifier is able to alter the expression of another gene; modifier genes can affect transcription and alter the immediate gene transcription expression. **Phenylketonuria** is an example ofhow environment can affect phenotypes: people affected by phenylketonuria lack activity of an enzyme that breaks down the amino acid phenylalanine. If phenylketonuria is not treated, the extra phenylalanine can keep the brain from developing normally, leading to intellectual disability, seizures, and mood disorders. A strict diet low in phenylalanine from a very young age can lead to the development of few or even no symptoms of the disorder. 

89 

### _Pleiotropy_ 

**Pleiotropy** occurs when one gene influences multiple, apparently unrelated, phenotypic traits. An example is **cystic fibrosis,** the most common genetic Mendelian disorder in Caucasians. The defective cystic fibrosis gene produces faulty chloride transport channels, causing mucus to build up in: 

- **liver and pancreas,** resulting in blocked fat digestion and vitamin deficiency, which cause night blindness, skin disorders, rickets, bleeding. 

- **lungs,** leading to a failure of cilia to flush mucus from lungs, causing respiratory infections. 

- **sweat glands,** resulting in fatty sweat. 

**90** 

## **Cytogenetics** 

**Cytogenetic** is a branch of genetics that studies the structure (morphology and number) and function of chromosomes. It includes: 

- **classic cytogenetics,** which is the analysis of banded chromosomes, colored with different banding methods. 

- **molecular cytogenetics,** which is the analysis performed at the molecular level, such as fluorescent in situ hybridization and comparative genomic hybridization array. 

One in 200 newboms presents with a chromosomic abnormality, and more than 50% of miscarriages present with ( and are probably due to) a chromosomic abnormality. One in 100 individuals is a carrier of a balanced chromosomic anomaly, associated with infertility and high frequency of miscarriages. The vast majority oftumors present with multiple chromosomic abnormalities. 

### _Karyotype_ 

A **karyotype** can be defined as a picture of the chromosomes of an individuai, describing the **number** of **chromosomes** and their **morphology** (as they appear under a light microscope). This picture is taken when chromosomes are more visible, namely during mitosis metaphase. Every plant or animai species is characterized by its own set of chromosomes, which remains stable in number and morphology, generation after generation (thanks to mitosis). 

The normai human karyotype is characterized by **46 chromosomes,** organized in 23 pairs: **22 pairs** are pairs of **autosomes** ( chromosomes from 1 to 22), while the remaining pair is a **pair** of **heterosomes** ( chromosomes X and Y). 

### _Chromosome classifìcation_ 

Three key features can be used to describe and classify chromosomes: 

- **size.** 

- **centromere position:** humans do not possess telocentric chromosomes, while mice only have telocentric chromosomes. 

- **banding pattern:** chromosomes in metaphase can be identified using staining techniques (the so called "banding techniques"). Different banding techniques are based essentially on the type of dye used to color chromosomes and on the treatment used to prepare chromosomes. 

### _Banding pattern and technique_ 

The basic procedure involves: 

- cells to be analyzed and cultured for a while, in arder to expand. 

- cells to be stopped in metaphase, where the condensation of chromosomes is maximum. 

- cells to be spread on a slide, fixed, and then colored with a specific dye or treatment to visualize bands. 

Altematively to blood, other cells that are typically used for karyotyping (and for prenatal diagnosis) are fibroblasts, bone marrow, solid tumors, and immortalized cell lines. 

91 

A **band** is defined as that part of a chromosome which is clearly distinguishable from its adjacent segments by appearing darker or brighter with one or more banding techniques. Band pattems are always reproducible, and specifically identify a chromosome. A chromosome is subdivided into many bands, each of them called with a progressive number (with the lowest number proximal/close to the centromere ). 

### _Staining methods_ 

There are four main methods to stain chromosomes: 

- **G** (Giemsa) **banding,** which is a system of dark and light bands. lt uses **trypsin** and the coloring with Giemsa. The light bands tend to be euchromatic regions, early-replicating, GC rich, and containing many genes. Dark bands, instead, tend to be heterochromatic regions, late-replicating, AT rich, and containing few genes. 

- **Q** (Quinacrine) **banding,** which is a system of fluorescent banding, based on the fluorescent dye Quinacrine, an alkylating agent, with higher affinity for AT regions. Bright bands are AT rich, while darker bands are GC rich. 

- **R** (Giemsa) **banding.** The R stands for **"reverse",** referring to the use of Giemsa preceded by a step of denaturati on ( at high temperature) in a saline solution. This determines the same dark/light banding ofthe classic G-banding, but all bands are inverted with respect to the G bands. 

- **C** (Giemsa) **banding.** The C stands for **"centromeric region",** and refers to the use of Giemsa, preceded by a step of denaturation using a solution of barium hydroxide. This determines a specific dark coloring of heterochromatin constitutive regions, and the centromere is the main one. 

### _Karyotype resolution_ 

Depending on how much condensed the chromosomes are, the karyotype may have a total of 300, 400, 550, up to 850 bands (high-resolution karyotype). 

The schernatic representation of a karyotype showing all the cytogenetic bands is called **karyogram,** while the schernatic representation of a single chrornosorne is called **ideogram.** 

## Molecular cytogenetics 

**Molecular cytogenetics** refers to the cornbination of molecular biology and cytogenetics techniques. Two rnain approaches exist: 

- **fluorescence in situ hybridization.** 

- **comparative genomic hybridization arrays.** 

Both techniques are based on the **nucleic-acid hybridization** phenornenon. During nucleic acid hybridization, the following are needed: 

- a **probe,** which is a sequence ofDNA labelled with a fluorophore. The probe should be prepared as a single strand. 

- a **heterogeneous mixture** of **DNA** to be screened, again prepared as a single strand (by denaturation). 

92 

Probes and DNA are mixed together, so that the probe will recognize its target by base pairing. Being the probe labelled, this ensures the identification ofthe target as well. 

### _Fluorescence in situ hybridization_ 

The **labelled probe,** which was denaturated in arder to obtain a single strand, matches to corresponding - **metaphasic chromosome** denaturated as well, so that strands separate - according to base pairing. After this hybridization, occurring through incubation in an oven for 16 to 72 hours at the proper temperature, everything that is not bound to chromosomes is washed out, meaning that all probes that did not recognize anything on chromosomes will be eliminated. Chromosomes are then analyzed: the probe is stained in yellow, and recognizes a sequence specific for telomeres, thus hybridizing with them. Consequently, the telomere of the chromosome will become yellow. 

Let's consider fluorescence in situ hybridization being performed on a **Duchenne muscular dystrophy** carrier: the red probe evidences the centromeric region of the X chromosomes (thus showing that the carrier is a female ), while the green probe was specifically designed to target **exon 45** of the dystrophin gene ( carried on the X chromosome ). If the person possess two exon 45, two chromosome X will be attached to the green probe, but in case of a carrier, only one chromosome will present exon 45, while the other will have a **deletion** (indicating Duchenne muscular dystrophy). 

### _Comparative genomic hybridization arrays_ 

In each spot of the array, a single gene is represented, and contains dozens of the same probe, a 20-25 nucleotide long specific single-stranded DNA sequence. In this way, instead of analyzing one specific gene, **ali genes** are **tested,** in arder to find out if there is anything wrong in the genome ofthe person. 

The patient's DNA is labelled in green, while a reference DNA is stained in red; the two DNA are mixed, hybridized, and put on the array. In each spot of the array, there will be a signal, either red, green, or yellow: a yellow spot means that the DNA from the patient and the control DNA are recognized by the pro bes attached to that spot, so that sequence of the gene is present in roughly the same quantity in both DNAs. Instead, if the patient possesses three copies of the gene (instead of two ), that spot becomes greener, since the green-stained patient DNA will outnumber the red control one. Instead, if the patient has only one copy of that specific gene, the spot will become red. 

If there is no issue, the whole array will be yellow; otherwise there will be spots that look green if the patient possesses one extra copy ofthat specific gene **(duplication),** or red if they have one less copy of the gene **(deletion).** Sometimes, these variations are not pathogenic. 

### _What is a norma! karyotype?_ 

There can be differences among individuals, both at the nucleotide level and at the chromosome (structural) level. These differences can be called **polymorphism:** a polymorphism is a DNA sequence variation occurring commonly within a population, and usually should not be pathogenic. 

Chromosome polymorphisms that have been commonly described are referred to as structural variants. These are: 

- generally stable. 

93 

- present in all cells in a person. 

- inherited from one generation to the next in a Mendelian fashion. 

The most common chromosome polymorphisms are: 

- the highly variable **centromeric regions** of chromosomes 1, 9 and 16. Tue extended version of chromosome 9 is present in 8% of the generai population. 

- the satellites of **acrocentric chromosomes.** 

- the distai end of the Y chromosomes. 

A **fragile site** is an inheritable locus on chromosomes that seems to form a gap or a constriction along the chromosomes. These sites are called fragile because they tend to break spontaneously, when the celi is exposed to replication stress, or during the preparation ofkaryotypes. More than 120 fragile sites have been identified in the human genome. Many are very common in the generai population. 

**Rare fragile sites** are found in less than 5% of the population, and are often composed of two or three nucleotide repeats. These can be associated with diseases. Clinically, the most important rare fragile site is **FRAXA,** which is associated with the fragile X syndrome, the most common cause of hereditary mental retardation. 

94 

### **Chromosome abnormalities** 

**Chromosome abnormalities** referto changes in the structure and/or number of chromosomes. They can be classified as **constitutive** (if present at birth) or **acquired/somatic** (if acquired after birth, for example during cancer transformation). Abnormalities in chromosomes can be found through classica! karyotyping, if their length is above 5-10 Mb (resolution limit of this technique). These may have a phenotypic effect, depending on whether they are balanced or not. 

In many cases, **balanced chromosome abnormalities** are not associated with a specific phenotype ( disease ). However, an **imbalanced abnormality** is usually associated with a phenotype: the more extended the abnormality, the higher the number of genes involved, the more severe the phenotype. 

## Chromosome abnormalities can be: 

- **numeric,** which include **polyploidy** (triploidy or tetraploidy) and **aneuploidy** (trisomy, tetrasomy, monosomy). 

- **structural,** which include deletions, duplications, ring chromosomes, translocations, inversions, insertions. 

### _Polyploidy_ 

**Polyploidy** refers to the presence of more than two paired (homologous) sets of chromosomes, while **aneuploidy** is a number anomaly referring to single chromosomes. 

**Triploid human genome** (69 chromosomes) is the most common anomaly found in specimens from spontaneous miscarriages, and indeed it accounts for 15% of all miscarriages. Triploidy may arise from: 

- **double fertilization** (66%). 

- **fertilization** of a **2n egg,** with meiosis occurring abnormally in the female (10%). 

- **fertilization** of an egg by a **2n sperm,** with meiosis occurring abnormally in the male (24%). 

About 6% of spontaneous miscarriages carry **tetraploid human genome** (92 chromosomes ), which is also frequent in solid tumors. The most common cause of tetraploidy is represented by **endomitosis,** where the DNA duplication is not followed by cell division, and this can happen during early stage of development. Very rarely, it can depend on a double fertilization of a 2n egg (meaning that there are two concomitant aberrant events, unlikely, but still possible ). 

Aneuploidy 

**Aneuploidy** includes: 

- **trisomy,** with the presence of one additional chromosome compared to the wild-type situation. 

- **tetrasomy,** with the presence of two additional chromosomes compared to the wild-type situati on. 

- **monosomy,** with the absence of one chromosome compared to the wild-type situation. 

The frequency of newboms with aneuploidy is 0.36%, but this percentage greatly increases with the age of the mother. 

95 

Aneuploidies are generated by **non-disjunctions** during meiosis I: both chromosomes of the homologous pair segregate together in the daughter cells. However, disjunction may also occur during meiosis II, when both sister chromatids segregate together in one daughter cell. Moreover, non-disjunctions can happen during mitosis, and this occurs during early stages of development: in this case, the fetus will be a **mosaic,** composed of cells with different sets of chromosomes; **mosaicisms** due to mutations affect genes involved in skin/eye pigmentation. 

Aneuploidies can affect both autosomes and sexual chromosomes. 

### _Aneuploidies in autosomes_ 

**Autosome monosomy** involves the absence of one autosome compared to the wild-type situation, and is always **lethal** and incompatible with life. Monosomy for an autosome can be found only in spontaneous m1scamages. 

**Trisomy** in autosomes is characterized by the presence of one additional autosome compared to the wildtype situation, and can be lethal. However, different examples are known of newboms having trisomy. In many cases, they die after few days, weeks or months. 

Trisomy of chromosome **13** ( 4 7, XX+ 13) is known as **Patau syndrome.** 

Trisomy of chromosome **18** (47, XX+18) is known as **Edwards syndrome.** 

Trisomy of chromosome **21** (47, XX+21) is known as **Down syndrome,** which affects one in 850 live births, but is largely increased with mother's age. 95% of Down syndrome patients have 47 chromosomes, with an extra copy of chromosome 21. About 2% of these 95% of patients are mosaic for two cell populations, one with normai karyotype and one with trisomy 21. About 4% ofpatients, instead, have 46 chromosomes, one of which is a **Robertsonian translocation,** between chromosome 21q and the long arm of one ofthe other acrocentric chromosomes (usually chromosome 14 or 22). 

### _Aneuploidies in sex chromosomes_ 

Differently from autosome aneuploidies, numeric aberrations of sex chromosomes are all **well-tolerated,** compatible with a normai or quasi-normal life, andare **not rare** (one in 400 live births in males, 1 in 600 live births in females ). Phenotypes are not severe iflacking one sex chromosome ( except for reproductive problems ), but severe in case of **tetrasomies or pentasomies.** Main examples of aneuploidies in sex chromosomes include **Turner syndrome** and **Klinefelter Syndrome.** 

**Turner syndrome** affects females and its most recurrent genotype 1s 45,X, with one m1ssmg chromosome. Other genotypes can be 46,XXq or 46,XXp, with large deletions of the q and p arms of chromosome X. In Tumer's syndrome, the X chromosome contains many loci that do not undergo complete **X inactivation,** several of which appear to be necessary for ovarian maintenance and female fertility. The genetic bases for the other features of the syndrome have not been defined, but presumably reflect **haploinsufficiency** for one or more X-linked genes that do not normally undergo inactivation in the female. In haploinsufficiency, havingjust one normal allele (wild-type) is insufficient to produce the wild-type phenotype; this means that even though the other allele (variant allele) may still produce its standard amount of product, the total product is not enough to achieve the normal phenotype. 

**In Klinefelter** syndrome, which affects males, the most recurrent genotype is 47,XXY (one additional chromosome ), and mosaicism is frequent. Other possible genotypes include 48,XXXY and 48,XXXY. 

96 

Other sexual chromosome aneuploidies include: 

- **female XXX,** with fertile female phenotype, normai appearing, and cognitive defects in 25% of cases. 

- **female XXXX,** rare, with female phenotype and mental retardation. 

- **female XXXXX,** very rare, with female phenotype, severe mental retardation, and dysmorphologies. 

- **male XYY,** with fertile male phenotype, normai appearing, usually taller than average. 

- **only Y karyotype,** lethal. 

### _Structural chromosome abnormalities_ 

- **deletions,** genetic aberrations in which a part of chromosome is m1ssmg; any number of nucleotide can be deleted. **Terminal deletion** involves a deletion at the end of a chromosome owing to a break in the chromosome. **Interstitial deletion** is characterized by a deletion within a chromosome, originating from two breaks in the chromosome. **Microdeletion** is a relatively small deletion, barely detectable with karyotyping. 

- **duplications,** genetic aberrations in which a part of a chromosome is present twice, so there is the presence of extra genetic materiai. Any number of nucleotides can be duplicated. **Direct duplication** occurs when the duplicated region maintains the same direction as the originai, while **inverted duplication** takes piace if the duplicated region is oriented in the opposi te direction as the originai. 

- **ring chromosomes,** which are chromosomes whose arms have fused together to form a ring. This occurs very rarely, and, in order to form a ring, both ends of a single chromosome break. The resulting double-deleted chromosome has **sticky ends,** which tend to collapse together to form the ring. 

- **translocations,** chromosome abnormalities caused by rearrangements of parts between nonhomologous chromosomes. **Balanced translocations** are characterized by an even exchange of materiai with no genetic information (not extra nor missing), and ideally full functionality. **Unbalanced translocations,** instead, are characterized by unequal exchange of chromosome materiai, thus resulting in extra or missing genes. Unbalanced translocations can either be: 

   - a. **reciprocai translocations:** in this type of translocation, genetic materiai is exchanged between two non-homologous chromosomes. Reciprocai translocations without loss of genetic materiai are usually harmless. However, carriers of balanced reciprocai translocations have increased risk of giving rise to gametes with unbalanced chromosome translocations, leading to miscarriages and children with abnormalities. 

   - b. **Robertsonian translocations,** which involve acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22). Acrocentric chromosomes are chromosomes whose centromere is subterminal, meaning it is closer to the end rather than the center. The reciprocai exchange of parts, in this case, determines the production of one large metacentric chromosome and one extremely small chromosome, which may be lost from the organism with little eff ect, since it usually contains few genes. Carriers ofRobertsonian translocations can be completely normal and fertile, but have increased risk of giving rise to gametes with unbalanced chromosome translocations. Among acrocentric chromosomes, there is also chromosome 21, responsible for Down 

97 

      - syndrome. The most frequent Robertsonian translocations causmg Down syndrome is between chromosome 21 and 14. 

- **inversions,** which occur when a single chromosome undergoes breakage and rearrangement within itself, with a resulting inversion of the involved region. Inversions can either be **paracentric** (when they do not involve the centromere) or **pericentric** (when they involve the centromere ). Carriers of inversions can be completely normai and fertile, but have increased risk of giving rise to gametes with unbalanced chromosome translocations. 

   - Paracentric inversions can cause lots of problems during meiosis: indeed, crossing over, in order to take piace, needs particular chromosomal structures to form, and in case of paracentric inversions, the obtained recombinant chromosomes are completely abnormal. 

   - In pericentric inversion, the obtained recombinant chromosomes are either normai or severely affected. 

- **insertions,** which consist in the insertion of a large sequence into a chromosome, derived from a donor chromosome (which results deleted). 

### _How do these aberrant chromosomes generate?_ 

Structural abnormalities can be determined by chromosomal breakage or unequal crossmg over ( crossover between homologous sequences that are not paired precisely). In details: 

- a single break in one chromosome will produce a terminal deletion. 

- two breaks in one chromosome can result in an interstitial deletion, a ring chromosome, or an mvers1on. 

- two breaks in two different chromosomes can produce structural changes, including reciprocai and Robertsonian translocations. 

- unequal crossing-over can result in duplications or deletions. 

98 

### **Genetic diversity** 

The sequence of nuclear DNA is approximately 99 .5% identica! between two unrelated humans. Many DNA sequence differences have little or no effect on outward appearance, whereas other differences are responsible for causing diseases. 

**Genetic variations** are differences in the DNA sequence between individuals of the same species. A **locus** is a segment of DNA that occupies a particular position or location on a chromosome. **Wild-type** or **common allele** refers to the prevailing allele of a gene, usually present in more than half of the individuals in a population. A **variant or mutant allele** is the other version of the gene, which differs from the wild-type allele because of the presence of a **mutation.** If there are two or more relatively common alleles (with more than 1 % of frequency) at a locus in a population, that locus is said to exhibit **polymorphism.** 

A mutation is a permanent change of the nucleotide sequence of the genome of an organism. Mutations result from: 

- **damage** to the **nucleic acid** that is not repaired. 

- **errors** during **replication.** 

- the **insertion or deletion** of segments of DNA by mobile genetic elements. 

Polymorphism is a DNA sequence variation occurring commonly within a population, and usually should not be pathogenic. 

### _Classifìcation of mutations_ 

Mutations can be classified by their effect on **size** or extent, **function** of the DNA sequence that is hit by the mutation, and **outcome** on fitness. 

**Chromosome mutations** change the number of chromosomes in a cell, like aneuploidy. Subchromosomal mutations change only a portion of a chromosome, like translocation, deletion, ringchromosome, inversion. Gene mutations are alterations ofthe sequence ofDNA, involving **substitution, deletion or insertion** at the nucleotide level. Among gene mutations, the most commons are **point mutations,** which affect one single nucleotide, and can be: 

- **base substitution.** 

- **base insertions.** 

- **base deletions.** 

When insertion and deletion occur together, they are called **indels.** 

When going from a purine to a purine or from a pyrimidine to a pyrimidine a **transition** occurs, while when going from a purine to a pyrimidine or vice versa, a **transversion** occurs. The last base of a triplet can wobble, but this mutation is usually silent, leading to the production of the same amino acid as the "normal" sequence. 

Mutations can hit the protein **coding regions** (exons), **splice sites** (which signal which sequences must be removed from the pre-mRNA to produce mature mRNA), or **regulatory regions.** 

99 

### _Point mutations_ 

Possible point mutations (insertion, deletion, substitution) have different potential consequences on the translated protein. Point mutations can be: 

- **frameshift mutations,** which happens when there is an insertion or deletion, and the number of nucleotides that are inserted or deleted is not three or multiples of three. This means that the reading frame gets completely shifted, so also the following codons will be altered. 

- **silent mutations,** which do not have an effect on the protein that the gene codes. 

- **missense mutations,** which lead to the coding of a different amino acid. 

- **nonsense mutations,** when the mutated codon is a stop codon, so translation stops. 

- **elongation mutations,** with the production of a protein that is longer than the original one. 

Consequences on protein level are not easy to predict. Not all missense mutations lead to a change in the function of the protein, but some of them can cause the protein to fail to work properly, to be unstable and degraded, or to localize correctly into the cell. In order to predict the possible pathogenicity of missense mutation, it is possible to look at: 

- the **type** of amino acid change. Imagine substituting a very small non polar amino acid like alanine with a large polar basic amino acid like arginine: in this case, the mutation is likely to be pathogenic. 

- the **conservation among species.** By aligning the sequence of the same protein of different species, it is possible to see if the amino acid hit by the mutation is conserved or not among species. In case of conservation, it means that the amino acid is crucial in the function of the protein, whereas if it changes greatly, then is not that important. 

- the **position** in the protein. A mutati on on an amino acid of the active site of an enzyme, it is very likely to affect the protein function, much more as it occurs if the mutati on is situated on a flexible loop. 

It is possible to evaluate the possible consequences of a missense mutation by functional studies in animal or cell models, or using specimen directly from patients. 

Hemoglobin is composed of four subunits, two a ( encoded by HBA) and two _B_ ( encoded by HBB). Tue **Glu6Val mutation** in HBB decreases the solubility of deoxygenated hemoglobin and causes it to form a gelatinous network of stiff fibrous polymers that distort the red blood cell, giving it a sickle shape. 

### _Effects of nonsense mutations_ 

Consequences of nonsense mutations at the protein level are much more easily predictable: the introduction of a **protein termination codon** may determine the production of a **truncated protein** or the activation of the **nonsense-mediated mRNA decay** pathway. An example is **(3-thalassemia,** caused by a deficient synthesis of B-globin; nonsense mutations localized within B-globin gene are responsible for the phenotype. 

100 

### _Effects of elongation mutations_ 

**Hemoglobin constant spring** is a variant of hemoglobin in which a mutation in the a-globin gene produces an a-globin chain that is abnormally long. The quantity of hemoglobin in the cells is low because the messenger RNA is unstable, and some is degraded prior to protein synthesis. 

### _Effects of frameshift mutations_ 

In frameshift mutations, from the point of insertion or deletion onwards, the sequence of codons will **not** be **readable** anymore. Point mutation can frequently lead to an exonic nucleotide insertion/deletion, with the introduction of a **premature termination codon,** the production of a truncated protein, and the activation of the nonsense-mediated mRNA decay pathway, as already described for nonsense mutations. 

**Consensus sequences** occur in the junction regions, where the intron ends and the exon starts: the last two nucleotides in introns are always A and G, while the first nucleotide of an exon is most frequently G. The first two nucleotides of an intronare always G and U, while the terminal nucleotide of an exon is often G. Ifthese positions are hit by mutations, most likely the splicing machinery will not recognize the end ofthe intron and the beginning ofthe exon anymore, so a **functional splicing site** could be **destroyed** and a novel one would be produced (activation of a **CRYPTIC splice site),** leading to the production of **aberrant mRNA molecules,** as a consequence of an abnormal mRNA maturation process.
