Mutation affecting the consensus sequence may also lead to the exclusion ofthe exon **(exon skipping):** as a consequence, the encoded protein will miss all amino acids coded by the skipped exon, or there will be a frameshift (if the number ofbases contained in the skipped exon is nota multiple ofthree). 

### _Mutations in regulatory sequences_ 

Mutations affect regulatory sequences like the TATA box, transcription factor binding sites, 5' and 3' UTRs, and the enhancer. Mutation at the level of the TATA box, transcription-factor binding sites and enhancer may determine an **abnormal** level of **expression** of the gene (usually lower levels), while mutati on in 5' and 3' untranslated regions may change mRNA stability or translation efficiency, thereby reducing the amount of protein product that is made. 

### _Mutations and fitness_ 

- **deleterious mutations** decrease the fitness of the organism. 

- **beneficiai mutations** increase the fitness of the organism and have evolutionary implications. 

- **neutral mutations** possess neither a deleterious nor beneficiai effect and usually lead to polymorphisms. 

### _Polymorphisms_ 

Classes **ofpolymorphisms** include: 

- **chromosomal** (structural variants) **polymorphism,** like the length ofY chromosome. 

- **single nucleotide polymorphism.** 

- **microsatellites.** 

101 

The most common polymorphisms in the human genome ( about 90% of all human genetic variation) are **single nucleotide polymorphisms,** which include base substitutions (vast majority) and small insertions and deletions (indel). 

Normally, a locus characterized by single nucleotide polymorphism has only two alleles (one wild-type and one polymorphic ), corresponding to the two different bases occupying that particular location on the genome. The distribution of single nucleotide polymorphisms is uneven around the genome: many more single nucleotide polymorphisms are found in noncoding part ofthe genome, in introns, and in sequences that are at some distance from known genes. Half of single nucleotide polymorphisms localized in exons do not alter the predicted amino acid sequence **(synonymous** single nucleotide polymorphisms, basically silent mutations), but others can have functional consequences **(non-synonymous** single nucleotide polymorphism). The fact that single nucleotide polymorphisms are common does not mean that they are without effect on health or longevity. Most of the time, single nucleotide polymorphisms are not directly causing a serious disease, and to be this frequent, they have instead a **predisposition effect,** making affected individuals more susceptible to certain pathologies. Other effects on health involve their impact in drug metabolism. 

**Microsatellites** are another class of common polymorphism, and are considered indels, being due to the inserti on of a short DNA sequence, one or more times in tandem, generating up to dozens of repeats ( also called short tandem repeats). They are normally multiallelic due to variable numbers ofrepeats inserted. 

Alleles in the population are often numerous, leading to many possible combinations of alleles (genotypes). These polymorphisms are exploited for **fingerprinting** and DNA testing. 

102 

## **Protein folding** 

After translation, proteins need to be folded. The polypeptide in its linear form is not functional and needs to be properly folded in order to get its **tertiary structure,** which also guarantee its function. Translation completes the flow of genetic information: translated polypeptide chains are not functional yet, and must be folded into distinct 3D conformations. In many cases, multiple chains assemble into a **complex** and many proteins also undergo **post-translational modifications.** 

The strongest bonds In the tertiary structure of proteins are covalent bonds between cysteines, called **disulfide bonds.** Other strong interactions include **hydrogen bonds, Van der Walls forces,** and **electrostatic interactions.** 

When a protein folds, it buries most of its hydrophilic residues in an interi or core, since it is present in a hydrophilic environment ( cytoplasm). Hydrophilic residues, instead, are exposed to the cytosol and intramolecular interactions occur. The sum of all these energetically favorable arrangements determines the final folding. Through evolution, the amino acid sequence of each protein has been selected not only for the conformation that it adopts, but also for an ability to fold rapidly. 

### _Howard Stein and ribonucleases_ 

Howard **Stein** was awarded the Nobel Prize for Chemistry in 1972 for his work on ribonucleases to study the link between protein amino acid sequence and its biologica! conformation. 

He used a **reconstitution approach** (reconstitute a biologica! process outside of a cell, in a test tube), and in particular, he studied **pancreatic ribonucleases.** Stein purified the protein, and applied heating and chemical-reducing agents that caused its **denaturation,** breaking its interactions (disulfide bonds) and turning it into a linear polypeptide. After denaturation, the ribonuclease was not able to digest RNA anymore, proving that the tertiary structure is essential for function. Stein then removed the denaturing agents in a physiological buffer and in presence of oxygen, and the protein slowly regained its conformation (most of the time), thus being able to perform its function again. This showed that in the majority of cases, the protein was able to fold again by itself. 

This proved that all the information to properly fold a polypeptide chain are contained in its **primary amino acid sequence.** The correctly folded state **(native conformation)** is the most **energetically favorable.** Understanding the mechanism of protein folding remains an active area of research: this process is extremely complex, and we are still unable to deduce the three dimensiona! structure of a protein directly from its amino acidic sequence. Tue spontaneous folding _in vitro_ is much slower than that in vivo, which is assisted by enzymes. 

X-ray crystallography is the most effective way to determine how proteins are folded. 

### _The role of chaperones_ 

Folding is important in order to achieve the functionality of the protein, and aberrantly folded proteins lead to **aggregation,** where misfolded proteins cluster together, leading to **pathological consequences.** In many cases, it may take a long time to proteins to find the correct folding, so **chaperon proteins** are used to aid and facilitate the folding of other proteins, without conveying additional information, but by 

103 

simply binding and stabilizing the unfolded, or partially folded polypeptides that are intermediates along the pathway leading to the final correctly folded state. 

There can be a variety of paths to achieve the final folded structure, and some intermediates may tend to aggregate before final conformation is reached. Molecular chaperones are **ubiquitously** expressed and **evolutionary conserved,** and bind non-covalently to unfolded proteins. In many cases, chaperon function needs the **expenditure of ATP.** 

Far example, chaperons bind to the N-terminus of a protein as soon as it comes out from the ribosome, preventing it from aggregating or folding in an improper conformation (indeed, not always the first available interaction will be the right one ). 

Chaperones assist the correct folding of neosynthesized proteins, orchestrate the assembly and disassembly of multi-protein complexes, prevent protein aggregation, guide translocating proteins across organellar membranes through action at both the cis and trans sides. Finally, they stabilize damaged proteins ( undergoing chemical or physical stress) to help them renature or send them to degradati on. 

Chaperone levels and activity are increased in **stressful conditions,** to help the cell keeping its protein properly folded and functional (homeostasis ). Chaperon proteins were first discovered as protein produced in large amounts in response to a heat-shock. 

Two ofthe most known classes of chaperones include **HSP60** (Heat Schock Protein 60, or chaperonin), and **HSP70** (Heat Schock Protein 70). 

### _HSP70 chaperones_ 

**HSP70 chaperones** assist the folding of polypeptide chains by allowing the protein to fold only when it is out of the ribosome. In general, by binding co-translationally (during translation), the HSP70 helps each module ofthe growing polypeptide chain to assume the right conformation; indeed, not always the first available interaction will be the right one, so by binding to portions ofthe growing polypeptide, the chaperones makes sure that the folding occurs in the proper way, preventing potentially arising interactions that are not supposed to be the final ones. 

During translation, many ribosomes sequentially translate the same mRNA, forming a polyribosomes: thus, their emerging polypeptides are quite close to each other, and **aggregation** may become a problem. 

HSP70 chaperones transiently bind to short hydrophobic segments of the polypeptide chain itself, preventing the generati on of unwanted interactions. They also stabilize the unfolded polypeptide chain during transfer to mitochondria, which indeed happens in the unfolded state, so that the protein can be squeezed thanks to a translocator, and this would not be possible with the large globular tertiary structure. Hence, HSP70 chaperones maintain the polypeptide unfolded so that it can be transported in a linear way to its destination. 

Chaperones require **ATP energy** in each cycle of binding and unbinding to the substrate. 

### _HSP60 chaperonin_ 

**HSP60 chaperonin** is responsible for re-folding a protein when it assumed the wrong conformation. HSP60 chaperonin is formed offourteen subunits organized in two rings and a lid, so they forma **cavity,** 

104 

a chamber, in an oligomeric complex. Also these chaperones require ATP to function, in order to fuel binding and unbinding reactions of the process. 

HSP60 chaperonin helps releasing the wrong interactions in a misfolded protein, by detaching wrong interactions with binding with the protein itself, until it can be refolded in the proper way. HSP60 chaperonin also prevents aggregation of partially folded intennediates, which can be very dose to each other; they isolate each peptide thanks to their inner channel. In this way, the isolated protein can get to its final confonnation without being disturbed by interactions with other proteins. Once folded correctly, the polypeptide can then be released from the chamber. 

Sometimes the two kinds of chaperones act sequentially: initially, the HSP70 chaperone class binds cotranslationally to the emerging peptide, and a partially folded intennediate gets transferred to the chamber of HSP60 chaperonin, where it is located and completely folded to its final structure. Once this has occurred, the protein is released in the cytoplasm. 

The little amounts of proteins that cannot be degraded and are misfolded, instead, lead to **protein aggregates.** 

### _The proteasome_ 

The **proteasome** disposes of misfolded proteins that cannot be properly re-folded, by removing all the failures of the translation process, which are qui te frequent. 

The proteasome uses ATP to digest proteins, and the cell recycles the amino acids, which go back to the cytosol. It has the shape of a **cylinder,** fonned by **proteases** whose active site faces the cylinder in order to confine the degradation process to a specific place. Proteins that must be sent to the proteasome are tagged by **ubiquitin,** which gets attached to the protein and signals that it must be degraded. The stopper domain of the proteasome recognizes the ubiquitinated protein, which is unfolded, and then feeds into proteases. 

### _Protein misfolding and diseases_ 

Many disease-causing genetic mutations alter proper protein folding; decreased folding capacity and chaperone activity may cause **aggregation** and cell damage **(neurodegenerative diseases),** while increased folding capacity is used by cancer cells to better survive and grow. 

- **Alzheimer's disease** can be sporadic ( occurring without any family history) or familial (inherited); regardless of the type, the risk of getting any of these diseases dramatically increases with age. Both genetic and environmental risk factors are present; among the environmental factors, there is an increasing amount of oxidative damage. 

   - Fora particular type of protein to aggregate to survive, grow and damage an organism, it must be highly resistant to proteolysis. Many of the protein aggregates that cause problems fonn fibrils built from a series of polypeptide chains that are layered one over the other as a continuous stack of ~ sheets. This resistance presumably explains why this structure is observed in so many of the neurologica! disorders cause by protein aggregates, where it produces abnonnally staining deposits known as **amyloid.** 

105 

- **Prion diseases** are a group of rare diseases caused by protein aggregation in the brain, affecting both humans and animals. They are characterized by **progressive neurodegeneration,** ultimately leading to death. Symptoms include psychiatric and behavioral problems, insomnia, loss of movement coordination, memory loss, dementia. 

   - In the late 1950s, an epidemie of neurodegenerative disease called **kuru** suggested that proteins could be infectious. Kuru was discovered among populations of the Fore tribe of the eastem highland of Papua New Guinea, and the disease was associated with their cannibalistic funeral practices. Researchers showed that kuru could be infective in chimpanzees after intracerebral inoculation with brain suspension from kuru patients. **Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease** or Spongiform encephalopathy could be caused by the ingestion ofbeef containing toxic protein particles. Prions are not an exclusive phenomenon of mammals, but also occur naturally in unicellular organisms, such as yeasts. When an abnormal prion protein forms, when interacting with a normai protein it forces it to become diseased, thus spreading in the system. It is not properly replicating, but it is forcing the proteins that are already present to become toxic. These toxic proteins form aggregates that lead to neurodegeneration and visible symptoms in patients. 

### _Post-translational modifications_ 

**Post-translational modifications** are covalent modifications normally occurring on a single amino acid on the chain with the addition of a chemical group and some more complex molecules. Some posttranslational modifications are reversible, while other are not, and each of them requires specific enzymes so that groups and complex molecules can be added to the specific amino acid. Examples of some posttranslational modifications include **phosphorylation/dephosphorylation, methylation, acetylation, ADP-ribosylation, uridylation.** 

These modifications can either regulate the protein biologica! activity, locati on, and interaction with other molecules. They may occur in a specific cellular location or at specific moments. They occur at distinct amino acid sites. 

- **Proteolysis** is an important step in the maturation of many proteins: an example is the removal of the initiator methionine from the amino terminus of many polypeptides; moreover, active enzymes or hormones form via cleavage of larger precursors. Insulin, for instance, is translated as **pre-pro-insulin,** with a signal peptide at the N-terminus, a B sequence, a connecting peptide, and an A sequence. There is a first proteolytic cleavage that tums pre-pro-insulin to pre-insulin by removing the signal peptide; a second proteolytic cleavage occurs in two places and gives rise to the major form of insulin ( active ), which would not be possible without a proteolytic cleavage. 

- during **glycosylation,** oligosaccharidic chains (glycan) are added to proteins, mostly in the ER (N-) and Golgi apparatus (N- and 0-). Two most common types of glycosylation take piace on **asparagine** (N-glycosylation) or on **serine** and **threonine** (O-glycosylation). Glycosylation aids correct folding of modified proteins, confers solubility, is used as recognition site in cell-cell interactions, and are used as surface signals. 

- **Phosphorylation** occurs at serine, threonine, or tyrosine residues. Phosphorylation is widely used as a mechanism to switch on or off proteins. 

106 

## **Protein sorting** 

After synthesis, a protein can either remain in the cytosol Gust the liquid, while the cytoplasm also contains organelles) or it can be **sorted** to a **cellular compartment** to perform its function. The sorting itself can happen both during ( co-translationally) and after synthesis. When a protein is synthetized in free ribosomes (the ones floating in the cytoplasm), it can be sorted to the nucleus, mitochondria, cytoplasm, or peroxisomes. Instead, protein synthesis occurring in ER-bound ribosomes are then transferred to the Golgi apparatus, the plasma membrane, lysosomes, endosomes, the endoplasmic reticulum, or they can be excreted. 

A universal requirement among all destinations Is a **localization signal** and a **sorting receptor/translocator** ( a protein itself) guiding the protein to the correct destinati on. lt is possible to distinguish two main groups of localization signals: 

- a **signal sequence,** which is a continuous stretch of amino acids. 

- a **signal patch,** which is composed of separate amino acid motifs brought together when the protein folds. 

Some signal sequences gets cleaved off after reaching the correct localization, while others are maintained. 

What directs a protein to its destination are the **physical properties** (acidity, basicity, and so on) of signal sequences and their location within the protein, more important than the exact amino acid sequence. 

A way in which scientists understood how these signal sequences work was by removing them and placing them in another protein. For example, ifa sequence signaling an ER destination was placed to a protein that normally stayed in the cytosol, that protein would be delivered to the ER. This proved that these sequences are both **necessary** and **sufficient** for the delivery of proteins to their destinati on. 

Proteins are normally soluble and need to be brought to membranes which are instead impermeable to hydrophilic large molecules, so proteins cannot diffuse freely. Thus, in order to bring proteins from the cytosol to the inside part of an organelle, the cell has developed multiple mechanisms: 

- **gated transport,** for example to the nucleus: there are pores in the nuclear membrane through which the protein can flow, but they work as **selectivity filters,** allowing the passage only of certain molecules. 

- **transport across membrane,** for proteins destined to mitochondria, chloroplasts, or peroxisomes, where there are no membrane pores. 

- **transport through vesicles.** 

## Gated transport 

**Gated transport** occurs through **nuclear pores,** and small molecules can be moved freely by diffusion, while macromolecules are actively transported. Proteins can be transported through these pores in their fully folded conformation, since the pores are big enough to allow the passage ofthe whole 3D structure. The localization signal is not removed after sorting (after the protein has reached the nucleus), and this avoids the need to re-sort these proteins from scratch at each cell division, when the nuclear envelop disassemble and then reforms. 

107 

The **nuclear envelope** is formed by two membranes, with the **inner nuclear membrane** containing some proteins that bind the chromosomes, and other that anchor the nuclear lamina. The **nuclear lamina** is a meshwork of filaments that lines the inner face of the membrane and provides structural support. The **outer nuclear membrane** is in continuity with the ER. 

The nuclear envelope in all eukaryotic cells is punctuated with nuclear pores, which are large, elaborate structures composed of a complex of about 30 different proteins (like **nucleoporins),** characterized by **unstructured domains,** meaning that they do not possess a folded module and are flexible. These unstructured tails are rich in short repeats of amino acids **(FG repeats** ), and these regions internet with each other, forming a network that works as a selectivity filter. In this way, the unstructured tails regulate both free and active transport. 

Another characteristic of the transport through nuclear pores is that it can be **bidirectional,** with both import and export of proteins in and out of the nucleus. Proteins needed for replication and transcription and histones themselves must be imported, while mRNAs and structures that are synthetized in the nucleus but perform their function in the cytoplasm are exported. 

This transport can be either **passive or active.** Passive transport consist of free diffusion dependent on size-selection: proteins below 40 kDa and with a diameter less than 9 nm can freely diffuse, while molecules bigger than 40 kDa undergo active and protein-mediated transport. Many players are involved in active nuclear transport: 

- proteins ofthe **nuclear pore complex.** 

- **nuclear localization signals** (which mediate the import), or **nuclear export signals** (which instead mediate protein export). 

- **nuclear receptors,** which are actually transporting the protein across nuclear pores, and include **importins** and **exportins.** 

- **monomeric Ran GTPase.** 

### _Nuclear localization signals_ 

**Nuclear localization signals** for import and export can be both located anywhere along the protein (and frequently in more than one locations) and the signal is not removed after transport. 

### _Nuclear receptors_ 

**Nuclear receptors** bind both to localization signal proteins and nuclear pore complex proteins, and possess a pocket where they bind the nuclear import or nuclear export sequence. They can also bind the proteins of the nuclear pore complex, and grab on **FG repeats,** advancing towards the inside or outside of the nucleus by moving on these tracts in a competitive fashion. 

### _Nuclear localization signals_ 

**Nuclear localization signals** are recognized by cytosolic proteins called nuclear import receptors. These receptors help direct a newly synthetized protein to a nuclear pore by interacting with the tentacle-like fibrils that extend from the rim of the pore. The empty receptor then returns to the cytosol via the nuclear pore for reuse. 

108 

### _Ran GTPase_ 

**Ran GTPases** are enzymes that possess two states, which normally have different conformations that dictate their behavior: one is bound to **GDP,** the other is bound to **GTP.** What regulate their cycle from the GDP- to the GTP-bound state are two classes of other enzymes which help this transition, known as **guanine exchange factors (GEFs) or Ran GTPase activating proteins (GAPs).** Guanine exchange factors help exchange the used GDP molecule for a new GTP molecule: indeed, they help Ran release GDP and get a GTP in its place. 

GAP proteins are instead needed for the Ran GTPase itself to activate the hydrolysis of GTP; GAPs promote the passage from the GTP- to the GDP-bound state, while GEFs promote the apposite change, from GDP to GTP. This is what fuels the active transport across nuclear pores, and because of the localization of GAP and GEF, a **gradient of Ran proteins** in the two states is generated. Ran-GAP is almost exclusively present in the cytosol, meaning that as soon as Ran bound to GTP comes out from the nucleus, it would immediately be activated by GTP hydrolysis. Instead, ran-GEF (promoting the GDP to GTP change) is highly enriched in the nucleus (since it binds to chromatin), so as soon as Ran-GDP comes to the nucleus, GDP for GTP is exchanged, leading to the formation ofRan-GTP. This means that Ran would be bound to GTP way more efficiently, and there would be a much higher concentration of Ran-GTP in the nucleus, while in the cytosol there would be abundance of Ran-GDP: this creates a gradient of these two conformations, which actually generates the propelling farce for the direction of the transport. 

A cargo protein with a nuclear localization signal needs to be transported to the nucleus. The import receptor **(importin)** is thus bound to the localization signal on the carbo protein, and starts travelling across the nuclear pare, making its way across the meshwork. Then, because of the abundance of GAF, Ran-GTP would be highly concentrated in the nucleus and would immediately bind to its pocket in the same importing receptor. This binding of Ran-GTP causes the release of the cargo, most of the time induced by conformational changes. Then, the importin, still bound to Ran-GTP is transported back to the cytosol following the Ran-GTP gradient, so in this way importin can be recycled. As soon as the importin binds to the Ran-GTP reaches the cytosol, the GAP residing there helps hydrolyze GTP, which becomes GDP. Once it is GDP-bound, Ran has no more affinity to importin, since its conformation has changed: thus, both Ran-GDP and free importin are released in the cytosol, and importin is now able to bind another cargo protein. 

During export, the cargo protein possesses an **export signal** recognized by an export receptor. Since in the nucleus there is high concentrati on of Ran-GTP, the receptor binds to Ran-GTP: exportin differs from importin because when it is bound to Ran-GTP it can bind the nuclear export sequence but not the apposite. While importin either binds Ran-GTP or the nuclear localization sequence, exportin does the apposite, binding both of them. The binding of the cargo protein, indeed, facilitates that to Ran-GTP, so the gradient of Ran-GTP pushes exportin and its cargo across nuclear pores. Once it reaches the cytosol, the high concentration of GAPs help hydrolyze GTP, which becomes GDP: this conversion from RanGTP to Ran-GDP determines the release ofboth Ran-GDP and ofthe cargo protein. At this point, exportin can be recycled back to the nucleus. 

109 

### _mRNAs_ 

**mRNAs** are bound to proteins, like poly-A binding proteins or eap-binding proteins, whieh internet with exportins, thus allowing the export of mRNA. These proteins are loaded onto the RNA as transeription and splieing proeeed, and eontain **nuclear export signals** that are reeognized by export reeeptors that guide the RNA out of the nucleus. Upon entry into the eytosol, the proteins eoating the RNA are **stripped off** and rapidly returned to the nucleus. Other RNAs, sueh as tRNAs, are exported by different sets of nuclear export receptors. 

To sum up: 

- nuclear proteins should possess a proper **localization signal.** 

- protein sorting into nucleus is mediated by **nuclear pore complex** and **nuclear receptors.** 

- **Ran GTPase** is needed to provide energy and directionality to the transport. 

### _Transmembrane transport_ 

**Transmembrane translocators** are transmembrane proteins sitting on the membrane of the targeted organelle, allowing the protein that needs to enter that organelle to travel through its membrane. In this case, proteins are kept unfolded to cross membranes, since pores are very tight, and the localization signal is usually removed after sorting. 

**Mitochondria** are tubular-shaped organelles enclosed in two separate membranes: they are the **powerhouse** of the cell and possess their own DNA and ribosomes, since they originate from endosymbiotic bacteria. Mitochondrial genome is small, and codes for some proteins involved in mitochondrial functions, rRNA and tRNA. However, mitochondria are dependent on the main genome for essential proteins, and indeed, most mitochondrial proteins are synthetized in the cytosol and then sorted. Proteins translated inside the mitochondrion do not need to be sorted, since they are already there. However, the majority of mitoehondrial proteins is eneoded in the nucleus and transeribed in the eytosol, so they need to travel through mitochondria and cross one or both membranes to reach their destination. In order to do so, these proteins possess localization signals at their **N-terminus,** normally known as **mitochondrial targeting sequence;** there are some basic residues widespread in a sequence that is quite hydrophobic. Most of the time, these sequences generate a-helices, known as **amphipathic helices,** which have polar or charged residues on one side and hydrophobic ones on the opposite side, so that they ean orient and internet with both hydrophilie and hydrophobie sites, thus being recognized to reeeptors that will transloeate them in mitoehondria. 

**Chaperones** bind the polypeptide emerging from the ribosome and keep it unfolded, thus preventing wrong folding of the first portion of the protein emerging from the cytosol. However, in this case they remain bound to the polypeptide until translation is finished and it reaches the mitochondrial surface, since the protein must remain unfolded so that it can pass through the mitochondrial membrane. Chaperones are also able to bind some reeeptors on the mitoehondrial surface that help direet the translocation ofthe protein into the mitochondria. Finally, chaperones inside mitochondria help bind the polypeptide chain and keep it unfolded until it has completely passed through; then, they aid its folding. 

Transmembrane translocators belong to two families: 

110 

- **translocators** of the **inner mitochondrial membrane** (TIM). 

- **translocators** of the **outer mitochondrial membrane** (TOM). 

Some TOM proteins constitute the translocation channel itself, through which the polypeptide can pass across the membrane; other translocators have domains that stick out from the cytosol, with receptors and pockets that recognize the target sequence on the polypeptide chain and chaperones themselves. 

On the inner mitochondrial membrane, there are **TIM 22** and **TIM23 complex,** which have channels and subunits sticking out and engaging in the pairing of these translocators with those on the outer mitochondrial membrane. Mitochondrial respiration generates an **electrochemical gradient,** which provides energy for the completion of the transport of polypeptides inside mitochondria. If a mitochondrion is faulty, the electrochemical gradient will be disrupted and unable to aid in protein import; in this way, the cell can recognize faulty mitochondria and target them for degradation. Indeed, when mitochondria are faulty, protein get stuck in the transport machinery, initiating a cascade of events (including **phosphorylation** and **ubiquitination)** that trigger the proteasome to start degradation of faulty mitochondria. This mechanism is implicated in many **neurodegenerative diseases.** 

The signal sequence is recognized by the import receptor on the outer mitochondrial membrane; then, there is a moment of lateral diffusion of the inner and outer mitochondrial complexes, until they match. At this point, the transport of proteins through both membranes reaches its highest efficiency. This is needed when the final destination of the protein is the mitochondrial matrix; indeed, proteins in mitochondria can reach the matrix, the inner mitochondrial membrane, the intermembrane space, or the outer mitochondrial membrane. 

Other pathways can bring proteins into mitochondria: 

- in **co-translational targeting,** a small number of inner membrane proteins have been reported to be imported co-translationally. 

- **ER-mediated mitochondrial targeting** (ER-SURF), in which the surface ofthe ER itselfplays an active role in the intracellular targeting of precursors from ribosomes to mitochondria. RNA localization analyses revealed that mRNAs for many mitochondrial proteins are located on the ER surface, suggesting that these proteins are intentionally synthesized on the ER and then handed over to mitochondria; however, the details of the ER-SURF pathway are not well understood and still await elucidation. mRNAs for mitochondrial proteins are sometimes present on the surface of the ER, since they take advantage of **membrane contact sites,** frequently occurring between ER and mitochondrial membranes. Indeed, bringing the membrane of the ER where these RNA are being translated (close to mitochondrial membranes) is essentially what drives the sorting of the proteins. 

### _Sorting to peroxisomes_ 

**Peroxisomes** acquire most of their enzymes directly from the cytosol. A few membrane proteins arri ve via vesicles that bud from the ER and mitochondrial membranes. Peroxisome proteins can have different targeting sequences ( as short as three amino acids ), depending on their final destinati on (lumen or membrane). Tue sequence is recognized by receptor proteins in the cytosol: a cytosolic protein floating 

111 

in the cytosol binds and recognizes a cargo protein. Then, the receptor docks to proteins on the peroxisomal membrane, and two possibilities can be present: 

- the receptor itself integrates in the membrane allowing the bound protein to enter the lumen **(luminal proteins).** 

- the receptor engages a translocator in the peroxisomal membrane that aids protein insertion in the membrane **(transmembrane protein).** 

Afterwards, the receptor retums to the cytosol. 

**Gated transport** is a mechanism used by cells to send proteins to the nucleus, where they are transported in their folded state. The passage inside the nucleus is regulated by both active transport and free diffusion, depending on the size of the molecules that are being carried. 

Proteins that need to be delivered to mitochondria and peroxisomes undergo **transmembrane transport;** when transported to the mitochondrial membrane, proteins are kept unfolded, thanks to the action of chaperones, while proteins destined to peroxisomes are mostly folded (this is the only case in which transmembrane transport carries proteins in this way). 

### _Transport to the endoplasmic reticulum_ 

Many different types of proteins are sorted to the ER, among which there are water-soluble proteins that need to stay in the ER lumen or be secreted outside of the cell, or proteins that need to go to other organelles but must transit through the ER first. Moreover, transmembrane proteins will stay embedded in the ER. 

Most of proteins that have ER as final destination are synthesized by ribosomes attached to the ER itself (rough endoplasmic reticulum). While being synthetized, these proteins are also translocated to the ER itself, while free ribosomes unattached to any membrane are making all of other proteins encoded by nuclear DNA. Membrane-bound ribosomes and free ribosomes are structurally and functionally identica!; they differ only in the proteins they are making at any given time. 

In arder to deliver a protein to the ER, the following are needed: 

- a **localization signal** (signal peptide) at the N-terminus ofthe protein. 

- sorting occurring **co-translationally.** 

- localization to the ER being mediated by a **signal recognition particle** (SRP), and as soon as this signal peptide emerges from the ribosome (it is the first to be translated), the signal recognition particle recognizes it and binds to and SRP receptor localized on the membrane itself, so that the whole complex can be translocated. 

- localization signal being **removed** after sorting. 

Once the sorting is completed, the localization signal is cleaved off. Some ribosomes of the common pool of ribosoma! subunits are recruited by RNAs that can code for cytosolic proteins or proteins targeted to other organelles, so they remain in the cytosol as **free ribosomes** translating for soluble proteins. Other ribosomes, instead, will be recruited by RNAs encoding for ER proteins, with a **signal recognition particle** at the N-terminus, so that they can be recognized by signal recognition particle receptors, which are **translocators,** having a hole through which the protein itself can fit to reach the ER unit. 

112 

When the complex is formed, the ribosome itself with the mRNA protein that is being translated is recruited to the ER surface: now the N-terminus of the protein can enter the translocator, and once the binding between the emerging polypeptide and the translocator occurs, then the other proteins of the complex can be shed off. 

The signal recognition particle is a **ribonucleoprotein** with a hunch formed by RNA portions, and proteins that either mediate the binding to the signal sequence or interfere with the ribosome itself to slow translation down, in order to give time to the ribosome with the emerging signal peptide to be carried to the ER. Thus, the signal recognition particle tells the ribosome to slow down so that it can be brought to the ER surface. Once the binding between the peptide and the translocator starts, then the signal recognition particle is released, so there are no more pauses and translation can resume. 

Here the two different paths for transmembrane proteins and soluble proteins divide. 

### _Soluble proteins_ 

Once having been translated by the ribosome and attached to the ER surface, and once the whole protein has been fed inside, a **signal peptidase** (a protein that can cut peptide bonds) cleaves the signal peptide off, and it remains in the membrane, while the synthetized protein is released in the lumen. 

### _Transmembrane proteins_ 

For transmembrane proteins **(single-pass proteins,** which only cross the membrane once), instead, an additional internal signal is required for retention in the membrane, by telling the translocator that a part of the protein must be retained in the membrane. Once this portion has been inserted in the membrane, proteins cannot change their orientation anymore. The sequence of the stretch that must remain in the membrane signals the translocator to stop pushing the polypeptide inside, and once the translocator reads the sequence and stops, the signal peptidase is recruited and chops off the signal peptide, so that a new N-terminus is created, and the C-terminus (last portion of the polypeptide synthetized by the ribosome) remains in the cytosol. 

Transmembrane proteins with more than one transmembrane portion **(multi-pass proteins)** have a starttransfer sequence that is not at the beginning of the polypeptide and that acts as a signal recognition particle (but is further down). However, it is not cleaved, and gets to the translocator as before; then, there is the feeding of everything else that comes after it, until the following stop-transfer signal comes to the translocator. In this way, both N- and C-terminus are kept in the cytosol. The signal sequence recognized by the signal recognition particle is not at the N-terminus, but is internal, and does not get cleaved off. 

This mechanism of starting and stopping transferring can happen multiple times in more complex proteins. 

At this point, the protein is either in the lumen of the ER or is embedded in the ER membrane (if it is a transmembrane protein ). 

113 

### _Vesicular transport_ 

However, the final destination may also not be the ER itself. Through **vesicular transport,** a vesicle made of membrane where the cargo is both what is inside the vesicle and the membrane of the vesicle itself is created. Target organelles include Golgi apparatus, endosomes and lysosomes, cell membrane and extracellular space. The membrane itself of the donor organelle buds, and gives out a piece of its own membrane to create a vesicle; everything contained in the vesicle - including the membrane surrounding it - will be delivered to another membrane, to which the vesicle will fuse, and it will deliver to that organelle both the cargo inside and the membrane surrounding it (both lipids in the bilayer of the membrane and the transmembrane proteins embedded in it). 

Proteins contain specific signals that determine their inclusion into vesicles directed to different cellular sites. Depending on which signal a protein contains, it will be sorted to different vesicles targeting various organelles. 

Vesicle trafficking has several important properties: 

- occurs between **membrane-enclosed compartments** (so for example vesicles cannot deliver proteins to the proteasome or to ribosomes, since they do not possess a membrane). 

- requires **budding, transport** and **fusion** of vesicles. A donor membrane must bud out and pinch offa vesicle that will be transported along microtubules to the destination organelle, when it will be caught and will fuse with the destination membrane. 

- is **bidirectional** (not always though). The transport can occur from the ER to the Golgi apparatus, but proteins can also be recycled back from the Golgi apparatus to the ER. 

- transports **soluble proteins** (contained inside vesicles), **transmembrane proteins** (embedded in the membrane ofthe vesicle itself) and **membrane lipids** (composing the membrane). 

- many **specific proteins** are involved, residing on both vesicles and on target compartment, all required for specificity and directionality of the transfer to occur. 

- involves **cytoskeleton** (mostly microtubules), as a track to transport vesicles to their destination. 

### _Adaptins and coat proteins_ 

**Adaptin** proteins help select cargo proteins. There are information on the protein itself, recognized by **cargo receptors,** which read this localization signal, and bind to it. Most of the time, these cargo receptors are recognized by adaptins, which are adaptor proteins that form a bridge between the cargo receptor and **coat proteins.** There are different types of adaptins depending on the vesicle that is being formed and on coat proteins. 

When a vesicle buds from a membrane, it needs a **coat,** a complex assembly of proteins that help shape that membrane into a rounded vesicle. There are mostly three classes of coat proteins: **clathrin, COPI,** and **CO PII,** which also determine the destination of the vesicle that has been formed. 

Let's consider a soluble protein in the ER that is recognized by a cargo receptor thanks to a localization signal. The cargo receptor ( attached to the membrane) binds the protein in the ER; adaptin will bind the cargo receptor and will be bound by the coat protein, which is shaping the membrane in order to form a bud. This is a way of synchronizing proteins that need to be delivered to the vesicle and those that are forming the vesicle. The coat serves at least two functions: 

114 

- helps shape the membrane into a bud. 

- captures molecules for onward transport. 

The coat forces the membrane to become rounded and form the vesicle. 

As soon as the vesicle buds, the coat disassemble **(uncoating process)** and this is necessary for the membrane of the vesicle to be recognized by the membrane of the organelle. 

### _Delivery of vesicles to the target organelle_ 

Vesicles pinched offa membrane are delivered and fuse with the acceptor membrane, in a process needing **accessory proteins.** 

The vesicle also houses **v-SNARE** proteins (where "v" stands for "vesicle"), which are important for the fusion process. Another protein, **RABs** or **markers,** identify the origin of the vesicle and its destination, and are able to tel1 the cell and the protein that sees that vesicle floating around where the vesicle comes from, and where it needs to go. 

During target recognition, **tethering proteins** (receptors) on the acceptor membrane receive the vesicle: the recognition between these tethering proteins and the marker on the vesicle mediates the capturing of the vesicle by the acceptor compartment. 

The following step is **docking,** a process during which the two membranes are brought so close to each other that they are able to touch, and this is allowed by the recognition and the binding between v- SNAREs and **t-SNAREs** (where "t" stands for "target"). These two proteins recognize each other and bind in a coiled fashion: they zipper together, allowing the membranes to get so close to each other that fusi on occurs, so that the vesicle becomes part of the target membrane itself. In this way, the cargo protein is delivered to the interior of the organelle, and the transmembrane proteins and the lipids on the vesicle are now part of the membrane of the acceptor compartment. 

Through this mechanism, many routes are present in the cell to carry proteins and membranes around: 

- from ER to Golgi apparatus, also known as **anterograde transport.** 

- from Golgi apparatus to the ER, also known as **retrograde transport (recycling** pathway). 

- from Golgi apparatus to lysosomes. 

- from Golgi to plasma membrane orto extracellular space, known as **secretory pathway.** 

- from the plasma membrane or from the extracellular space to lysosomes, known as **endocytosis** (not used to sort newly-synthetized proteins). 

When a protein synthetized in the cytosol needs to be secreted, it goes through multiple steps: from the ER, it is transported to the Golgi apparatus, and then either through secretory vesicles or through direct vesicles (to the celi exterior) is carried to its final destination. 

### _Anterograde transport_ 

Proteins destined for the Golgi apparatus (where they will work), or for the plasma membrane, lysosomes, and extracellular space (they goto the Golgi apparatus as an intermediate station) undergo **anterograde transport.** Buds coming out of the ER and going to the Golgi apparatus are packaged in 

115 

COPII-coated vesicles that leave the ER from **ER exit sites,** which are places where ethe endoplasmic reticulum is smooth. There are at least two ways in which proteins flow from the ER to the Golgi apparatus: 

- **unspecific leakage,** not mediated by target signals: even proteins destined for the ER itself can slowly and unspecifically be packaged in vesicles directed to the Golgi apparatus. 

- **active recognition** ( selective recruitment of cargoes displaying exit signals ), through cargo receptors and coat proteins, mediated by adaptins that connect them. As soon as the vesicle buds off, it sheds the coat, so that the membrane can be exposed and recognized by the target organelle. 

### _Retrograde transport_ 

**Retrograde transport** brings back proteins from the Golgi apparatus the ER. There are proteins that unspecifically leave the ER even if they are not supposed to, and as soon as the cell realizes that those proteins have been wrongly sent to the Golgi apparatus, it packages them into other vesicles coated with a different coat (COPI), which targets the proteins back to the ER. 

One way in which this pathway works is that soluble proteins that need to stay in the ER have a signal, the **KDEL signal** (lysine-aspartate-glutamate-leucine), recognized by a KDEL receptor, which interacts with the COPI coat covering the vesicle that needs to go back to the ER. The KDEL receptor in the Golgi apparatus binds to the protein, the COPI coat assembles around it, and signals that it needs to be carried back to the ER. In this way, the KDEL carrying protein can be brought back from the Golgi apparatus to the ER. 

The binding and unbinding of the KDEL to the receptor is ensured by an **acidity gradient** that controls the affinity of the binding. In the Golgi apparatus, the lumen is slightly more acidic than in the ER, and in this environment the KDEL and KDEL receptors have higher affinity. As soon as the complex arrives in the ER, where the lumen is less acidic, there is less affinity for the binding, so the protein is released. This occurs for soluble proteins, while transmembrane proteins are directly recognized by the COPI coat. They also have a signal in their sequence, the **KKXX signal,** directly recognized by the COPI coat, so that these transmembrane proteins can be brought back to the ER. 

### _From Golgi apparatus to lysosome_ 

The pathway from the Golgi apparatus to lysosomes is mostly used by **hydrolases,** which function in lysosomes and need to be accumulated there. Hydrolases destined to lysosomes are modified with a **posttranslational modification,** consisting in unusual **oligosaccharide, mannose-6-phosphate.** Indeed, they are first glycosylated in the ER, and then, when they reach the Golgi apparatus, the glycosylation is modified with a mannose-6-phosphate: this **phosphorylation** is the feature that signals that the protein needs to go to lysosomes. This modification is recognized by specific receptors bound by specific adaptins; then, **clathrin-coated vesicles** help the vesicle to bud from the Golgi apparatus and move to late endosomes, carrying the hydrolase with them. Late endosomes are even more acidic than the Golgi apparatus, and this low pH allows the receptor to detach from the glycosylated protein, so that it is recycled back to be used again. The hydrolase is now in the late endosome, which will slowly mature into a lysosome by acquiring more hydrolases, lowering its pH, and becoming very acidic. This route is irreversible. 

116 

### _Secretory pathway_ 

The **secretory pathway** transports proteins towards the plasma membrane and the extracellular space, according to two modalities: 

- **constitutive exocytosis** ( continuous process, present in all cells ). Tue secretion products are produced and immediately secreted (not stimulus-dependent). Indeed, it is a continuous process that happens in every cell (starting in the Golgi apparatus), in order to supply the plasma membrane with both proteins and lipids. It is a sort of default pathway, so no targeting signal is required. 

- **regulated exocytosis** (stimulus-dependent, not continuous only in secretory cells). Regulated exocytosis pathway is dependent on **specific stimulation** (release of hormones, pancreatic enzymes, neurotransmitters). Proteins are stored into secretory vesicles for later release when the stimulus comes. One way in which proteins are stored into these vesicles is through **aggregation:** in this case, the cell exploits the aggregati on phenomenon to increase the concentrati on of proteins that can be packaged into one vesicle, so that more proteins can be put in a single vesicle and a lot of product can be released upon stimulation. These secretory vesicles accumulate just below the plasma membrane, very close to it, ready to fuse with it when the stimulus comes. Once they fuse, many times, lipids and proteins (like SNARE proteins that allow the fusion of the vesicle) that were carried on the membrane but are not destined to the plasma membrane will be recycled back through endocytosis. 

_How are proteins retained in the ER and Golgi apparatus?_ 

## Some proteins need to be kept either in the ER or in the Golgi apparatus. 

As far as the ER is concemed, the cell uses the **KDEL signal** to retain proteins by telling the cell that those proteins are resident in the ER, so if they are found in the Golgi apparatus, they will be bound by the KDEL receptor, packaged in COPI-coated vesicle, and brought back to the ER. Transmembrane proteins instead have the **KKXX signal,** which works in the same way. 

Another pathway that allows proteins to stay either in the ER or in the Golgi apparatus is the **KIN recognition,** which involves the **aggregation** of proteins working in the same compartment. Proteins that work in the Golgi apparatus aggregate with each other in a way that prevents them from being packaged in a vesicle, since they become too big. 

F or the Golgi apparatus, there is also another way to retain proteins there, which is the **length** of the **transmembrane region:** indeed, the thickness ofthe bilayer itself is different in the Golgi apparatus and in the plasma membrane, which is thicker. Ifa protein has a shorter transmembrane region, it cannot fit in vesicles destined for the plasma membrane, and this prevents these proteins to be sent there, so they are instead retained in the Golgi apparatus. 

117 

### **Protein quality control in the endoplasmic reticulum** 

Proteins need to pass the **quality control** to finally finish their journey; indeed, proteins must have a correct folding. In this protein quality control, the endoplasmic reticulum plays a centrai role: from the ER, proteins can go to the Golgi apparatus and can be sorted again within the endomembrane system. Thus, it is important that only good protein exit the endoplasmic reticulum and are sorted to the cell. Proteins that are not folded correctly need to be degraded, and not to be sorted to the rest of the cell. Indeed, the **ER** performs this quality control system enabling only properly folded proteins to leave its compartment through **vesicular transport.** 

### _ER-associated degradation_ 

Whenever a protein is misfolded, the cell tries to fold it again, trying to help it get the right conformation. However, sometimes the process is not successful and therefore the protein has to be **eliminated.** The process that is used in the endoplasmic reticulum is called **ER-associated degradation** (ERAD). Tue misfolded protein is translocated back through the retro-translocation process to the cytosol, where it is **ubiquitinated** and sent to the **proteasome** for degradation. 

This mechanism is important for the cell because misfolded proteins are very dangerous, since they can **aggregate** and get deposited within a tissue, inducing damages and cell death. However, sometimes the process is even too well-performing and proteins that are not well folded but that would stili be partly functional are in any case degraded, as occurs in cystic fibrosis. 

**Cystic fibrosis** is one ofthe most common genetic diseases caused by a mutation within the CFTR gene, which encodes for a transmembrane chloride channel. The most frequent mutation that causes the disease is a deletion of a single amino acid, **phenylalanine,** localized in position 508 of the protein. Indeed, people who have this particular deletion will have a mutation at a DNA level that causes the synthesis of a polypeptide lacking this final phenylalanine in position 508. Only properly folded proteins can leave the ER through vesicular transport, so improperly folded proteins are exported from the ER and degraded in the cytosol through ER-associated degradation (ERAD). Modification ofthe protein compared to the wild-type (normai situation), causes a partial misfolding of the protein, and, as a result, the protein is degraded by the ER-quality control system. Thus, these proteins most of the times are not able to reach the membrane because they are degraded before. However, if they could reach the plasma membrane, this channel would be able to carry out at least some of the function, but the quality contro! system interferes with this and prefers to eliminate the protein that is not correctly folded, hence causing the channel not to reach its destination on the cell membrane. 

This example shows that the cell prefers to eliminate anything that is misfolded to avoid any problem. However, sometimes if this system could be bypassed for some specific proteins, some pathological situations could be adjusted: indeed, if this channel, that is partially misfolded, could reach the plasma membrane, a little bit of function could stili be there, and therefore severe symptoms could be avoided. In conclusion, the quality contro! system is fundamental for many reasons, but sometimes it can lead to a damage of the cell function, being **very stringent.** 

118 

### _Unfolded protein response_ 

Sometimes the quality control system can become **overwhelmed:** for example, when the cell is under stress, the rate of misfolded proteins increases, and the dismissed folded proteins accumulate in the endoplasmic reticulum, which is not able to keep the pace and to fold back all the misfolded proteins, in arder to re-establish a normal situation. When many misfolded proteins accumulate in the endoplasmic reticulum, the ER can trigger a mechanism that is called **unfolded protein response** (UPR), that allows the endoplasmic reticulum to grow in terms of physical space and in terms of proteins, which are required to try to fold again the misfolded protein. 

The UPR gets activated when unfolded or misfolded proteins accumulate in the ER, and it is characterized by the presence of sensors that are able to sense the presence of misfolded proteins in the ER, by recognizing stretches of amino acids that should be buried inside the protein, but that instead are present on the surface of proteins, and this is a signal that the protein has not been folded correctly. Then, this sensor in the ER is able to recognize an increase of misfolded protein in the cell, and the signal is sent to the nucleus to synthesize more ER components and **chaperone proteins,** which will help to restare a normai situation. The UPR response is based on a series of actions that the cell tries to put in place to restare the normal endoplasmic reticulum activity. 

First of all, the UPR **reduces** the **import** of **proteins** in the endoplasmic reticulum; for example, by synthesizing a **repressor** of **translation,** translation is attenuated, meaning that few proteins will be translated, and it is a way to try to manage the pressure on the endoplasmic reticulum. It is also possible to **increase** the **ER folding capacity** by synthesizing chaperones that will help to try to refold the misfolded proteins. Then, the UPR **increases** the **degradation capacity** of the cell, and the synthesis of all those **proteases** involved in the proteasome elimination process. If all those mechanisms still are not effective in restoring the normal situation, then the last resource is to activate the suicide program and to induce **cell death.** 

The cell always tries to fix the problems, but whenever they are too big or the cell is too damaged, it is better for the organism to get rid of the damaged cell, dangerous for the organism itself. If there is a stress that causes the accumulation of misfolded proteins in ER, the cell produces different responses: 

- **slow down protein synthesis.** 

- **activate chaperone genes** involved in the translation of proteins that are able to synthetize proteins the work in the endoplasmic reticulum. 

- **activate degradation** mediated by the **proteasome.** 

- if all these strategies do not work and the pressure on ER folding capacity does not go down, the cell activates **apoptosis,** so programmed cell death. 

119 

### **Celi signaling** 

Cells need to **communicate** with each other to carry out their functions correctly, and **celi signaling** is how they do so. In particular, in the mechanism of **signal transduction,** the secretion of molecules is received by target cells through the presence of **receptors.** One molecule (the incoming extracellular signal) is received by another molecule (the receptor, localized on the surface of the cell). This interaction activates a cascade of signals, known as **amplification,** that will alter the cell behavior, by modifying its metabolism, for example. 

The molecule that is produced by a cell can interact with a specific receptor, and this will cause a transduction (a translation) ofthis signal into an effect at the cell level, leading to a change in its function. 

_Types of communication_ 

There are four main mechanisms that can be used by cells: 

- **endocrine communication,** which is probably the most used. A specialized signaling cell is able to produce **hormones,** which are secreted in the bloodstream. The bloodstream transports this signal throughout the body until it reaches the target cells ( distant from signaling cell), that have a specific receptor for this molecule, and will be able to transduce the signal. Being the cells put in communication so **distant,** hormones are able to travel long distances in the blood in a short amount of time. 

- **paracrine communication.** Target cells are in **close proximity** to the signaling cell. The cell signaling produces a mediator (a signal molecule), which is not able to travel long distances, so it remains in the proximity of the signaling cell. Thus, other cells that are localized within the same tissue or that have a similar localization will be able to receive the message, but only if they have the right receptor for it, and they will respond to the signal. This is a kind of local communication: when there is an inflammation, chemicals are produced in the tissue and they influence nearby cells. 

   - A special use of this type of communication is the **autocrine communication,** where the cell is able to respond to a signal produced by itself. This is a way in which a cell can induce **selfproliferation** and **self-stimulation,** and it is something that also cancer cells do, since they are able to induce their own proliferation by producing growth factors. In this way, they are able to proliferate and to survive without the need for other signaling and cells. 

- **neuronal communication.** Neurons can produce **neurotransmitters** (very specific chemicals) that will be able to stimulate target cells through contact. At the end of the axon, there is the secretion of neurotransmitters that will induce an effect only on target cells. In this case, the molecule travels along the axon, so it is able to reach also target cells that are distant from the cell body of the signaling cell. 

- **contact-dependent communication.** Direct inter-physical interaction is needed between the signaling cell and the target cell, which expresses a receptor on its surface. There is a communication between two adjacent cells, and the signals can act over a long or short range. 

**120** 

### _Extracellular signals_ 

A typical cell is exposed to hundreds of different signal molecules, but it could be able to respond to only few of them. This is because in order to respond to the signal, the cell must possess the right receptor. This signal could induce different effects within the cell, including cell survival, cell growth, cell division and differentiation; moreover, signals can alter cell shape, movement, metabolism, or gene expression. Cells can receive the same stimuli, but when combined with other signal molecules, the effect can be different. Usually, ifa cell is not stimulated, it will die, undergoing apoptosis. 

**Signals** are able to bring messages, and the role of **effector proteins** is to amplify and propagate the message within the cell, transforming it into a physical effect. Moreover, the response of the cell can be diverse, even to the same signaling molecule, depending on the cell type, on the tissue, on the organ where the cell is located and on the type of receptor that it possesses. 

For example, the response of three different cell types (heart pacemakers, salivary glands and skeletal muscle cells), to the same molecule (acetylcholine) can widely vary. In the case ofheart pacemaker cells and salivary gland cells, there is a similar receptor, the **G-protein coupled receptor,** but the effects that are produced are different. For heart pacemaker cells, acetylcholine is a signal to decrease the rate of firing, while in salivary gland cells the reception of this signal induces the secretion of granules that contain components of saliva. Thus, depending on the type of cells and the cell role in the organism, the effect of the same molecule can be different. Moreover, in skeletal muscle cells acetylcholine induces contraction, so the receptor here is different from the previous ones. 

### _Cell response_ 

Cell response can be **fast or slow,** depending on what kind of mechanism the signal activates within the cell. Indeed, the signal molecule needs to be bound to the specific receptor and this can activate a cascade of events: for example, protein functions can be altered, and some modifications in the pool of proteins that are already present in the cell may be induced, leading to their **phosphorylation** and consequent activation/inactivation. 

In the fastest response (seconds to minutes), by acting on something that is already present, it is possible to alter the cytoplasmic machinery and the cell metabolism very rapidly, like in the **phosphorylation** and the **dephosphorylation pathways.** 

Instead, the slower response (minutes to hours) occurs when the interaction between the receptors and the signaling molecule activates another cascade of events that leads to the activation of **transcription factors** that target specific genes on the DNA molecule and bind to them. Indeed, for example, the cell may receive a growth stimulus, and in order to respond to it and activate mitosis, the cell needs to produce all the protein machinery that is involved in transcription or in protein synthesis. This takes time, because RNA transcription must be performed, and then RNA molecules need to be translated into proteins; at this point, the proteins will be effectors of the signaling molecules. Anyhow, at the end of the day, the effect is an alteration of cell behavior, cell metabolism and cell shape. 

Most extracellular signal molecules are large and **hydrophilic,** therefore they need to bind to specific receptors on the cell surface, because they would not be able to pass the membrane by themselves. 

121 

Instead, smaller, **hydrophobic,** extracellular signal molecules are able to pass through the target cell plasma membrane and bind to intracellular receptors. 

Hydrophobic signal molecules pass through the plasma membrane and bind to their receptor proteins located either in the cytosol or in the nucleus. The receptors, once activated, act as transcription regulators, promoting or inhibiting the transcription of specific target genes. Some hydrophobic signaling molecules such as **tyrosine, cortisol or steroid hormones** are very small and hydrophobic, so they menage to cross the plasma membrane and enter into the cell, where they can internet with their receptors. These receptors, once bound to the signaling molecules, get activated and undergo a conformational change that increases their affinity for the DNA molecule, therefore inducing an active transcription of target genes. 

### _Signa! transduction_ 

When there is an extracellular signal molecule that binds to the receptor on the cell surface, the cell "perceives" extracellular stimuli, which get converted into a series of intracellular reactions. This means that the binding ofthe extracellular signal molecule to the receptor induces a **conformational change** in the receptor itself, that in tum will activate a series of intracellular signaling molecules **(second messengers).** In turn, these signaling molecules will target other proteins, known as **effector proteins,** responsible for causing an effect within the cell, such as an alteration of metabolism, shape or movement, gene express10n. 

Within this cascade of events, proteins that take part in the process can play different roles: 

- some proteins simply relay the signal onward (receptor - binding of the signaling molecule - conformational change ofthe receptor - signal transmission onward). 

- at a certain point, an important protein is activated, and it has the role to **amplify** the signal received. This means that this protein will produce many intracellular messenger molecules, that in a cascade will activate target proteins. 

- other molecules are able to integrate signals from more than one intracellular pathway before relaying them: for example, many mechanisms can be occurring within the cell at the same time, and each one of them is not independent of what happens in the cell. Thus, the cell must be able to **integrate** all the stimuli and all the mechanisms, so proteins that are able to sense and integrate these signals are required. 

- finally, effector proteins distribute the signal, targeting specific proteins within the cell and evoking a complex response. 

### _Phosphorylation and GTP-binding proteins_ 

Signals are transduced through intracellular molecular switches, like **phosphorylation** and **GTPbinding proteins.** 

The phosphorylation cascade is the process by which a target protein can be phosphorylated by the addition of a phosphate group, or dephosphorylated by its removal. This molecular switch is very convenient for the cell, because it is very quick and it can have an effect within few seconds. However, it comes with a cast, which is **ATP consumption.** Phosphorylation cascades are an important mechanism for the regulation of signal transduction, and usually what happens is that when a **kinase** is activated, in 

122 

turn it is able to activate a series of other kinases by phosphorylation. Phosphorylation can occur at **serine/threonine residues** (in case of serine/threonine kinases) and at **tyrosine residues** (in case of tyrosine kinases ). 

Another mechanism is the one mediated by **GTP-binding proteins.** An example of GTP-binding protein are those involved in the trafficking of the nucleus: indeed, **Ran proteins** (responsible for the transport of proteins across the nucleus) are characterized by a different state based on the binding to GTP or GDP. **G-proteins** can be bound to GTP or GDP: when they are bound to GTP, they are active and they transduce the signal; when they are bound to GDP, they are inactive and there is no signal transduction. GTP and GDP can be exchanged, so when the protein gets activated, there is not a phosphorylation of the GDP molecule, but there is an exchange of the molecule, which loses affinity for GDP and acquires a new GTP molecule. Instead, usually GTP-proteins possess a **GTPase activity,** meaning that they will spontaneously hydrolyze the GTP molecule (making it loose a phosphate group ). 

### _Surface receptors_ 

Three types of cell surface receptors exist: 

- **ion channel coupled receptors,** which change the permeability ofthe membrane to selected ions. 

- **G-proteins coupled receptors,** which play an important role through switching mechanisms based on the ability to bind GTP or GDP, thus activating the intracellular signaling cascade. 

- **enzyme-coupled receptor,** which trigger the activation of a wide variety of intracellular signaling pathways. 

### _G-protein coupled receptors_ 

G-protein coupled receptors represent the largest family of cell-surface receptors, with more than 700 GPCR in humans, whose role is very important for the transduction of signals. An important characteristic that all these G-protein coupled receptors share is the structure: they are characterized by the presence of **seven transmembrane domains,** and indeed they are also called **seven-pass transmembrane receptor proteins.** They are able to recognize a wide variety of signal molecules, including proteins, small peptides, or derivatives of amino acids or fatty acids. 

When G-protein coupled receptors internet with a ligand (signaling molecule), they undergo a conformational change that induces their activation. G-proteins are characterized by the presence of 3 subunits: _a,_ B, _y._ The **a-subunit** is the one that interacts with GDP and GTP molecules. In the unstimulated state, the a-subunit has GDP bound to it, and the G-protein - anchored to the plasma membrane by lipid tails present on _a_ and _y_ - is not active. When an extracellular signal molecule binds to its receptor, the G-protein gets activated. The receptor is an intramembrane protein, and the membrane is not a static structure, but it is fluid, so everything that is inserted in the membrane is able to move and to flow. Once activated, the receptor is able to flow within the membrane and find the G-protein. The interaction between the receptor and the G-protein causes a conformational change in the G-protein, that activates it by causing the a-subunit to lose its affinity for GDP and exchange it for GTP. Once bound to GTP, the protein is active and as a consequence the a-subunit detaches from the _B-y_ complex. The two activated parts _(a_ and B-y) of the G-protein can directly internet with targets. 

123 

Once the G-protein is activated, it can induce the activation of target proteins. However, at some point, the effect ofthe signaling molecule will stop, and this means that at some point the G-protein will undergo inactivation. Inactivation is controlled by the a-subunit, which has an intrinsic GTPase activity, so it eventually hydrolyses its bound GTP to GDP, retuming to the inactive conformation. GTP hydrolysis and inactivation usually occur within seconds after the G-protein has been activated. Hence, the G-protein has few seconds to transduce the signal and activate target proteins, that in tum will amplify the signal brought by the first messenger molecule. 

G-proteins activate membrane-bound enzymes that produce small messenger molecules. The two main pathways that are the most used ones, whose main enzymatic targets are: 

- **adenylyl cyclase** (cAMP pathway). 

- **phospholipase C** (inositol phosphate pathway). 

There are other minor pathways that can be activated: 

- **guanylyl cyclase.** 

- **non-enzymatic targets** (ion channels ). 

### _cAMP pathway_ 

The G-protein is able to activate **adenylyl cyclase,** which is an enzyme involved in the production of **cAMP** ( cyclic AMP). The reaction starts from an ATP molecule: adenylyl cyclase is able to remove two phosphates and to catalyze the formation of a very peculiar bond between the phosphate group and the sugar part of the molecule, forming cAMP. cAMP is a second messenger, is produced in very high quantity, and is able to convey the message to the rest of the cell, activating a series of target effector proteins. The a-subunit of the G-protein can exist in different forms: **G1** (inhibitory), Gs (stimulatory), and GQ. This means that the activated G-protein a-subunit can switch on the adenylyl cyclase (Gs) or inhibit it (G1).When Gs is activated, there is a sudden increase in the synthesis of cAMP from ATP. 

To terminate the signal, **cAMP phosphodiesterase converts** cAMP to AMP, cleaving the cyclic bond and producing a linear AMP molecule. This stops the second messenger production. 

The target of cAMP could be the activation of PKA, **protein kinase A** ( cAMP-dependent protein kinase ). This kinase is usually inactive in the cell because it is formed offour subunits: two catalytic subunits and two regulatory subunits. When the protein is in this conformation, it is not active, since catalytic subunits are kept inactive by the presence of the regulatory subunits. When cAMP is produced, this molecule is able to bind to the regulatory subunits and, as a consequence, decreases their affinity for the catalytic subunits, which are thus released and are free to target specific effector proteins within the cell. Since PKA is a kinase, it will phosphorylate target proteins, altering their activity. 

**Epinephrine,** a signaling molecule in skeletal muscle cells, is recognized by a G-protein coupled receptor, also called **adrenergic receptor.** The binding of the molecule induces the activation ofthe G- protein that is coupled to the receptor, and of its a-subunit in particular. This is a stimulatory subunit, so as result there is the activation of adenylyl cyclase, and the production of great amount of cAMP. cAMP activates protein kinase A, already present in the cell, but in an inactive state due to the presence of the regulatory subunits that were inhibiting the catalytic activity. The active kinase is able to phosphorylate 

124 

target proteins, such as the phosphorylase kinase, and this induces a cascade of phosphorylation events. Indeed, PKA activates another kinase, that is able to activate by phosphorylation other kinases, and the final effect is the induction of glycogen breakdown and the inhibition of glycogen synthesis. 

PKA, activated by cAMP, is able to phosphorylate kinases that are already present in the cell, therefore inducing a change in the metabolism, but it can also activate **transcription factors.** PKA phosphorylates and activates regulatory molecules (transcription factors ), that are able to bind and promote specific genes and induce the transcription. This kind of signaling pathways can target both quick responses (glycogen breakdown), but also some response that take more time (transcription of genes). 

### _Inositol phosphate pathway_ 

A signaling molecule can be sensed and received by the GPCR; the interaction between the receptor and the signaling molecule activates the G-protein. However, in the **inositol phosphate pathway,** the G- protein activates **phospholipase C** instead of adenylyl cyclase. 

Phospholipase C is able to cleave inositol phospholipid, that is attached to the plasma membrane, in two molecules: 

- **diacylglycerol** (DAG), that remains stuck in the membrane. 

- **inositol-1,4,5-trisphosphate** (IP3), that diffuses in the cytosol. 

Both molecules acts as second messengers. The G-protein that is able to activate the phospholipase C pathway is characterized by the presence of an a-subunit called **GQ:** 

- once the signal molecule activates the receptor, it is able to activate the G-protein by inducing the exchange of GDP with GTP. 

- the a-subunit is able to activate phospholipase C, which cleaves the inositol phospholipid into diacylglycerol and IP3. 

- at this point, diacylglycerol recruits another kinase, **protein kinase C** (PKC) to the membrane. 

Instead, IP3 induces **calcium release** from the ER into the cytoplasm: indeed, the ER is a reservoir of calcium ions, and on the membrane of the ER there are receptors able to recognize and bind to IP3. Whenever phospholipase C activates the cleavage of the inositol phospholipid and IP3 is produced, there is an activation of calcium channels on the membrane of ER, and calcium diffuses in cytoplasm. 

Calcium can participate in the activation of a series of processes in the cell; for example, it activates PKC, which not only needs to translocate to the membrane thanks to the interaction with diacylglycerol, but also needs to bind calcium ions to be active. Once activated, PKC can phosphorylate target proteins that can mediate the eff ects within the cell. 

Calcium concentration in cytosol is maintained very low compared to the extracellular compartment, or to the ER and mitochondria, which are the places where the cell tends to stare calcium ion. The cell possesses some transporters that pump calcium ions from the cytosol to the external environment, or that pump calcium ions inside the ER or mitochondria, if it wants to stare them. Whenever there are these signals mediated by phospholipase C and there is the production ofIP3, the effect is the release of calcium from the ER into the cytosol, leading an increase in calcium concentration, which will activate a series of events inside the cell. Not only calcium is needed to activate PKC, but one of the most common 

125 

responsive protein to calcium is **calmodulin,** which needs calcium ions to get activated. Once activated, it can be the permanent regulatory subunit of an enzyme complex or it can alter the activity of target proteins. Indeed, calmodulin is part of the **CaM-kinases,** or **Ca**<sup>**2**</sup> **+/calmodulin-dependent protein kinases,** that are activated not only by the presence of calcium, but also by the interaction with activated calmodulin. Among the targets of calmodulin there are many enzymes and membrane transport proteins.
