Sometimes, the _B-y_ complex can play an important role in these activation pathways. For example, it is able to interact with ion channels inducing a change in their conformation. The _B-y_ complex is able to **open potassium channels** localized in the plasma membrane, therefore allowing the flow of potassium ions across the membrane, thus increasing the permeability of the membrane to potassium ions. The inactivation ofthe a-subunits then retums the G-protein to its inactive state. Therefore inhibiting also the activity of the _B-Y_ complex, and, as consequence, the ion channels will retum to a closed state. 

The signal transduction pathway has the function of inducing some effects within the cell, but the cell also needs at some point to **shut down** the **signaling,** after the effect has been caused. Signal transduction can be switched-off in different ways: G-proteins have an intrinsic **GTPase activity,** so they can be switched-offthanks to this mechanism. However, there is also the need to remove the second messengers and to desensitize the receptor inducing the removal of ligands. 

### _Receptor desensitization_ 

The block of the signaling performed by the receptor can be obtained in different ways. 

- **endocytosis** and **temporary sequestration** in endosomes. It is possible to have a receptor sequestration pathway where once the receptor is bound to the signaling molecule, the activation induces a series of effects within the cell (activation of G-protein, cAMP cascade or phospholipase C cascade ), but also induces the intemalization of the receptor bound to its signaling molecule. The receptor is therefore included in a vesicle that is destined to **endosomes;** the pH of the vesicle causes the detachment of the signaling molecule from the receptor, so that the signaling molecule gets degraded and the receptor can be recycled to the surface, ready to perform another interaction with another signaling molecule. 

- **destruction** of the **receptors** in **lysosomes** (receptor **downregulation).** Sometimes, the receptor can be sent to degradation, so it is not recycled back to the cell surface, but it is sent to the lysosomes, where both ligand and receptor will be degraded. This pathway results in a downregulation of the receptor, meaning that once the receptor is intemalized, it is degraded. Thus, the pool of receptors available on the cell surface will be decreased. 

- **rapid inactivation** of the receptors, with the receptor itself that gets inactivated. 

- **change** in a **protein involved** in **transducing** the **signal.** A direct molecule interacting with the receptor in the transduction cascade can be inactivated. 

- **production** of an **inhibitor** that blocks the transduction process. Usually, this inhibitory protein is produced as an effect of the signal cascade, meaning that this kind of pathway also has a way to block the cascade itself, thus preventing the signal transduction to go on indefinitely. Hence, as a result of the activation cascade, there is the production of a new inhibitory protein that will stop the transduction process. 

126 

### _Inactivation by phosphorylation_ 

The receptor can also be blocked by **phosphorylation.** When the G-protein coupled receptor is activated, it activates the G-protein and all the pathways after it, but it can also activate a protein kinase, called **Gprotein-coupled receptor kinase** (GRK), able to phosphorylate the receptor in some cytosolic part of multiple sites. Once the receptor is phosphorylated, it can be bound by another protein, called **arrestin,** which prevents the protein from interacting with other receptors. This is an example of how a receptor can be inactivated by inhibiting its activity, so by inducing its interaction with a protein that masks its interaction site with the G-protein. 

### _Remava! of second messengers_ 

N ot only the receptor needs to be inactivated to restore the steady conditi on, but the second messenger must be removed to switch-off the signal. cAMP can be removed by a specific enzyme, **cAMP phosphodiesterase,** that is able to break this peculiar bond between the phosphate group and the sugar, therefore producing a linear AMP molecule and switching-off the signal. 

Then, also calcium levels can be rapidly restored within the cell, by activating a series of pumps **(Ca2+/Na+ antiporter, Ca2+ pump)** that move calcium ion to the extracellular environment orto the deposits present in the cell, in ER and mitochondria. 

### _G-protein GTPase activity_ 

Finally, G-protein itself has an intrinsic **GTPase activity** that enables it to switch-off the signal within few seconds. In this case, when the GTP displaces the GDP molecule (when the G-protein is activated), the a-subunit has its GTPase activity that hydrolyses GTP into GDP, releasing a phosphate group. This is a sort of a safety mechanism that the G-protein has, and that allows it to be automatically switched-off whenever it has been activated. 

### _Effects of bacteria on the G-protein coupled receptor_ 

Bacterial toxins are able to interfere with the activity of G-protein. **Cholera toxin** and **pertussis toxin** are both produced by pathogens, but with different mechanisms of action, even though both toxins hit the transduction mechanism mediated by G-proteins. Cholera toxin is produced by the pathogen agent of **cholera** (the bacterium _vibrio cholerae)_ and is an acute intestina! infection caused by ingestion of contaminated food or water. Pertussis toxin is produced by the pathogen agent of whooping cough (the bacterium _bordetella pertussis),_ and is a highly contagious respiratory disease affecting children. 

Normally, once the receptor is activated, the G-protein gets activated as well, and later GTP is hydrolyzed and the G-protein becomes inactive again. These toxins hit this mechanism. 

Cholera toxin is able to inhibit the GTPase activity of the G-protein, in particular ofthe Gs subunit. As result, since the G-protein is not able to hydrolyze and "inactivate itself' anymore, there is an hyperactivation of adenylyl cyclase, and a continuous production of cAMP, which causes an excessive release ofwater and ions, causing **diarrhea.** 

Regarding pertussis toxins, the mechanism is slightly different. The subunit that is hit is an inhibitory subunit, and what the toxin does is to inhibit the activation of the inhibitory subunit; as a result, the 

127 

inhibitory subunit is not able to switch-off adenylyl cyclase, and the consequence is a hyperactivation of this enzyme, leading to an increased production of cAMP and to the stimulation of **coughing.** 

_Kinase activity_ 

Additionally, two situations are possible: 

- the ligand activates the receptor, which already contains the catalytic activity. This class of receptors include **receptor tyrosine kinase, receptor serine/threonine kinase,** and **receptor guanylyl cyclase.** 

- the ligand activates the receptor, which lacks the catalytic activity and must be associated with another enzyme that will be responsible for carrying out effects at a cellular level. This kind of receptors are known as **enzyme-coupled receptors.** 

### _Receptor tyrosine kinase_ 

In humans, **receptor tyrosine kinases** can be classified in twenty subfamilies. These receptors differ from each other for their specificity to the ligand and for the type of reactions and pathways that are activated within the cell. All these receptors are characterized by three big domains: 

- **intracellular catalytic domain,** with tyrosine kinases activity. 

- **transmembrane domain,** through which they remain attached to the membrane and pass it. 

- **extracellular domain,** specific for the binding with the ligand. 

One of the most important elements in the transduction pathway that is mediated by receptor tyrosine kinases is represented by the **RAS proteins,** belonging to the class of **monomeric G-proteins.** 

RAS activation depends on the binding with a nucleotide: its active form ( conformation) is associated with the binding of a GTP molecule, whereas the inactive conformation is associated with the binding of GDP molecole. RAS, being a G-protein, possesses a hydrolytic activity on GTP, and it is able to hydrolyze GTP into GDP. The activation of RAS occurs when GTP is bound to it, thanks to the interactions with accessory proteins. In this case, there are **GEF proteins** (guanine exchange factors) which mediate the exchange of GDP- released by the G-protein - with GTP- taken up by the G-protein itself. Instead, the hydrolytic activity of the G-protein is helped and activated by a **GAP protein,** which mediates the activation of this intrinsic activity. 

There are different types of RAS proteins in cells (H-, K-, N-RAS), which can have a tissue-specific distribution; overall, they have small differences, but are very similar, and share the mechanism of transduction pathway. There are also different proteins that belong to the GEF family, and different proteins belonging to the GAP family.

<!-- Start of picture text -->

128 

RAS is attached to the membrane through a lipid anchor, so the protein stays in contact with the membrane but at the same time is localized on its cytosolic surface. RAS has a centrai role in mediating the signal transduction pathway, whose activation is usually connected to **celi growth;** thus, by mutating RAS, very severe effects may arise at the level of the cell activity. Indeed, this is confirmed by the fact that about 30% of human tumors are characterized by mutations in RAS proteins, which make RAS **hyperactive** and able to act by itself. Indeed, RAS becomes active also in the absence of a stimulation by the receptor, or it can become unable to perform its GTPase activity, thus always remaining in the active form, even if the signal of the receptor is shout down. The consequence ofhaving a constitutionally active RAS form is a **constantly-induced celi proliferation** and **celi growth,** and this is the very advantage characteristic for cancer cells and one of their hallmarks. 

Receptor tyrosine kinases are usually present as monomers; however, they are able to internet with a signal molecule in the form of a dimer. This requires, in order to allow the relaying of the signal into the cell, a **dimerization** of the receptor, hence two receptors must simultaneously bind to signal molecule. Thus, when RTK binds to the signal molecule, it dimerizes, so that two monomers come together; when they are in close proximity, they are stimulated to **phosphorylate** themselves **reciprocally,** meaning that one monomer can phosphorylate the other and vice versa, on specific tyrosine residues. 

Once the cytosolic tails of the receptors have been phosphorylated, they can act as **docking sites** for other cytoplasmic proteins. After the activation and the phosphorylation of tyrosine residues, proteins are recruited to the membrane by binding with phosphorylated tyrosine residues. These proteins are able to internet with the phosphorylated tails through specific domains, and can in turn have themselves a kinetic activity. Altematively, these proteins can also just act as a scaffold, meaning that they simply act as a docking platform for the recruitment of other enzymes, that will play a specific role within the cell. 

Actually, RAS does not directly internet with the receptors, but it interacts with a series of mediators that are recruited at the level ofthe cytosolic tail. Indeed, there is an adaptor protein called **Grb2,** which acts as a scaffold, is recruited at the level ofthe receptor, and is able to recruit **RAS-GEF** proteins. 

The RAS-GEF protein stimulates the activation of RAS by inducing the exchange of GDP with GTP; as a consequence, RAS becomes activated and it is able to then activate a specific cascade within the cell. 

RAS activates a **cascade** of **kinases** that activate in tum other kinases, so that the final effect of this pathway is the activation of **MAP kinase** (Erk). The MAP kinase is responsible for the activation of a series of targets, which can be proteins that are already present in the cell, or it can induce changes in gene expression, being able to phosphorylate transcription factors, therefore changing their activity. However, MAP kinase is just the last step of the cascade of events; indeed, it was activated by another kinase, known as **MAP kinase kinase** (Mek), which was activated by the kinase that was activated by RAS and that is called **MAP kinase kinase kinase** (Raf). These kinases are called MAP kinases because they have a catalytic role in activating **mitogen-activated protein kinases** (MAPK). Mitogens are signaling receptors that stimulate cell growth and proliferntion, so the pathway involving RAS plays a role in cell proliferntion and cell growth.

RTKs can also modulate other pathways; for example, they can recruit **phospholipase C-f'/** to the membrane, and it is involved in the release of second messengers that will increase calcium levels. Phospholipase _C-y_ is able to direetly internet with the reeeptor, so no intermediates are needed, sinee it possesses speeifie domains (sueh as SH2) that allow it to direetly internet with speeifie phosphorylated tyrosine residues on the reeeptor tails. The reeeptors ean reeruit also a **GAP protein,** whieh is a GTPaseaetivating protein that will trigger the intrinsie GTPase aetivity of the RAS protein, so is involved in shutting down the signal transduetion. Another very important pathway that is aetivated by RTKs is the one mediated by **phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase.** This kinase is able to phosphorylate target lipids that are loealized in the plasma membrane, so that they will aet as doeking site for the reeruitment of other proteins to the plasma membrane.

Another pathway is the one mediated by **Src kinases,** whieh ean be direetly reeruited by the aetivated eytosolie tails, and whieh mediate the phosphorylation of other target proteins on tyrosine. Src, PLC-y, 

130 

PI 3-kinase and the GAP protein are all able to interact with the receptor because they possess very conserved domains that are specific for the interaction with phosphorylated tyrosine. They have two main domains that are called SH2 and PTB: SH2 stands for "Src homology region", and refers to the fact that it was find in the Src protein for the first time; PTB stands for "phosphotyrosine-binding domain". They both enable a protein that carry them to bind to the receptor and mediate some effects. 

When RTKs are activated, PI 3-kinase can directly interact with the phosphotyrosine produced on the cytoplasmic face of the receptors, and phosphorylate the inositol phospholipid. At this point, the phosphorylated form of the lipid acts as docking platform, so it can recruit other signaling molecules to the membrane and activate them. For example, there can be the recruitment of a protein kinase that in turn can phosphorylate and activate another important mediator, **Akt.** Akt is a serine-threonine kinase and is able to phosphorylate other proteins on serine and threonine residues; it is also called **protein kinase p** (PKT). The main pathway that is activated when PI 3-kinase is involved in the activation of this kinase (Akt), important to promote **celi survival.** 

Phospholipase C cleaves phosphatidyl-inositol into diacylglycerol and IP3, so it generates second messengers that diffuse into the cell and activate all the kinases and proteins. PI 3-kinase, instead, has a different function because it is a docking site for the recruitment of other signaling proteins. In order to shut down the signal, it is necessary to dephosphorylate lipids, and there are some phosphatases that perform this job, such as **PTEN,** which removes phosphate from the inositol ring (it is also a gene that can be mutated in cancer cells). 

When PI 3-kinase is activated, there is the final activation of Akt. Among the targets of Akt, there are two important elements: 

- **TOR,** which, once activated, induces **celi growth,** promoting protein synthesis and inhibiting protein degradation. 

- proteins involved in the **apoptosis pathway,** triggered when there is an unbalance between proapoptotic and antiapoptotic proteins. If the balance is in favor of antiapoptotic proteins, the cell survives, but if the balance is in favor of proapoptotic proteins, the cell commits suicide. 

Akt has a survival role, since activates **Bcl2,** which is a **pro-survival factor.** There are two proteins: **BAD,** which is **proapoptotic,** and Bcl2, which is antiapoptotic. When they are present in a complex, Bcl2 is not active, but when a survival signal comes from the extemal environment, there is the activation of receptors and also the activation of Akt, which phosphorylates BAD, leading to the release of Bcl2. In this way, BAD is inactive, and Bcl2 is active, hance promoting cell survival. 

### _RTK inactivation_ 

The inactivation ofRTK can be performed by **phosphatases,** which remove the phosphates localized on the receptor; altematively, the receptor can be included in a vesicle and can be inactivated by **endocytosis.** Thus, once the receptor has carried out its function, it is included in a vesicle sent to the endosomes, where the receptor can be recycled on the surface or can be sent to lysosomes for degradation. 

Since the signaling molecules induce growth and survival of the cell, any mutation that arises in these pathways can be very dangerous, because it can lead to the activation of molecules even when they should not be activated. Indeed, RAS is a protein that is often mutated in some types of cancer, and this kind of 

131 

mutations can lead to the activation of the RAS protein even in the absence of stimulation by the receptor. A cell that gains the possibility to proliferate without being stimulated represents a potentially dangerous situation, because it can lead to the development of **cancer.** Thus, many proteins involved in these pathways have to be tightly controlled by the cell, because their activation or inability to get inactivated can lead to this uncontrolled proliferation. 

### _Cytokine receptors_ 

**Enzyme-coupled receptors** do not possess an enzymatic activity, but they are associated with other proteins that carry out this kind of function, as happens in the case of **cytokine receptors,** which are able to bind cytokines, involved in **inflammation processes.** These receptors have different structures, being associated with accessory proteins called **JAK** (Januse kinase ). When the ligand ( cytokine) binds the receptor, it causes the receptor's **dimerization** and the activation of the associated kinases (Januse kinases ), which are able to phosphorylate themselves and to also phosphorylate the receptor itself. Once the receptor is phosphorylated, it is able to recruit regulatory proteins, which in this case belong to the **STAT** family. STAT proteins are phosphorylated by JAK and are able to internet with the receptor, since they possess specialized SH2 domains that recognize the phosphorylated tyrosine on the receptor. 

In this case, the receptor does not have a kinetic activity by itself, but it is associated with some other proteins, JAKs, that provide for this function. Once the STAT proteins are phosphorylated, they are released by the receptor and they dimerize, forming a particular structure. Once they have formed the dimer, there is also the formation of a **nuclear signal patch,** present only when the proteins are dimerized. Thus, the complex can be transported into the nucleus, where it regulates the expression of specific genes. 

Indeed, STAT proteins act as **transcription factors,** and in particular, they regulate the expression of genes that are involved in the inflammation process. 

### _Ligand-gated channels_ 

**Ligand-gated channels** change the permeability of the membrane to selected ions, and once opened by the interaction with the ligand, they allow the passage of specific ions. These receptors are present in neuronal and excitable cells, and they can assume different states, either an opened or closed conformation. When they are in the open state, ions can flow through them; when they are in the closed state, they inhibit the passage of molecules. It is possible to find different types of channels in cells: 

- **voltage-gated channels,** where the opening and closing of the channels depends on change of membrane potential, so on a voltage-dependent mechanism. 

- **ligand-gated channels,** regulated by a ligand, which can either be extracellular or intracellular, even a mediator that is produced during a signal transduction pathway (like IP3 opening calcium channels on the endoplasmic reticulum). An example of ligand-gated channels are those present in neurons, where they are able to bind neurotransmitters that induce the opening of the channels and the flow of some kind of ions in post synaptic cells, which determines a change in the membrane potential and an electric signal. At the level of excitatory synapses, there is the release of neurotransmitters such as **glutamate,** which activates sodium channels and therefore causes a flow of cations in the cell, which causes a change in membrane potential towards a **depolarization.** lnstead, in other cases, there are other neurotransmitters that open channels for 

132 

anions, and in this case there is the flow of chloride ions inside the cell, causing a change in the membrane potential towards a **hyperpolarization,** since negative charges are introduced inside the cell. 

   - The **muscular acetylcholine receptor** is formed by different subunits that create a complex within the plasma membrane. The receptor is usually in a closed conformation, and this is due to the fact that hydrophobic chains are localized at the level of the gate, therefore occluding the pore. When **acetylcholine** binds to this receptor, the effect is a conformational change that induces also the movement of the chains that were occluding the pore, and this allows the channel to open and determines a flow of positive charges across the membrane. The selectivity of the channels also depends on the amino acids that compose it (those making up the gate in particular), and therefore will be selective for the kind of ions that can pass. 

   - In **ionotropic receptors,** it is the neurotransmitter itself that opens the channel, while in **metabotropic receptors,** the neurotransmitters can bind other kinds of receptors, such as the G- protein coupled receptors, so there is the opening of channels mediated by some effector proteins. 

- **mechanically gated channels,** such as the ones present in the ear, so that auditory ear cells are able to sense the presence of sound and induce an opening of the gate. 

### _Nitric oxide signaling_ 

In response to acetylcholine that is secreted by nearby nerves, endothelial cells secrete **nitric oxide,** thanks to a specific enzyme called **nitric oxide synthase.** In this kind of cells, acetylcholine is sensed and recognized by a G-protein coupled receptor. Acetylcholine is an example of molecule that can bind to ion channel receptors but can also bind in different cell types to G-protein coupled receptors. The response at the level of endothelial cells is the activation of the associated G-protein, which will be characterized by the **Q subunit.** As a result, there is the activation of **phospholipase C,** which produces diacylglycerol and IP3. **IP3** induces an increase in calcium concentration within the cell, since it binds to receptors on the endoplasmic reticulum that open calcium channels. **Calcium** is an important activator of **calmodulin,** which in turn activates nitric oxide synthase, which is able to process and to convert arginine into nitric oxide. Nitric oxide is a **gas,** so it is able to diffuse very rapidly, and to cross the membrane and reach neighboring cells, especially smooth muscle cells, where it stimulates **guanylyl cyclase** to produce cyclic GMP, which will activate **protein kinase G,** inducing muscle relaxation and dilation of blood vessels. Nitric oxide has also an **anti-inflammatory** and **anti-thrombotic effect.** This mechanism is the one at the basis of **nitroglycerin,** used as a treatment against **angina pectoris** ( chest pain); indeed, nitroglycerin is directly converted into nitric oxide, and therefore it induces the relaxation of smooth muscles. 

133 

### **Apoptosis** 

**Apoptosis** is a form **ofprogrammed celi death,** and is a process that cells activate during development, but also in adult tissues, to maintain homeostasis. Apart from development, apoptosis is important also in other physiological conditions: indeed, in adults, it is responsible for maintaining a balance between cell proliferation and cell death. Many tissues are characterized by a very high turnover of cells, meaning that cells are continuously produced and eliminated. The tissue must retain its **integrity,** meaning that it must maintain a stable and adeguate number of cells, unless it has to proliferate and increase its volume. 

**Homeostasis** is obtained thanks to the balance between the mechanisms of apoptosis and cell proliferation. Moreover, apoptosis is also important as a **defense mechanism,** and is activated whenever the cell is damaged and cannot be repaired anymore. Whenever a cell undergoes an injury, there is a problem during replication (mitosis ), or DNA is damaged (which can be induced by chemical or physical events), the cell tries to repair the damage by inducing a series of events that will lead to the activation of repairing mechanisms. However, when the cell is not able to repair the damage, then it is safer for the organism to eliminate the damaged cell, otherwise its permanence would lead to very severe consequences. The organism performs this by activating the apoptosis process. 

Another important function, strictly related to the previous ones, is the involvement of apoptosis in **immune system function.** During immune system development, cells of adaptive immunity are selected such that only the ones that recognize as dangerous "self' cells are eliminated (since they would lead to the development of autoimmune pathologies ), while all the others are retained. 

Once the apoptosis process is activated, it is possible to observe **morphological** and **biochemical modifications.** One ofthe most evident marks is **DNAfragmentation** with chromatin condensation, and the nucleus undergoing fragmentation itself. At the end of the process, the cell implodes, forming **apoptotic bodies** (fragments of the cell), which will be very rapidly eliminated by phagocytosis carried out by specialized cells. 

### _Necrosis_ 

Apoptosis is not the only way cells can die; the other most important modality of cell death is **necrosis.** N ecrosis and apoptosis are very diff erent from each other: apoptosis is a very clean process and does not lead to inflammation, meaning that it does not have side effects for the tissue, while necrosis occurs when a tissue is injured, so it can be defined as the **pathological death** of one or more cells or even of a portion of tissue or an organ. N ecrosis also leads to **inflammati on,** which in tum can cause even more severe damage. Indeed, necrosis is a passive process, mainly caused by injuries to the tissue; for this reason, it usually involves a group of cells or even big pieces of the tissue of an organ. The cell swells and bursts and, as consequence, its content is released within the tissue, causing inflammation. 

### _Apoptosis dysregulation_ 

Apoptosis is instead an active process, not only because it is activated in specific conditions, but also because it requires **ATP** to be carried out. It usually involves single cells that are no longer needed or that are damaged, and are thus targeted by this system. However, apoptosis does not happen indiscriminately, but it applies only to targeted cells, which shrink and form **apoptotic bodies** (fragments that are then eliminated). As a consequence, there is no inflammation nor release of cell content. There 

134 

are specific signals that can trigger and activate this program. Once activated, there are molecular events that lead to the activation of **effector proteins** responsible for all true morphological modifications within the cell. It is important to maintain a balance between cell proliferation and cell death, since in normal tissues homeostasis is required, therefore new cells must replace the older ones when they die, in order to maintain tissue integrity and a constant number of cells in the tissue. 

However, problems can arise when this situation is not balanced, meaning whenever there are too many or very few dying cells. When apoptosis is not carried out correctly, there are few cell deaths, and this situation can be characteristic of some diseases: for example, **cancer cells** manage to escape apoptosis, which leads to **cancer** cell **proliferation** even though the organism is trying to switch it off. **Autoimmune diseases,** instead, are characterized by the fact that there are autoreactive lymphocytes, able to recognize cells belonging to the organism as pathogens. These aberrant lymphocytes should have been eliminated, but since apoptosis failed, autoreactive cells survived, leading to autoimmune diseases. Instead, when the rate of apoptosis is excessive, there is the risk to develop other problems: for example, **neurodegeneration** takes place when neurons undergo apoptosis, leading to nervous system disorders. 

_Morphological and biochemical changes associated with apoptosis_ 

Some marks are specific for apoptotic cells and allow to distinguish them from normal and healthy cells. 

##### **APOPTOTIC CELLS LOSE CONTACT WITH NEIGHBORING CELLS AND THE EXTRACELLULAR MATRIX** 

Indeed, apoptotic cells start to **shrink** and lose contact with the matrix, and instead of having an elongated shape, they start to become **round.** The rounded appearance that they get indicates that these cells are no longer able to internet with the environment, namely with other cells or with the proteins of the matrix. This is caused by the fact that proteins involved in **junctions** ( either cell-to-cell or cell-to-matrix) are destroyed during apoptosis. Hence, cell-to-cell adhesion is lost because of the degradation of proteins involved in the junctions. 

##### **APOPTOTIC CELLS DISPLAY AN ALTERED MITOCHONDRIAL PERMEABILITY** 

Once apoptosis is activated, mitochondria undergo structural changes and their membranes become **more permeable,** therefore mitochondrial proteins diffuse into the cytoplasm. **Cytochrome C** is a protein that is normally localized within mitochondria, but in apoptotic cells it becomes widespread in the cytoplasm. This is an indication that, due to the activation of the apoptotic program, mitochondrial proteins, and in particular cytochrome C, are no longer able to be retained inside. 

##### **NUCLEAR ALTERATI ON** 

When apoptosis is activated, the nucleus changes its appearance, becoming progressively more **fragmented,** until, after a longer time, its pieces are present everywhere within the cell. Indeed, in apoptotic cells, the nucleus starts to fragment and to break down into smaller units; moreover, some modifications occur at the DNA level. When apoptosis has not been activated yet, the DNA molecule is intact, but as time passes after apoptosis activation, the situation changes, and the integrity of the DNA molecule changes as well. After three hours, it is possible to notice that DNA has been broken down into small pieces. 

135 

##### **ALTERATION IN THE PLASMA MEMBRANE SYMMETRY COMPOSITION** 

U sually, in healthy cells, there is a particular phospholipid, known as **phosphatidylserine,** exposed on the inner leaflet of the membrane, towards the cytoplasm. Whenever the apoptotic program is activated, phosphatidylserine is **flipped** to the outer leaflet. Thus, there is a loss in the asymmetric distribution of this phospholipid, which will now be predominantly localized on the outer leaflet. This is important because apoptotic cells need to be eliminated by the organism, so some flag must be put on these cells to make macrophages eliminate them; indeed, the presence of phosphatidylserine on the outer leaflet is an **'eat-me'** signal. 

##### **MEMBRANE BLEBBING** 

The membrane of an apoptotic cell starts to **deform,** to lose its shape, and at the end, it forms small bodies **(apoptotic bodies),** that will be eliminated by the other cells. A normal cell, instead, has an intact membrane and a round shape, but when it becomes apoptotic, the shape of the cell changes completely, with formati on of smaller fragments that at the end of the process will pinch from the cell, forming bo dies that will be eliminated. 

_Molecular machinery of apoptosis_ 

The main effector proteins involved in apoptosis are: 

- **regulators,** which promote or suppress apoptosis. 

- **adaptors,** which internet with both regulators and effectors in response to specific signals. 

- **effectors,** which are enzymes that execute apoptosis. 

Effector proteins responsible to carry out the apoptotic program are called **caspases** (Cysteine ASPartate Specific proteASES), and are able to recognize and cut specific target sequences, thus being responsible for all the morphological changes, including fragmentation ofnuclear DNA, fragmentation ofthe nucleus itself (as they target nuclear lamins, proteins of the cytoskeleton responsible for nuclear shape), membrane blebbing (as they target cytoskeletal protein), and cell fragmentation. 

Caspases are always present in the cytoplasm as **zymogens** (inactive precursor enzymes), and this shows that the cell is always ready to activate the apoptotic process when needed. These pro-caspases must be activated through **proteolytic cleavage,** and they need to be bound to oligomerize in order to be functional. The **initiator caspase,** activated by several different stimuli, activates other **executioner caspases,** which carry out all morphological changes; there are many different targets for executioner caspases. 



136 

These caspases, by acting on almost a hundred targets in the cell, manage to destroy different features of cell activity, leading to cell death. 

### _The intrinsic pathway_ 

Apoptosis can have **extrinsic, intrinsic,** and **granzyme B pathways** (the latter is used by immune cells). Several stimuli, either coming from inside or outside the cell, irreversibly activate caspases: when this activation happens, the apoptotic program must start. 

The **intrinsic pathway** is regulated by a family of proteins called **Bc12;** some of them have an **antiapoptotic effect** (inhibit apoptosis) and others have a **proapoptotic effect.** Indeed, in the cell there is a continuous balance between these proteins, and whenever this equilibrium hangs in one direction, there is either the survival of the cell or the initiation of the death program. Growth factors lead to the activation of Akt ( a serine-threonine kinase ), which phosphorylates the BAD protein, thus causing the release of Bcl2, which becomes active. In this way, a growth signal induces cell survival, while its absence is an apoptotic signal. 

Intrinsic apoptosis is initiated by a variety of microenvironmental perturbations, including: 

- **growth factor withdrawal,** when the cell is not further needed by the tissue. 

- **DNA damage.** 

- **ER stress,** such as the stress given by protein folding. 

- **reactive oxygen species** (ROS) **overload,** which induces molecules oxidation. 

- **replication stress.** 

- **mitotic defects.** 

The criticai step is irreversible, and consists in **widespread mitochondrial outer membrane permeabilization** (MOMP), which is controlled by the Bc12 protein family; once it takes piace, it will lead to caspase activation without any further control. MOMP is mediated by **BAX** and **BAK,** two proapoptotic proteins of the Bc12 family, with the formation of **pores** across the outer mitochondrial membrane. BAX and BAK usually reside at the level of the mitochondrial outer membrane, can be activated by specific perturbations, and operate as cellular transducers of stress signaling. Once activated, they can form dimers, which lead to the formation ofbigger assemblies that insert into the mitochondrial outer membrane, forming big pores. The presence of these pores on the surface allows the diffusion of mitochondrial protein into the cytoplasm. In particular, the most important factor involved is **cytochrome C,** which is usually localized in the intermembrane space between the outer and the inner mitochondrial membrane. When these pores form on the mitochondrial surface, cytochrome C can diffuse into the cytoplasm, and it is very important for the apoptotic program because it will assemble with adaptor proteins (in particular with **Apaf 1** ), which are activated to form a macromolecular structure called **apoptosome.** The apoptosome is responsible for the recruitment and the activation of the initiator caspase ofthe intrinsic pathway, which is **procaspase-9,** followed by the activation ofthe other effector caspases. 

_The extrinsic pathway_ 

The **extrinsic pathway** depends on surface death receptors and the formation of the **DISC complex.** Depending on the interaction between specific receptors localized on the cell membrane with appropriate ligands, cells have on their surface what are called **"death receptors".** These receptors can be activated 

137 

when they bind to specific signals **(Fas, TNF),** which lead to the formation of a DISC complex **(death inducing signaling complex),** to the activation of initiator caspases and then of effector caspases. 

Many cells display on their surface a particular receptor that is called Fas ( death receptor). Fas is activated by a membrane-bound protein called Fas-ligand, present on the surface of specialized immune cells that are **killer lymphocytes.** When a cell is recognized as dangerous and has to die (a cell infected by a virus, for example ), there is an interaction between the Fas death receptor and the Fas-ligand, which triggers the activation of a signaling complex comprising a series of adaptor proteins and also **caspases 8 or 10.** When recruited to this activation complex, they are activated and can, in turn, activate executioner caspases, leading to a caspase-cascade and ultimately to apoptosis. The extrinsic pathway can be used to eliminate virus-infected cells, and is thus exploited by killer T-cells, which are specific types of T lymphocytes that can identify virus-infected cells and kill them in this way. 

Extrinsic and intrinsic pathways are a way through which the cell can activate apoptosis, and are very different, since the intrinsic pathway is mediated by stress and causes the mitochondrial release of cytochrome C and the formation of the apoptosome. Instead, the extrinsic pathway relies on the interaction of the Fas receptor with Fas-ligand, carried out by cytotoxic cells. Anyhow, these pathways must not be regarded as independent, because in the extrinsic pathway there a crossing with the intrinsic one through caspase 8-mediated proteolysis of a specific signaling protein. Indeed, caspase 8 can cut this molecule, which belongs to the Bcl2 large family, that in tum can promote the release of mitochondrial cytochrome C and the assembly of the apoptosome. 

### _The granzyme B pathway_ 

Another way to kill infected cells would be to use cytotoxic granules through the **granzyme B pathway.** Indeed, immune system cells can use also another pathway to kill cells, mediated by the granzyme B: some cells can inject the granzyme B into target cells and granzyme B, through a different pathway, can activate caspase, thus activating the apoptotic program. The granzyme B pathway involves the delivery of granzyme B into the target cell through specialized granules released by cytotoxic T lymphocytes or natural killer cells. These granules contain several proteases, as well as a performing protein **(perforin)** that mediates the entry of the granzyme. This protease can directly activate the effector cascades and therefore activate apoptosis within the cell. 

138 

### **Oncogenesis and cancer** 

**Cancer** is a disease that is widespread in people of all nationalities and is the second leading causes of death in western countries, just after heart disease. 

### _Cancer and age_ 

It appears that, as the population ages, the probability of developing cancer becomes greater, and the mean age of cancer onset is 66 years old. Generally speaking, when people are young, the probability is low, and then it becomes higher. There are some types of cancers that are more diffused in certain age groups: **neuroblastoma,** for example, affects mainly children, as well as **bone cancer** and **leukemia.** With more mutations in the cells, there is a higher probability of hitting key genes, leading to enhanced probability of developing cancer. After a certain age, the diagnosis becomes less precise and elderly people are less inclined to go to the doctor and get diagnosed. On the other hand, elders cannot undergo invasive treatments, and, by considering biology or genetics, when cells become older, the biologica! pathways become slower, leading to a lower proliferation and cellular activity and consequently to a decrease in this phenotype. Moreover, there might also be a sort of selection at this point: those who were predisposed to the development of cancer (because of genetics or environment) already had it. 

### _Cancer incidence_ 

Formales the most abundant canceris **prostate cancer,** while for females is **breast cancer.** At the second place, there is **lung** and **bronchus cancer,** followed by **colon** and **rectum cancer.** Cancer can proliferate thanks to genetic mutations but it can also be caused by environmental factors. 

Cancer can have **genetic causes,** meaning that, as an organism ages, cells undergo aging as well, with a higher probability of accumulating **mutations.** When talking about cancers, **environmental factors** (smoking, alcohol, viruses) must be considered as well. Prostate cancer is common in Europe, America and Africa, but by looking at Asia, there is a higher incidence of lung cancer that can be connected to the smoking habits of the population. In females, breast cancer can be considered the main type of cancer worldwide, but there are some areas where there is a higher rate of **cervix** and **liver cancers,** which can be connected with the **papilloma virus** (cervix and uterus), HIV or HCV (liver). 

### _Cancer mortality_ 

At the first place in mortality there is the **lung cancer** for both genders, since therapies for prostate and breast cancers are more efficient than the lungs ones. Indeed, lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer death worldwide. However, therapies, together with prevention and better education of people in terms of telling what the risk factors are and aiming at avoiding them, managed to lead to an earlier diagnosis and better treatment. There are some types of cancer that are particularly encouraging in terms of survival rates. Technical advances enable to perform an **early detection** of tumors, especially for some kinds of tumors such as breast cancer and prostate cancer. Moreover, advances in therapeutic options thanks to research play an important role. Research on different types of cancer at the molecular level allows to understand more specifically the pathways that are altered in specific cancers, including their molecular signatures. 

139 

## _Environmental factors_ 

Part of the prevention is also trying to avoid environmental factors that are known to be associated with cancer. **Tobacco** and **smoking** that are very important risk factors, especially for lung cancer but not limited to this type of cancer. 

Moreover, **alcohol consumption** is associated with liver cancer, and there are some chemicals that are involved in the transformation of cells and development of cancer (like **asbestos,** in the development of lung cancer). Some lights - such as **UV radiation** - are responsible for modification in the DNA, thus the surface that is most exposed (skin) is prone to an increase the risk of developing **melanoma.** 

lt is possible to recognize different kinds of environmental factors, some of which are connected with lifestyle - tobacco, smoking, diet not enriched in fruit and vegetables, sedentary lifestyle. Another important class of environmental factors is represented by **infectious agents,** like viruses or bacteria: for example, the human papilloma virus is responsible for a higher risk of developing cervical cancer, and hepatitis virus (B, but especially C) is a risk factor for developing liver cancer. Helicobacter pylori, instead, is associated with stomach cancer. 

However, even if these factors can increase the risk of introducing mutations in our DNA, mutations can arise in any case, so avoiding these environmental factors is a way to reduce the risk, not to eliminate it. 

## _Detection_ 

Nowadays, there are several tools specific for different kinds of cancer that enable to perform an early detection. For example, in case of breast cancer, **mammography** is performed in the population after a certain age; there is **colonoscopy** for colorectal cancer, screening of **PSA levels** of prostate-specific antigens performed through blood testing to detect prostate cancer. Cervical cancer can be screened through **PAP test,** while **dermatoscopy** is used to detect skin cancer and melanoma. 

## _Cancer as a "genetic disease"_ 

Cancer arises owing to **mutations,** mainly **somatic** (acquired), but also inherited or familiar. Somatic mutations are mutations that hit only one or few cells in an adult, and cancer can be described as a genetic disease. However, one mutation is not sufficient to trigger cancer development: indeed, there must be many different types of mutations in order to develop cancer cells, and must be localized in genes that are important for cell growth and cell survival. A mutated cell will start to proliferate until another cell acquires another mutation that leads to other properties; when cells start to proliferate even more, they become **invasive.** 

Cancer is a **genetic disease** in the sense that mutations are developed: indeed, modifications in the DNA code arise, mainly at the somatic level, so at the level of the adult differentiated cells. Cancer can be defined as a disease of **cell misbehavior,** in the sense that cancerous cells, that should be inserted in a tissue, do not respect the social rules anymore. Indeed, cells have to proliferate only when needed, and when they are no longer needed in a tissue, they should activate the apoptosis pathway. Most importantly, cells should be resident in a specific location within the tissue; instead, what happens in cancer cells is that these kinds of cells do not apply these social rules anymore, thus they proliferate without control, 

140 

invade the nearby tissue, and are not regulated by usual signals anymore, resulting in the complete destruction ofthe tissue. 

### _Cancer celi properties_ 

Cancer cells are characterized by **uncontrolled proliferation** and **colonization** of other **territories.** Cancer is a disease that develops in **stages:** cells little by little acquire new mutations, that can lead to the loss of some checkpoints. As the cell collects more and more mutations, it becomes more aggressive. **Hyperplasia** means that cells proliferate more, and this kind of situation is still benign because they behave normally, without disrupting the tissue. A second step is **dysplasia,** which means that the cells start to acquire a diff erent appearance, and this begins to be more dangerous. The step after is the development of an **_in situ_ cancer,** where the structure and architecture of the tissue are starting to be damaged. Finally, cancer cells acquire the ability to migrate by disrupting the basal lamina and getting access to the blood vessels. Then, cancer cells can spread to other organs and they can start to grow secondary tumors, called **metastasis,** depending on the type of mutati on that the cells acquire. 

Two types of cancer exist: 

- **solid tumors,** like lung cancer, where it is possible to observe a mass of cells that grows in an uncontrolled way within a specific, solid tissue. 

- **hematological tumors,** which regard blood cells (like leukemia). 

### _Cancer stages_ 

Cancer develops in stages, in a process that can also last many years, since it implies that the cell gains mutations in key genes that allow it to acquire more features and properties. At some point, a normal cell is hit by a genetic mutation in an important gene, like a gene that controls proliferation. At this point, there is a **hyperplasia** situation, where the cell number is increased, so, as result, the cell starts to proliferate much more than it should. Hyperplasia, however, is not cancer yet, since, apart from this increased proliferation, the structure of the tissue is maintained and is still normal. Then, it may happen that other cells acquire other mutations, so their proliferation becomes more active, and increases the probability that at some point there will be the introduction of a mutation in another point in the DNA. Despite DNA replication being a high fidelity process, DNA polymerase is not perfect, and it sometimes introduces mutations, even though the cell has some other mechanisms to cape with this - repair mechanisms that should be able to spot mutations. These new mutations could hit another important gene and, apart from causing a hyperproliferation, now these cells can also invade the nearby tissue and cause its reorganization, **destructing** the **normai architecture.** Indeed, in this case, more mutations hit, and it is possible to notice continuous destructive changes to the local tissues, where cells that carry all these mutations are particularly aggressive towards others nearby. 

Among these cells, some of them will acquire the ability to recruit blood vessels, and this is very important for the turnar, since cancerous cells need to be supplied with nutrients, which are brought by vessels. Therefore, cancer cells manage to survive and be successful in their invasion if they manage to induce **angiogenesis** - the development of new vessels that supply nutrients to the cancer. 

Moreover, blood vessels can be used by cancer cells for their dissemination in the organism; indeed, when some cancer clones have access to blood vessels, they can acquire the ability to detach from the 

141 

big mass and to disseminate into the body through the process **ofmetastasis.** These cells will be brought to other organs where they can start to grow again, and at this point, they will have a new tissue, a lot of nutrients, and no problems in terms of space, so they will proliferate, in order to be able to invade these new tissues efficiently. Cells that migrate and move to other districts form a metastasis, which is a secondary tumor that grows at a distant site from the primary one. 

Cancer develops in stages and it is important for it to be successful to gain genetic mutations in different genes. The success is guided by the acquisition of mutations in key genes that are involved in regulation of proliferation and apoptosis. 

Most of the times, cancer is soma tic, meaning that it arises within cells in the adult tissues, and not in the germline. Cancer requires an **accumulation** of **many mutations** over time: indeed, a single mutation is not sufficient to change a normai cell into a cancer cell, and the number of mutations that is necessary to induce the cancer transformation can vary from a handful to hundreds or thousands. Along the way, acquiring mutations becomes easier for cancer cells because, for example, there can be a first mutation that increases the proliferation rate. Thus, cells proliferate more, causing an increase in the probability of introducing new mutations in the DNA. Along the way, there may be another mutation that hits some gene that is important for error recognition in DNA replication (p53 is able to recognize mutations in the DNA). When these guardians are knocked out, then the genome becomes **unstable,** meaning that even though mutations are introduced, there is no active mechanism able to target these mutations and recognize them, trying to induce the cell not to replicate anymore. These mutations that are acquired along the way by the cancer cells can be responsible for the transformation ( **driver mutations** ), but there will be also other mutations **(passenger mutations)** that are acquired by the cell but that do not give any advantage in the transformation process. 

A cancer mass is a **heterogeneous complex** of cells: not all the cells that compose the mass are equal, and this is due to the fact that it is a process characterized by stages. Indeed, cancer is characterized by heterogeneous populations, meaning that a mass can be formed by different clones of cells, so different cells carry different mutations. From the cancer perspective, this is very advantageous because a mixture of cells that carry different mutations increases the probability that, at some point, some of these cells will hit another advantageous mutation that will help the mass gain specific features. Depending on the conditions, there will be some clones that will be able to expand and to prevail within the mass; others, instead, will acquire even some negative mutations for their evolution, which will lead them to death. From a molecular point of view, each cancer is unique because each cancer within every individuai isbe characterized by the acquisition of different types of mutations in different genes. Among this plethora of mutations, it is fundamental to distinguish the driver ones, which are important for the development of a therapy. 

### _Cancer therapy_ 

## Different **cancer therapies** already exist, including: 

- **surgery,** which can be used to remove the mass of cancer cells. 

- **radiotherapy** or **chemotherapy,** which use physical or chemical options that have the aim of damaging DNA and killing cancer cells. 

142 

- **anti-cancer drugs,** which hit different kinds of pathways that are essential for cancer development. 

Treatments like chemotherapy and radiotherapy can be localized in a specific area, but they will aff ect all the cells of the body or the area, whereas the knowledge of the molecular events at the basis of cancer can lead to a specific treatment. An example is **herceptin,** which is a drug developed in 1998: it consists of a monoclonal antibody called **trastuzumab,** which binds to specific receptors that breast cancer cells have on their surface. 

**Gleevec (imatinib** ), instead, is an inhibitor of a particular kind of kinase and is used to treat **chronic myelogenous leukemia** (CML), a rare blood cancer. CML is characterized by a translocation that occur between chromosome 9 and chromosome 22, and the chromosome that is formed by their fusion is called **chromosome Philadelphia,** after the city in which it was first discovered. The translocation occurs always in the same hotspot, involving two genes **(ber** and **ahi),** resulting in the formation of a fusion protein, called bcr-abl, which is a kinase that is not able to be properly regulated. This kinase remains active for a very long time, not being able to be switched off, and this causes the cell to proliferate at a very high abnormal rate (indeed, individuals affected by CML have a very high rate of lymphocytes in the blood). Since the fusion protein is a kinase, it has a peculiar active site, able to recognize a substrate and phosphorylate it; however, Gleevec binds to the active site and inhibits the kinetic activity of the fusion protein, which is inactivated and cannot reach the natural substrate. 

### _Hallmarks of cancer_ 

**Hallmarks** of cancer are all the features and characteristics that cancer cells must acquire in order to be successful in their proliferation and invasion: 

- **celi sufficiency in growth signals,** which is the ability of growing without receiving an extemal signal. 

- **insensitivity to anti-growth signals.** 

- **limitless replicative potential.** 

- **evasion of apoptosis.** 

- **angiogenesis ability.** 

- **tissue invasion** and **metastasis.** 

##### **CELL SUFFICIENCY IN GROWTH SIGNALS** 

Commitment to cell division is strictly regulated and dependent on stimulatory growth signals from other cells, and the receival of growth signals is important to activate the survival of the cell. Cancer cells, instead, acquire self-sufficiency in growth signals by: 

- producing their own signal molecules, thus performing **autocrine communication.** 

- **deregulating receptors** localized on the cell membrane, namely increasing their number. 

- activating some signaling pathway through mutations in key regulatory proteins. 

143 

In particular, growth factors, are recognized by **receptor tyrosine kinases** (RTK), which activates a series of intracellular cascades that culminate in the activation of the **Cdk complex,** able to phosphorylate the **retinoblastoma protein.** The role of the retinoblastoma protein is normally to sequester transcription factors, but, when it is phosphorylated, the transcription factors are able to bind the promoter of many genes involved in the proliferation program. Thus, the binding of the transcription factor on the genes of the DNA activates transcription, which becomes increased, inducing celi proliferation. 

Cancer cells are able to achieve this by: 

- **over-expressing** the **receptor for tyrosine kinases** (RTKs); in this way, cancer cells have a very high number of receptors compared to the normai cells. 

- **constitutively activating** the signaling pathway through different ways: the cell could acquire some kind of mutations that make it independent in the signal transduction pathway. An example is represented by mutations that hit the receptor for tyrosine kinases (RTK), inactivating it, so that its extracellular domain is lacking and only transmembrane and cytosolic domains are present. Moreover, this kind of mutated protein is completely inactive and cannot take anymore any extracellular signaling, so it becomes constitutively active, meaning that it is able to function and to induce and activate the transduction cascade even in absence of an extracellular stimulus. In this way, RTKs are **continually active,** telling the cell to grow and proliferate. 

- targeting other elements of the transduction cascade, like **RAS proteins,** which are part of the signaling pathway that is able to amplify the signal by targeting specific proteins downwards. Hence, mutations at the level ofthe RAS protein lead to a RAS protein that is always in its active state (always bound to GTP). As a consequence, no signal molecule or activation ofthe pathway are needed, since the mechanism is always active. 

##### **INSENSITIVITY TO ANTI-AGING SIGNALS** 

Within normai cells, there are usually **anti-proliferative signals,** which maintain the right homeostasis, are received always by cell membrane receptors, and stop proliferation. 

Many of these anti-proliferative signals are mediated by the **retinoblastoma pathway,** and pass thought modifications of the retinoblastoma (RB) protein, which sequesters the transcription factors that will activate genes inducing proliferation. The active and inactive state of the retinoblastoma protein are regulated by **phosphorylation** (inactivation) and **dephosphorylation** (activation). 

As a way to escape this anti-growth signals, cancer cells can disrupt the pathway of RB proteins, by inducing a mutation that **inactivates** the **RB protein,** so that it will not be able to sequester the transcription factor anymore, while instead binding the promoter and triggering transcription and so proliferati on. 

For example, tumor cells lose responsiveness to the **TGF-f}** antigrowth signal, which is normally recognized by specific receptors, thus activating a series of events that block cell proliferation. Cancer cells can lose this responsiveness by downregulating the receptors, which will not sense the presence of the TGF-~ and thus will not activate the signaling. Altematively, mutant receptors that may be able to bind to TGF-~ but are unable to transduce the signal, thus reaching the same objective. Moreover, there can be mutations on signaling molecules or on RB proteins. 

144 

##### **Loss OF CONTACT INHIBITION** 

Another antigrowth signal would be the sensing ofthe presence of other cells nearby, so that a cell stops proliferating when a certain **density of cells** is achieved, thanks to **cell-to-cell adhesion.** However, in cancer cells this signaling does not work anymore, so a very high cell density is no more a signal to stop proliferation, which instead continues. 

##### **EVADING APOPTOSIS** 

**Apoptosis** is the active elimination of a cell, and is a very important pathway that is activated when cells start to misbehave (in case of damage or high number of cells). In order for cancer cells to grow, an evasion from apoptosis must be reached. In particular, most of the times, cancer cells acquire the ability to evade apoptosis by disabling control mechanisms. In many types of cancers, indeed, there is a mutation in the gene that codes for the **protein p53,** which is usually activated by damages to the DNA, and which in turn can activate proapoptotic factors. Ifthe cell damage cannot be fixed, p53 can be activated and can lead to cell cycle arrest and apoptosis. Cancer cells have to disable this control, so by knocking out p53, there will not be a "guardian" for the genome and, even it gets damaged, the cell is not able to induce cycle arrest and apoptosis. As a consequence, despite the presence of a mutation or of serious DNA damages, the cell is not able to activate the suicide program. 

Another way to escape apoptosis is to **hyperactivate pro-survival** ( or antiapoptotic) **signals;** indeed, in some kinds of tumors, such as leukemia and lymphomas, there is a translocation that causes the **overexpression ofBcl2** (pro-survival). 

##### **LIMITLESS REPLICATIVE POTENTIAL** 

**Limitless replicative potential** implies the ability to become **immortal.** Normally, cells that are able to proliferate cannot proliferate infinitely, since at some point they reach the replicative senescence. After this, cells exit from the replicative phase andare not able to grow and divide (undergo mitosis) anymore. This is due to the fact that when DNA is copi ed, owing to the specifics of the mechanism, the end parts of chromosomes **(telomeres)** are not replicated, and this leads to their shortening. Telomeres are repetitive sequences localized at the end of the linear chromosomes, and, at a certain point, it is not safe anymore to undergo replication because telomeres are becoming too short to allow an efficient duplicati on without any risk for the genetic materiai. Because of this, after a certain number of rounds of replication, cells enter senescence and do not replicate anymore. This is a problem in the view of cancer cells, since they have to proliferate without any control; therefore, telomeres shortening and entering into senescence can be a way to stop their growth, so almost all cancer cells acquire the ability to proliferate etemally by reactivating a particular enzyme called **telomerase.** 

Telomerase is an enzyme that enables to copy the ends ofthe chromosomes, and it is active only in some kinds of cells or in some stages of development, for example in stem cells, in germ-line cells, or in the embryo, where a lot of proliferation is required. Then, in adult cells, telomerase is inactivated; indeed, in normai somatic cells it is possible to observe a progressive shortening, until they reach the **Hayflick limit,** the point at which it is not safe anymore to undergo DNA replication without risk for the genetic materiai. At this point normai, cells enter the senescence program, but cancer cells are able to reactivate telomerase and therefore maintain a certain length in telomeres, thus proliferating without any control. Hence, telomerase activity is very high in almost all cancers, especially ifthey are invasive. 

145 

##### **ANGIOGENESIS ABILITY** 

In order to obtain this uncontrolled proliferation, cancer cells need to be fed, so they need **nutrients,** which are carried by blood vessels. Indeed, a very important characteristic that tumor cells acquire is the ability to induce **angiogenesis,** so to induce the formation of new blood vessels. In an adult organism, angiogenesis is restricted only to specific needs, for example when there are wounds that need to be healed, and a way to activate angiogenesis is to create new blood, an activity that normally is very strictly regulated. However, cancer cells are able to produce specific factors that induce the recruitment of endothelial cells, thanks to their ability to produce pro-angiogenic factors. Angiogenesis is a very tightly controlled mechanism, and there are molecules, called **pro-angiogenic** and **anti-angiogenic,** that have opposite effects. **VEGF,** for example, is a pro-angiogenic factor, therefore it induces proliferation and formation of new vessels, while **thrombospondin-1** has the opposite effect, shutting down the recruitment and the formation of new blood vessels. Cancer progression involves the **angiogenic switch,** which is the ability to produce pro-angiogenic factors in order to induce an unbalance between these two mechanisms, towards the pro-angiogenic feature. Not only blood vessels are important to bring nutrients and therefore to allow all cells within the mass to be reached by all the chemicals that they need to grow and survive, but they are also important for the spreading of cancer cells, because they represent the way through which cancer cells can reach other parts of the organism. 

##### **TISSUE INVASION** 

Normally, cells occupy a specific position within the tissue, andare able to sense the presence of other cells within the tissue. However, cancer cells acquire the ability to **lose** this **contact inhibition,** and therefore they are able to proliferate and grow, forming new layers of cells. Moreover, they acquire the ability to move out from the originai location, reaching the bloodstream or the lymphatic system; when they do so, they induce the formation of **metastasis,** with cancer cells that leave the primary tissue, reaching the bloodstream, and getting to some other location within the body, where they have more space and more nutrients to grow. 

If cancer cells are able to adapt to the new tissue and the new conditions, a metastasis will form, which is a secondary tumor somewhere else in the organism. 

The process of invasion and metastasis requires a series of steps/molecular changes: 

- **detachment** from other cells. 

- **migration.** 

- **breach ofbasal membrane/matrix.** 

- **intravasation** and **transport** in **blood/lymphatic** vessels. 

- **colonization** of a novel environment. 

### _Immune system surveillance_ 

Other two important hallmarks of cancer cells are the **alteration** of **cellular energetics** and **metabolism,** and the ability to **evade immune system attacks.** When cancer cells become so out of control, they start to produce strange proteins that coat their surface. The immune system should have the ability to target them, but these cells develop the ability to evade this defense mechanism, and are even able to stimulate immune system cells in a way that they are not able to attack them anymore. Cancer cells not only have 

146 

the ability to evolve, but they are also able to adapt to whatever happens in the organism. Another very important characteristic is that cancer cells acquire their ability to evade the immune system surveillance. Indeed, the immune system has the ability to recognize also potential cancer cells. Cancer cells acquire so many molecular changes that they can be recognized by cells of both the adaptive and the innate immune system. When cancer cells start to grow and proliferate without contro!, they tend to express on the surface of the cells particular antigens **(tumor antigens)** that are not usually found in normai cells. This would allow cancer cells to be targeted by the immune system: indeed, both immune system cells such as natural killers, or adaptive cells such as T lymphocytes would be able to target them and kill them thanks to their **cytotoxicity.** However, cancer cells want to survive and therefore they try to escape immune system surveillance, gaining passes through mutations that occur in the DNA. For example, if the immune system recognizes specific antigens that are expressed on cancer cells, then cancer cells become able to not express this antigen on their surface, leading to an escape form the immune system surveillance. 

Cancer cells not only can evade the immune system in many ways, but they can also paralyze the cytotoxic components of the immune system through the secretion of **immunosuppressive factors.** Moreover, the immune system can sometimes promote tumor growth by inducing an **inflammatory microenvironment,** where different kinds of factors are released, such as survival factors, proangiogenic factors, extracellular matrix-modifying enzymes. Thus, the immune system has a double face role: on one side, it tries to target cancer cells, but on the other side, it can promote inflammation and lead to the secretion of factors that create a microenvironment suitable for cancer cells growth. 

### _Warburg effect_ 

When oxygen is present, cell usually tend to perform oxidative respiration, in order to produce energy in the form of ATP, necessary to perform all cellular activities. Instead, only when oxygen is limited, cells undergo glycolysis to produce pyruvate and then lactate. However, it was observed that cancer cells tend to choose the **glycolytic pathway** also when oxygen is present, and this leads to an altered sugar metabolism, also called **Warburg effect.** The use of glycolysis also in the presence of oxygen is called **aerobic glycolysis,** and it is chosen by cancer cells probably because it allows them to produce many different molecules that are needed for proliferation, starting from pyruvate. 

### _Proto-oncogenes_ 

In some cases, there is recurrence of specific mutations at particular sites in the genome, and this indicates that these sites that are recurrently hit are likely to carry genes that favor neoplastic progression. These genes can be divided into two main classes, **proto-oncogenes** and **tumor suppressor genes.** Protooncogenes, when mutated, lead to a hyperactivation that promotes carcinogenesis, and are genes that are involved in cell proliferation and in the inhibition of apoptosis. 

Proto-oncogenes, when mutated, are transformed into **oncogenes,** and they promote carcinogenesis. Tumor suppressor genes, instead, have an opposite function: indeed, they inhibit cell proliferation and promote apoptosis. Whenever mutations hit these genes, the result is that the just cited activities do not work anymore, and there is a promotion of carcinogenesis. In a normai cell, there are a pair of chromosomes for each gene, with one allele inherited from the mother and one inherited from the father. 

147 

When a mutation hits a proto-oncogene, it causes oncogenes to be abnormally activated. Mutated protooncogenes are called **gain-of-function,** since they become **hyperactivated,** and work even when they should not. These mutations lead to an **increase** in **protein expression** and **protein activity,** orto an **uncontrolled protein activation.** From a genetica! point of view, they are **dominant,** meaning that a mutation in a single allele is sufficient to promote carcinogenesis. For example, RAS, involved in the signal transduction pathway, is elicited by growth factors: if RAS is mutated with a mutation that activates it even without any stimulation, the consequence is that the transduction pathway is activated also when no growth factors are present. This is a kind of gain-of-function mutation, because it gives to the RAS protein the ability to be always active. In this way, a single mutation within just one of the two alleles that is able to produce this aberrant RAS protein is very detrimental for the cell, because there will be a lot of proteins that are produced that are able to induce the activation of the signal transduction pathway without any stimulation. 

Another example concems **gene amplification.** In this case, rather than a point mutation in the DNA, there is the duplication of specific loci that contain genes important in proliferation, such as **Myc,** a gene involved in cell growth. If the gene encoding for Myc is duplicated in the genome, it is possible to observe the production of a normal protein, but at a level that is much higher than the normal one. Finally, a third mechanism can be a **chromosome rearrangement:** cancer cells are characterized by genome instability, which means that the genome is more susceptible not only to point mutations, but also to chromosomal rearrangements. A very dangerous type of chromosome rearrangement is **translocation,** when the parts of two chromosomes fuse together, forming a new chromosome, as occurs in the case of chromosome Philadelphia. The fusion hits two genes in particular, bcr/abl, and to the production of an aberrant fusion protein, a kinase that is always active. 

Moreover, it can happen that a mutation or rearrangement in the nearby promoter region of the gene coding for Bc12 causes the promoter region to be substituted by some other promoters of genes that are expressed at a very high level, causing the production of the protein at a level that is much higher than the normal one. Different mechanisms can participate to the transformation of a proto-oncogene into an oncogene. Independently ofthe mechanism that is followed in cancer cells, the outcome is the activation of a protein or the expression at very high levels of a protein that is involved in proliferation or in the apoptosis process. 

_Tumor suppressor genes_ 

**Tumor suppressor genes** are genes that encode for proteins involved in the regulation of cell proliferation, so they usually inhibit proliferation and elicit apoptosis. These genes can be defined as guardians ofthe cells, because they try to activate those pathways that keep the cell safe and avoid hyperproliferation and uncontrolled proliferation. 

Turnar suppressor genes are present in two copies, one carried by each chromosome. In this case, it is necessary to mutate both copies of the turnar suppressor gene in arder to inactivate its function. When the first mutation hits the gene on one chromosome, the gene localized there will be inactivated. However, the second gene localized on the other chromosome will be able in any case to work. Then, ifa second mutation also hits the second gene, the result is that the turnar suppressor gene will be completely inactivated. Thus, starting from the information encoded in this gene, a protein will not be produced anymore, and since these genes are involved in the inhibition of proliferati on, if they do not work, the 

148 

effect will be an excessive cell survival and growth. Mutations that hit tumor suppressor genes result in the loss of gene activity, and are called **loss-of-function,** since when they hit the gene, it is not able to produce a functional protein anymore. From a genetica! point of view, these mutations are **recessive** because a mutation in both alleles is required to promote carcinogenesis. 

**Retinoblastoma** is a very rare type of childhood cancer that hits the eye. lt is recurrent in some families, but it can also be sporadic, so it can also appear without a familiar inheritance pattern. The gene that is mutated in this type of cancer is the Rb gene, which is a tumor suppressor gene encoding for a protein involved in the cell cycle progression and proliferation. One chromosome carries a mutated form of the Rb gene, but a second hit on the wild-type of chromosome can occur, and when both copies of the Rb gene have been inactivated, the effect is that the Rb protein is not able to be produced anymore, and therefore will lack control on the cell cycle regulation. This leads to excessive cell proliferation and thus to retino blastoma. The first mutation is inherited, and it confers susceptibility to tumors, while the second mutation occurs randomly in the somatic cell, and it is the one causing cancer to develop. 

There can be different mechanisms to inactivate the second, still working gene. For example, due to a very high genome instability it is possible to completely **lose** the **chromosome,** or to lose the region of the chromosome that carries this second copy, resulting in a cell that carries just the mutated tumor suppressor gene. Altematively, there can be **another point mutation** that hits the second chromosome, resulting in a cell carrying two mutated tumor suppressor genes. Finally, there may be an epigenetic activation ofthe genetic mechanism that can **silence** the expression of this tumor suppressor gene. 

Once the genome of the cell becomes mutated and unstable, it is easier to get all these other pathways to be deregulated. 

149 

### **Viruses** 

**Viruses** can be environmental factors in the development of cancer, but are not seen as living organisms; they are ubiquitous, and able to infect all living organisms. Moreover, humans carry in their genome, as part of their own genetic materiai, a part of virai genome, since there are particular kinds of viruses that are able to integrate their genetic materiai into the host cell genome. 

**Bacteriophages** are able to infect bacteria, but our body can also be colonized by viruses, even in nonpathogenic conditions, and they constitute the **human virome.** Despite the vast majority of viruses that infect humans are pathogenic, the infection has little or no impact on the health of our organism. Indeed, the body is endowed with an immune system, which acts as a defense system, where specific cells of the immune adaptive system identify pathogens and fight them. Whenever there is an infection whatsoever (caused by bacteria or viruses), the immune system recognizes the invaders and targets them. Viruses that are able to infect cells have developed a way to infect specific cells in specific organs in specific orgamsms. 

Viruses are also pathogens that are able to infect human cells and cause particular diseases, like **HIV,** which is a virus that causes **AIDS,** estimated to have killed 25 million people. **Influenza viruses** have caused three major pandemics in the 20<sup>th</sup> century, including the Spanish flu, and the Asian & Hong Kong flu in the 1960s. Additionally, **hepatitis viruses** may produce chronic liver infection. 

Viruses have a high rate of evolution, since they are able to adapt to a rapidly changing environment so as to be able to infect as many cells as possible. 

_Are viruses living organisms?_
