U sually, the sizes of viruses ranges from 20 nm to 700nm, and being so small, they can be visualized only by using electron microscopes. 

Viruses can be defined as **infectious obligate intracellular parasites,** because they are able to replicate only when they infect a target cell, by injecting their genetic materiai into it. In this way, they hijack the cellular machinery to build new virai particles, since they are not able to perform their own replication without infecting cells. 

Viruses are characterized by their genetic materiai, which can be either RNA or DNA, protected by a **protein coat.** Whenever they infect their host cell, viruses are able to **hijack** the machinery of the cell to perform the mechanisms necessary far their own replication and propagation, so they are able to produce their own mRNA, and to synthetize their own proteins, which will be encapsulated again into **virions** that will form other viruses able to exit the infected cell and infect other target cells. 

Because ofthese characteristics, in particular ofthe fact that they are not able to replicate by themselves, scientists tend to consider viruses as **non-living organisms,** but they somehow manage to propagate and infect cells. Apart from not being able to replicate autonomously, viruses are not able to perform any kind of metabolic reaction nor are they able to transport materials into and out of themselves, or to produce ATP. On, the other hand, viruses still have characteristics typical of living organisms: indeed, they contain a genome composed of nucleic acids, and are able to transfer their genome from one host cell to another. They are also subject to evolutionary pressure andare able to evolve; indeed, their genome 

150 

is subjected to very high selective pressure that makes them continuously change aver time. This last fact is very important, so that viruses can escape immune response by changing the proteins expressed on their surfaces. 

By considering the structure ofbacterial viruses, bacteriophages, it is possible to notice that they have a head that contains the genetic materiai, a neck, collar and sheath. The virus is able to adhere to the _E. Coli_ surface using the tail fibers, and to inject its genetic materiai directly into the host cell. Viruses are able to adhere to a target cell and recognize bacteria as the cells they have to infect, thus injecting inside them their genetic materiai, contained in the head. Once bacteriophages infect the target cell, they are able to replicate their own DNA to produce virai proteins, and this leads to the assembly of new virai particles, called **virions,** that will be then released from the cell, and therefore they will be able to attack other target organisms. Regarding bacteriophages, there are two categories according to the different life cycle they have: 

- **lytic phages,** able to perform the **lytic cycle.** 

- **tempered phages,** able to perform not only the lytic cycle but also the **lysogenic cycle.** 

### _Lytic cycle_ 

The **lytic cycle** is a more immediate type of infection that is carried out by lytic phages. A bacteriophage, able to recognize _E. Coli_ as a target (like the **T4 phage),** can adhere to the cell surface and then inject its DNA, the genetic materiai, into the cell. The DNA will be processed by the cellular machineries, becoming a double stranded linear DNA, and mRNA will be produced. With mRNA it is possible to produce proteins, therefore, after transcription, translation is carried out by cellular ribosomes. The final effect is the production of virai proteins. At the same time, virai proteins form virai particles, and virai DNA is replicated too. Once virai DNA and proteins are obtained, it is possible to perform the assembly of the new virai particles (virions), which will exit from the host cell by disrupting it. Indeed, in the lytic cycle, virions are freed from the host by **killing** the **bacterium** itself. 

### _Tempered phages_ 

**Tempered phages,** instead, are able to vary between the lytic and the lysogenic cycles. The starting point is the same: once the virus injects its genetic material into the host cell, the genetic material can undergo two different cycles. In the lytic phase, the phage (in this case the À phage, belonging to the tempered category) recognizes a particular bacterium as its target cell. lt can adhere to the surface and inject its genetic material into the cell; at this point, the phage's DNA, which initially was linear, is **circularized,** resembling the one of the host. Now the phage can go on performing the lytic cycle as before, thus transcribing DNA to produce mRNA, which gets translated into proteins, and the DNA gets copied multiple times to assembly and obtain new viral particles. Altematively, the viral DNA can be integrated in the DNA of the host cell: indeed, by a recombination process and by using specific **integrase** enzymes, the virus is able to induce the integration of its own genetic material into the bacterial chromosome. The phage is now called **prophage,** because it may remain "silent" within the host cells, but maintains its ability to give rise to new viral particles. The bacterium that integrates the prophage in its own genetic material is then able to go on with its life: it reproduces normally, grows, and undergoes many cellular divisions. 

151 

Occasionally, because of particular environmental conditions, the virai or phage DNA can be excised from the bacterial chromosome by another recombination event, and once this happens the virus enters into the lytic cycle again. F or example, particular environmental conditions (like the lack of nutrients) can be a trigger for the passage from the lysogenic to the lytic cycle. So, once the phage DNA is excised and forms its own circular genetic materiai, the lytic cycle starts forming virai particles, which are released by disrupting the host cell. 

### _Animai viruses_ 

When talking about animai viruses, the situation can be much more complicated. Indeed, animai viruses can be really varied in shape, organization and also in their content, but they share three main characteristics; 

- all ofthem possess **their own genome,** which can be either RNA or DNA. 

- all of them possess what is called a protein **capsid,** a structure made of proteins that encapsulates the genome and protects it. 

- some viruses also possess, apart from the capsid, an **envelope** made of lipids, that comes from the host cell. 

Differently from both prokaryotes and eukaryotes, in which the genetic materiai is carried by DNA molecules, in the case of viruses, RNA can be used as genetic materiai as well. The genetic materiai also can have different kinds of unusual structures: generally, organisms have DNA organized in double strands both in linear chromosomes (eukaryotes) or circular chromosomes (bacteria), while virai genomes are more heterogeneous. Indeed, there can either be **single stranded DNA** molecules, **linear** or **circolar;** moreover, double stranded DNA molecules can be covalently bound with terminal proteins or can form mixed structures between linear and circular. Apart from DNA, RNA molecules, either single stranded or double stranded, can be present as well. The structure of the virai genomes dictates also the mechanisms through which the virus will be replicated and through which it will hijack the molecular machineries of the cell. 

### _Virai genome_ 

Generally speaking, virai genomes are **small** and **simple,** and considering that virai particles are very small (their size is in the range of few nm), their genome must carry just the **essential genes** for genome replication and formation of the capsid. Indeed, genes present in the virai genome usually encode for three types of proteins: 

- **DNA polymerases** or, in case ofRNA genome, specific enzymes like **reverse transcriptase,** able to perform the opposite reaction with respect to what is stated by the dogma of molecular biology, thus synthesizing a DNA molecule from an RNA one. 

- **proteins** needed for **packaging** and **delivering** (structural proteins for the capsid). 

- **proteins** that **modify structure/function** of the host cell. 

The rabies virus, for example, only has five gene, with which it is able to colonize its host cell and hijack all its machineries. Indeed, it has to be able to produce proteins that convince the cell to work for the virus, and there are different strategies that viruses can use to convince the cell to transiate predominantly its own mRNA and to shut down translation and transcription of the host cell's genes. 

152 

### _Capsid and envelope_ 

An important roie is piayed by proteins that form the virai **capsid,** which is made of few subunits of proteins that are arranged in very reguiar arrays, most commoniy **helical or icosahedral symmetries** (the most energetically favorabie conformations). The capsid has di:fferent functions: 

- it **protects** the **genome.** 

- it **delivers** the **virai genome** to the host cell. 

- it **recognizes** and **packages new virai particles.** 

Some viruses aiso have an **envelope,** and based on its characteristic, it is possibie to distinguish them in two categories: 

- **non-enveloped (naked)** viruses, which do not possess the enveiope, andare oniy protected by the capsid. 

- **enveloped** viruses, where there are both the capsid and an extemai enveiope composed by a iipid biiayer derived from the membrane of the host cell, and presenting specific virai proteins on its surface. Whenever these viruses exit from the host animai cell, they carry with them a part ofthe membrane itseif. 

The enveiope is a portion of the host cell membrane, forming an extemai membrane iayer. It is a iipid biiayer derived from the host cell membrane after budding; proteins found on the enveiope are instead encoded by the virai genome, and the function of the enveiope is the attachment of the virus to the host cell. 

Viruses hijack the moiecuiar machinery and are abie to produce their capsid and virai transmembrane proteins, which will be iocaiized on the extemai part of the virus (but which are now on the cell membrane of the host cell). Then, when all the particles have been assembied and when the virions are ready to be reieased, the assembied virions, ioaded with the virai genome, and the capsid can internet with the virai transmembrane proteins and induce the formation of a **bud.** This bud will give rise to a new progeny, and the capsid will be included into the iipid biiayer enriched with virai protein. Phages were oniy abie to exit the host celi by disrupting its membrane, killing it; instead, animai enveioped viruses are abie to exit the host cell by budding, not necessariiy killing the cell. 

This budding process is important because it is reiated to how a virus propagates. Enveioped and naked viruses have di:ff erent ways of propagating: naked viruses do not have an enveiope and as a consequence they cause the **host celi lysis** and the host cell to **burst** in arder to get reieased (like papilloma virus, rotavirus, rhinovirus, and adenovirus ). These naked viruses are aiso more **environmentally stable** and more resistant to detergents and drying, as well as to gastric and biiiary acids. On the other hand, enveioped viruses carry this iipid enveiope and the capsid, allowing them to exit the host by budding (so without iysing it), but making them more susceptibie to specific environment conditions: indeed, they can get easiiy inactivated by detergents that destroy the iipid biiayer, by acids and heat. Some exampies of enveioped viruses include HIV or the influenza virus (HCV), which cannot survive the gastrointestinai tract, and are **environmentally labile.** 

153 

_Transmission and replication of viruses_ 

There are different types of transmission depending on the viruses considered: 

- **direct contact** (herpes virus). 

- **aerosols** ( common cold, measles virus). 

- **insect bites** ( dengue fever, yellow fever, Zika virus). 

- **contaminants** in **food** and **water** (hepatitis A). 

- **sexual transmission** (papillomavirus, HIV). 

- **blood** and **blood-containing materiai** (HIV, HBV, HCV). 

Viruses can replicate very fast, by quickly forming viral particles in the host cell. They replicate their different parts, including genome and capsid, and then assemble them. Their replication has nothing to do with the replication cycle of eukaryotes and prokaryotes, since one virus can give rise to thousands of progeny, which are very small and efficient. 

It is possible to recognize four steps in viral infections: 

- **attachment** and **entry.** First, the virus must recognize the host cell, with some proteins localized in the capsid and in the envelope mediating the attachment of the virus to the host cell. 

- **genome replication** and **synthesis ofviral proteins.** Once the virus is inside the cell, it releases the genome, which is replicated, transcribed and translated to synthesize the viral proteins. 

- **assembly.** Once the viral particles exit, they must be assembled. 

- **release.** Once the viral particles are ready, they can be released in different ways. 

_Vira! effect on cells_ 

The structure of a virus is strictly related to the mechanism that it uses to infect and then to exit from the cell once virion particles are produced. The viral particle attaches to the surface of a target cell and mediates its entry into it; then, there is the release of the genome, which moves from one side (undergoing replication and synthesis ofviral proteins) to the other side. The genome can be used to produce mRNA and then to obtain proteins, which at the end will assemble with viral proteins to form viral particles, which will be released from the cell. This is a general mechanism that is true for all the viruses, but specific steps may override depending on the type of virus and on the type of genome that the virus contains. Once the virus manages to infect the cell, it can have different effects on it: indeed, it can replicate forming many viral particles that can be released from the cell itself through a **lytic action,** killing the host cell in the process. However, an infection can be **chronic/persistent,** which is when the virus can persist for a long time inside the cell and produce viruses at very low levels. In this case, the immune system is not able to get rid of the virus, and this is exactly the example ofhepatitis B or HBV. 

In another situation, similar to that in bacteriophages, there is a **latent** or **proviral infection.** Indeed, the virus remains in the cell without actually acting, and can occasionally reactivate, remaining as a small circular product or becoming integrated in the genome of the host cell. This means that the genome of the virus can be integrated inside the genome of the host cell, and this situati on can remain **silent** for a really long time, sometimes even years; then, at a certain point, the virus can start to be reactivated and this will lead to the production of many viral parti cl es. This is the example of **HIV virus** (human immune 

154 

deficiency), but also of the **herpes virus** or the **varicella zoster.** The herpes virus, far example, gives cold sores, and once it infects the body it can remain latent, hiding in nervous ganglia, and sometimes, when the immune response is not behaving properly, activating again and starting to produce virai particles. HIV can remain latent and then give AIDS many years in the future, while the chicken pax virus gives a specific type of symptomatology in children, hiding in ganglia, but in adults it can reactivate, giving rise to **shingles** (usually when the immune system us depressed). 

Lastly, viruses can have a **transforming effect.** Virai infections represent a risk factor far the development of cancer; indeed, the infection by specific types of viruses is an environmental risk factor far cancer, as in the case of papilloma virus. Generally, some kinds of viruses are able to alter the growth properties of the cell by triggering the **oncogenesis process.** Viruses bind to specific receptors on the host cell surface, and gain entry into different types of cell, depending on the receptor they can recognize or interact with. 

### _Tropism_ 

**Tropism** can be defined as the affinity of a certain virus far a specific organism, a tissue or a cell type. All viruses that exist in nature are able to infect all organisms on Earth, but there are viruses that are specific far bacteria and animai cells, or viruses that can infect humans but not rabbits **(host tropism).** Moreover, among the same organisms, a virus may be able to target not all tissues or all organs, but just some specific ones **(tissue tropism):** the influenza virus, far example, normally infects lungs, but not neurons. Finally, viruses can also have specific preferences far some type of cell ( **cellular tropism):** an example is HIV, which normally infects immune cells but not neurons. 

It is also important to understand **cellular tropism,** where the type of receptor to which the virus binds determines which type of cells and organism will be infected and colonized. An example is provided the influenza A virus, which carries its genome inside, and whose capsid shows some specific proteins, which are able to recognize only specific receptors localized on the target cell membrane. Cellular tropism is given by the fact that different cell types present on their surface different receptors and therefare the virus is able to be absorbed and infect a cell type only if the cell presents these receptors. Influenza A virus, far example, binds to **sialic acid,** a small sugar that is attached to proteins and lipids on the cell surface of lung cells. The viral protein that recognizes the receptor is **hemagglutinin,** which is located on the virus envelope: once hemagglutinin interacts with sialic acid residues, it will mediate the entry of the virus into the cell and therefare its infection. 

Some viruses are able to adhere to target cells and to infect them thanks to the presence of one receptor, while others need more than one. In this case, there is a **primary receptor** and also a **co-receptor,** which enables the correct interaction between the virai particle and the cell membrane, and mediates the entry of the virai particle into the cell. A special case of these type ofthese viruses are **retroviruses,** which are enveloped RNA viruses. RSV (rous sarcome virus) and HIV belong in this category; during infection, the virai RNA is converted into double strand DNA by the virai protein **reverse transcriptase.** These viruses challenge the centrai dogma of flow of genetic infarmation because they undergo reverse transcription, obtaining DNA from RNA. 

An example is that ofthe HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), which needs a primary receptor (CD4), a molecule expressed on the membrane of macrophages and T cells, and a co-receptor (a chemokine 

155 

receptor, used by cells to mediate the chemokine signaling process and helping the entry of the HIV virus into the immune system cells ). These receptors are normally used by the cell to perfarm specific activities other than the coral infection: indeed, **CD4 and chemokine receptors** are involved in signal transduction pathways. However, the virus has evolved to be able to exploit the molecules localized on the cell membrane. 

### _Retrovirus es_ 

**Retroviruses** are a particular kind of viruses characterized by a genome consisting of **RNA molecules.** Once they infect the target cell, they are able to convert the RNA genome into a double strand DNA molecule by using a special enzyme called **reverse-transcriptase,** which they encode by themselves. Reverse-transcriptases are a very useful tool in molecular biology laboratories, because they enable to farm a molecule ofDNA starting from RNA. 

Retroviruses are able to target specific cells by exploiting interactions with specific receptors. Once the virus enters the cell, its genetic material and the content of capsid are released into the cytosol, where the reverse-transcriptase is able to obtain a double strand DNA molecule starting from the RNA genome of the virus. At this point, the double strand DNA can be permanently integrated into the host cell genome by a viral enzyme called **integrase,** able to cut the host DNA and insert the viral one. Then, the virus can remain silent far many years, integrated in the host DNA as a **provirus,** until, at a certain point, it gets activated, leading to the production of new viral particles, that are released by the cell and can infect other target cells. 

Understanding the infectious cycle of viruses is important to figure out an **antiviral therapy.** This is especially challenging far the HIV, far which there is no efficient vaccination nor a definitive treatment that is able to eradicate the viral infection. However, knowing all the life cycle of the virus and the proteins that the virus is able to synthesize enabled the researchers to target different steps in the viral particle maturation and to develop a therapy, called **HAART** (highly active anti-retroviral therapy), that is a combinati on of antiviral drugs able to target specific steps in the viral maturati on process and to slow down the progression of the disease. Far instance, drugs can target the activity of reverse transcriptase, thus inhibiting viral genome to be reversely transcribed into DNA; other drugs, instead, can target the integrase enzyme, hence inhibiting the integration of the viral genome into the host genome. Moreover, by using technologies such as CRISPR, scientists are trying to find genes that play a role in HIV infection and that could cure or eradicate the viruses. 

### _Coronaviruses_ 

**Coronaviridae** is a family of viruses that cause diseases in mammals and birds. Some of them are able to infect humans causing **respiratory infections,** ranging from the common cold to respiratory syndromes. They are named coronavirus after their shape, because they resemble the solar corona. Until the beginning the 21<sup>st</sup> century, researchers believed that coronaviruses could cause only mild symptoms in humans, but the outbreak of SARS in 2003 demonstrated that there can be members of this family responsible far much more severe diseases. Coronaviruses that infect humans generally cause only common cold, whereas other three viruses, like **MERS-Co V** ( middle east respiratory syndrome ), **SARSCo V** (severe acute respiratory syndrome) and **SARS-CoV-2** (COVID 19) cause much more severe syndromes. 

156 

These particular viruses are supposed to grow in nature within a reservoir host, like the **bat.** Then, they are able to infect humans thanks to the passage into an intermediate host with which humans come in contact and that causes the infection ofhuman cells. It is believed that the bat is the reservoir host for all the three viruses, while instead there are different intermediate hosts, including the **civet cat** for SARSCo V, the **carnei** for MERS-Co V, and the **pangolin** for SARS-Co V-2. 

The genome of these viruses is a **single strand RNA** molecule. In particular, on the surface, there are **SPIKE proteins,** involved in the recognition of receptors on target cells. These receptors are called **angiotensin converting enzyme 2** (ACE2), andare able to internet with the spike proteins; then, thanks to the interaction with other protases, viruses gain entry into the cell. The spike protein binds to ACE2 receptor of target cells, mainly lung cells, and this stimulates the cleavage of spike protein by proteases such as **PMPRS2,** thereby inducing the fusion of the virus membrane with the host one and the release of the viral content into the cell 's cytoplasm. Then, the virus is able to take control over the cell machinery and to command the production of new viral particles, hence leading to the infection of other cells. 

157 

### **Genome editing techniques** 

The **CRISPR-Cas9** system allows to perform modifications in the DNA of cells and organisms, an objective that researchers tried to pursue since the 1970s. Genomes of eukaryotic organisms, especially in human cells, are composed ofbillions of nucleotides ofbases. The ideato target specific bases and to introduce modifications is for sure appealing, because it could allow to correct mutations and to improve phenotypes and clinical symptoms of specific diseases. Different techniques have been developed in the course ofthe years, starting from the discovery of **restriction enzymes** in the 70s. 

### _Restriction enzymes_ 

Researchers in the late 70s carne across these particular enzymes, able to cut the DNA of organisms; by managing to cut the genome, this technology can be used to perform **knockouts** (which destroy the function of a gene) or **knockin** (with the genetic material introducing something exogenous into the DNA of the cell). 

**Restriction enzymes** represent the first step, since they can be used to cleave the DNA. Tue type of the cleavage is indicated as **"double strand break"** (DSB), meaning that they are able to cut both the strands of the DNA double helix. 

Breaking the genetic material can be very dangerous in the cell, because it can lead to a loss of genetic materiai orto breaking of genes, therefore interrupting the functionality of specific genes. Hence, the cell tries to correct this problem and stick together the two parts of chromosomes generated from the break as soon as possible. There are two main pathways that the cell can follow to solve the problem: 

- **non-homologous end joining,** in which the cell activates a panie mode and tries to stick together the two extremities, but by doing so it will **not** be so **precise.** What happens is that most of the time, it introduces **indel mutations,** either losing some information or inserting additional nucleotides. This kind of way usually leads to **knockouts,** so to a situation where the gene of interest that was cut in the first place does not work anymore. By introducing an insertion or perf orming a deletion, it is possible to change the reading frame of translation and to introduce a premature termination codon. 

- **homology-directed repair,** which instead is a much more **high fidelity technique,** since another piece of DNA is used to try to repair the damage. The genomic DNA and the extremities that have been recognized by specific proteins are able to bind to DNA molecules. By feeding the system with some exogeneous DNA that is partially homologous with the genomic DNA that was broken, it is possible stimulate the repair of the break in a more precise way, because at this point the exogenous DNA will work as a template for the repairing DNA molecule. By inducing the homology-directed repair and supplying the system with some kind of exogenous DNAmolecules that can act as a template, it is possible to introduce specific regions of exogenous DNA in the target region on the cell DNA. 

### _Meganucleases_ 

The first attempt was historically done with **meganucleases,** cutting endonuclease enzymes able to perform cleavages in the DNA double helix. They are able to do it precisely, because they recognize a target sequence that can be of different length, from 14 to 240 base pairs, and are able to cut the sequence, 

158 

thus inducing a double strand break. This could be a mechanism to a target specific gene regions; however, two main drawbacks are present: 

- these meganucleases are **naturally occurring molecules,** so bacteria able to recognize different sequences are needed. Thus, the probability to find a meganuclease that targets the region of interest is actually very low, meaning that only few sites of the genome can be targeted with this strategy. 

- the majority of double strand breaks are repaired through the **error-prone mechanism** of the **non-homologous end joining.** Even though exogenous DNA is provided to try to activate the high fidelity homology-directed repair, most of the time the non-homologous strain joining mechanism is activated, therefore inducing more danger, with deletions or random insertions at the cutting site, losing the objective of this modification. 

A solution that the scientists tried was to re-engineer the meganucleases to alter their DNA targeting specificities, but problems remained, and only a very small fraction of genomes could be specifically targeted using this method. 

The idea is to introduce double strand breaks in the genome and activate the homology-directed mechanism, so the high-fidelity repair mechanism. In this way, it would be possible to design an exogenous DNA piece, like a plasmid, which is a circular DNA molecule, quite small, that can be easily modified in the lab. Then, some recognition sequences for this specific meganuclease and the sequence encoding a specific gene of interest or the sequence to modify can be inserted in the vector. By finding a way to deliver this plasmid and the meganuclease in cells, the meganuclease would be able to cut the genomic DNA in a specific position and at the same time cut the vector (the plasmid) in the recognition site. Thanks to the partial homology sequence that was inserted on purpose within the vector, it is possible to stimulate the insertion ofthe gene of interest inside the genomic DNA ofthe cell. 

Meganucleases were anice first try that was important in showing that targeting the genome was feasible. However, meganucleases lacked a lot of important characteristics, like the **flexibility** to target any desired region in the DNA, so the next generation of genome editing tool was based on the use **ofzinc-finger.** 

_Zinc fingers_ 

**Zinc fingers** refer to specific domains that can be present in transcription factors, proteins able to bind the DNA and induce transcription. Zinc fingers are characterized by motifs that are able to bind the DNA in a sequence-specific manner. Each module recognizes a sequence of three nucleotides, so given this particular characteristic, each module with a certain target specificity can be taken and fused with others, in order to give rise to a big fusion protein characterized by many modules, each targeting a free base pair sequence. By finding the right modules and putting them together in a specific order, it is possible to target a lot of different sequences. However, in genome editing, targeting is not sufficient, since DNA must be cut. Hence, these modules can be fused with a nuclease **(Fok** I is the most commonly used) able to cut the DNA and generate a double strand break. 

One of the most important problems, not only for zinc fingers, but also for all tools in general, is that whenever a specific regi on must be targeted, there is a certain degree of probability of targeting also other locations in the genome ( **off-target effect).** The off-target effect can be caused by the fact that the human 

159 

genome is very big (with three billions ofnucleotides in a haploid genome), so the probability ofhaving a sequence that is similar to the one that is targeted can be pretty high. Hence, despite being precise, this mechanism is able to recognize non-perfectly matched sequences. 

However, Fok I (able to cut the DNA) works only in a **dimer form,** meaning that two subunits in dose proximity must be provided to the nuclease, so that it can work. This implies that for the nuclease to target these regions, two zinc fingers in dose proximity are required simultaneously. This has an important effect on the off-target, and therefore on the undesired cleavages around the genome, because Fok I is able to cleave only when both zinc fingers match the DNA around the target. Even though one of the zinc finger can recognize other locations on the genome, the probability that also the other zinc finger would be able to recognize another site in proximity is very low. 

This is a way to decrease off-targets, since two simultaneously targets are needed to have the activation of the nuclease activity; indeed, if only one recognizes the target, the enzyme does not cut. Thus, zinc fingers seem to be a next level compared with meganucleases, because they are more flexible, and can also have a very **high specificity** for the target regi on of interest. 

### _TALEs_ 

An evolution of this system, just before the advent of CRISPR technology, is represented by **TALEs.** TALEs are modified enzymes derived from a bacterium that is called _Xanthomonas,_ a pathogen that attacks plant cells, causing damages to crop and agriculture in generai. However, _Xanthomonas_ is also able to secrete "TALEs" **(transcription activator-like effectors),** able to enter the cytoplasm of plant cells and to induce modifications in the transcription of target genes, making cells more susceptible to infections. The important characteristic of those protein is that they have DNA binding domains, and in particular these domains are characterized by motifs composed of forty-four amino acids able to recognize a specific nucleotide. These domains are able to work with a much more **specificity** compared to zinc fingers, since they target the specific nucleotide, so this domain can be exploited to create fusion proteins where each domain will target a specific nucleotide, and the combination of domains could theoretically allow to target any sequence of interest in a more flexible way then with zinc fingers. Then, DNA must be cleaved, and there is the creation of fusion proteins that carry not only the DNA binding domain, but also the cleavage domain of the Fok I nuclease, similarly to what was seen for zinc fingers. 

However, some problems arose for this technology. Even though this mechanism was absolutely feasible to target any genomic region and to perform genome editing, researchers also worked to improve the genome editing efficacy. Yet, every time a specific sequence must be targeted, the **entire protein** must be **redesigned,** creating a new fusi on protein to carry the domain of interest, and then the nuclease domain of Fok I. This in **not cheap,** and managing and modifying proteins can be **technically difficult;** thus, even if the right combinati on of domains to target the regi on of interest is found, there is no certainty that the protein will be produced or that the design will work. This technical difficulty had the consequence that only some labs where able to adopt this technique and to work with it, but the large majority of the scientific community did not get involved in this genome editing tools. 

160 

### _CRJSPR_ 

The discovery of the function of **CRISPR** carne from the yogurt bacteria, and indeed it represents the bacterial immune system, activated whenever a bacterium has to face phages infection. CRISPR stands for **"Cluster-regularly interspaced-Short-Palindromic-Repeat DNA sequences";** this name derives from the genomic loci that correspond to the CRISPR system, since the CRISPR array, that is the one to which this name is referring to, is a piece of bacteria genome characterized by the presence of **repeats** and **spacers.** Repeats are DNA elements that are interrupted by specific DNA sequences that change each time, known spacers. Near the CRISPR array, within the genome, there are also **Cas genes,** called **"CRISPR associated genes",** encoding for proteins that are involved in the system. 

The first discovery of this repeated elements was done in 1987 in _E. Coli,_ but only in the first 2000s there was a discovery of the function of these repeated elements. The rnost irnportant elernent in the CRISPR structure is represented by **spacers,** which are a record of infection frorn which the host bacteriurn has recovered. Thus, whenever the bacteriurn encounters a phage, it is able to take parts of its genornic sequence and to put thern into its own bacterial genorne. The identity of the spacers is very polyrnorphic and depends on the bacteriurn type, and on the type of infection the bacteriurn has to face. Indeed, the bacteriurn is able to take a piece of virai DNA and to put it into its CRISPR array, in a step known as **adaptation;** then, in a second step called **CRISPR RNA biogenesis,** the CRISPR array is actively transcribed to give a srnall RNA, the **CRISPR RNA.** CRISPR RNA is used in the last step of the defense rnechanisrn **(CRISPR interference),** where this srnall RNA guides the protein apparatus cornposed of Cas proteins to the target, which is the virai genorne. 

The first step is adaptation, narnely the rnechanisrn through which the bacteriurn is able to steal a piece of the virai genorne and insert it into the CRISPR array. Within this process, a very irnportant role is played by the **Cas proteins** (Cas cornplex), able to identify specific regions in the virai genorne and to incorporate them within the bacterial genome. The region that is recognized on the virai DNA is called **protospacer,** and in particular an important role is played by a short sequence, called **Pam.** Parn stands for **"protospacer adjacent motif',** and is a short sequence (ranging frorn two to five nucleotides) recognized by the Cas proteins and specific to each CRISPR Cas subtype and bacteria. Cas proteins are not only ab le to perform Parn recognition and to cleave a srnall piece of the virai genorne, but also possess an **integrase** activity that allows thern to insert this short DNA rnolecule into the bacterial genorne. 

After adaptation, which consists in the recognition of virai DNA and in the insertion of a piece of it in the CRISPR array, there is a second step, the biogenesis of CRISPR RNA. Indeed, the CRISPR array is actively transcribed, and this leads to the production of a long precursor called pre-CRISPR RNA, which carries not only the spacer sequences, but also the repeat sequences. When the precursor is processed, it leads to the formation of shorter RNA rnolecules, each characterized by the presence of one spacer and a fragrnent ofthe repeat sequence. The repeat sequence is partially cornplernentary, and this results in the formation of a **"harpin structure",** where there is a loop at the top and srnall cornplernentary regions below. These CRISPR RNAs are very irnportant in the interference process, because they can cornplex with Cas proteins and can guide them to the target, that is recognized by **complementarity** between the spacer region and the virai DNA. 

This is another exarnple of a rnachinery that is cornposed by proteins and RNA parts, where the specific recognition of the target is rnediated by the RNA part of the cornplex. Thus, the CRISPR RNA, and in 

161 

particular its spacer regions, are the ones responsible for the recognition and the targeting of the virai DNA. Whenever this targeting takes piace, the Cas protein or other effector proteins in the celi are activated, and the virai DNA is cleaved. This workflow always holds true, but the exact mechanism of action - so the type of Cas activated or the activation of cellular nucleases - depends on the type of Cas system used. Indeed, there are **six different types** of **Cas** that have been discovered, and each of them is characterized by the presence of different eff ectors proteins, so each Cas has a signature that can work in different ways. 

To sum up, the CRISPR-Cas mechanism was originally discovered as bacterium immune system. Indeed, whenever the bacterium encounters a virus, it is able to steal a little bit of its genome and put it in its own bacterial genome, in the CRISPR array. Whenever a bacterium will encounter the same virus another time, it will already have a CRISPR RNA ready to target that virus. Therefore, this is like an adaptive immune system that allows the bacterium to maintain memory ofthis encounter, and therefore to be able to respond to the infection in a faster way. 

However, a question that could rise at this point is: how can the bacterium avoid self-immunity? How can the bacterium be able to distinguish between the virai DNA and its own DNA? In order to understand this, it is necessary to know that the spacer acquisition is **replication-dependent.** Imagine having a phage that is attacking the bacterium: the virus infects the bacterium machineries because its aim is to expand and produce a lot of copies of itself, in order to be able to attack other cells. Thus, DNA replication of viruses reaches a very high level after the infection, because every virion that will be produced- so every virai particle - needs to contain the virai DNA along with some proteins. Hence, the acquisition of the spacers of this immune system of the bacterium occurs during replication, because, since it occurs at a very high level, there can be the formation of **DNA breaks** within the replication fork. When a double strand DNA break occurs in bacteria, a protein is called **RecBCD** is able to localize and to bind to the extremities ofthe chromosome. Usually bacterial DNA is circular, so terminals of DNA are not usually exposed, since the molecule does not have any; thus, whenever double strand DNA breaks occur, there is the localization of this RecBCD protein that unwinds and degrades the DNA, until it reaches a **Chi site,** which is a sequence composed of eight nucleotides that is highly enriched in bacterial genome. This Chi site is a signal for the protein to stop the degradation of the DNA, and to recruit a complex that can repair the double strand DNA break. 

The bacterial genome is really enriched in these Chi sequences, but the virai genome is not, so whenever there is a double strand DNA break in the bacterial genome, there is a recognition of the site, the recruitment of this double strand DNA break repair complex, and the inhibition of the recognition of spacers by the CRISPR mechanism, which therefore spares the celi DNA. The Cas complex works with the RecBCD and tries to take up some degradation products that are produced by the Cas complex, but this is not possible because there are no Chi sequences that can recruit the repair mechanism in viruses. Virai DNA, indeed, is not enriched in these sequences, so whenever it is present, the RecBCD complex is able to bind its ends, degrade it, and activate the Cas complex, that is now able to take up a sequence and to insert it into the CRISPR array. Hence, the presence of the Chi sequences is the method through which the bacterium **avoids autoimmunity,** namely putting in its own genome within the CRISPR array sequences that could target itself. 

162 

### _CRJSPR-Cas9 in medicine_ 

There are six diff erent types of CRISPR, each characterized by a signature protein. The most common is the **CRISPR-Cas9** system, but an interesting alternative, especially when working at the RNA level, is represented by the **CRISPR-Cas13,** which can be used - since the target molecule is RNA- to perform a regulation of gene expression. 

Inside the bacterium there is a foreign DNA (a viral DNA, for instance), that was injected into the cell. The bacterium is able to steal a little bit of the foreign DNA and to put it into the CRISPR loci. For the CRISPR-Cas9 system the signature protein is represented by **Cas9,** but apart from Cas9 and the CRISPR array, an important role is played by the **transactivating crRNA,** or **tracrRNA.** Whenever the transcription of the CRISPR array is activated, also the transactivating crRNA is produced, and this is an important feature of the interference complex that will be formed. Cas9 needs to form a complex with both the CRISPR RNA (crRNA) and the transactivating RNA (tracrRNA). The crRNA has a complementary part that is able to target and bind to the tracrRNA, and for Cas9 to have its nuclease activity and to work as a molecular scissor, both crRNA and tracrRNA must form a complex with the Cas9. Only at this point the machinery will be able to target viral DNA and cleave it. 

CRISPR-Cas9 is nowadays the most widely used tool to perform **genome editing.** For CRISPR-Cas9 to work, Cas9, crRNA and tracrRNA (both transcribed when the CRISPR array is active) are needed. These two RNAs have a sequence of complementarity that is represented by the repeat system of the crRNA. However, it is possible to engineer these RNAs, and to provide the system with a synthetic RNA complementary to the genomic region that is targeted. This RNA is the same as the crRNA, and can be fused together with the tracrRNA. This system employs Cas9 and a longer RNA, called **guide RNA,** which carries the information both of crRNA and of tracrRNA, since these information are fused together within the same molecule. The advantage of this system is its **flexibility,** sin ce in order to target different genes, it is sufficient to redesign the guide RNA, while the Cas9 protein works in the same way with any kind of guide RNA. Moreover, synthesizing an RNA molecule is technically easier and cheaper than dealing with proteins, and the system is very easily adaptable to every region of the genome that needs to be targeted. Indeed, the CRISPR-Cas9 system - originally used with prokaryotes - is adaptable to perform genome editing in eukaryotes, and in particular human cells. Its low cost and the very **straightforward** technology makes CRISPR-Cas9 to be used by almost every laboratory. 

When performing a CRISPR experiment, Cas9 and the guide RNA must be delivered, exploiting **plasmids,** which are very easy to manipulate; thus, it is possible to clone the genomic region that encodes for the Cas9 within a plasmid, and put the guide RNA sequence of interest into another plasmid, finally delivering these vectors into the cell. In this way, the cell will be able to produce both the Cas9 protein and the guide RNA. In this way, it is possible to target specific genomic regions. Once the DNA that encodes for Cas9 and for the guide RNA is present in the cell, transcription and, in the case of Cas9, translation can be carried out. Then, the complex with Cas9 and guide RNA can be assembled, keeping in mind that such RNA carries the characteristics of both the transactivating and the CRISPR RNAs. In this case, the CRISPR RNA carries a sequence **complementary** to the target region on the genome. 

However, there are two big problems in this technology: 

163 

- the **delivery** into target cells is **not** always **easy,** and it can be mediated by viruses or by non-viral vectors, depending on the necessity to deliver DNA to be transcribed and translated, mRNA, or directly the Cas9 protein. 

- **target specificity** (the same problem seen with zinc finger proteins); even ifa single genomic region is targeted, the system can also target and cut regions with a complementarity less than 100%. Indeed, for bacteria, it is better to have a system that recognizes the target also with nonperfect complementarity, allowing a response also to slight variations of the virus. Despite being an evolutionary advantage for bacteria, this is not positive for genome editing. 

However, targeting can be improved in different ways: 

- by using **bioinformatic tools** to try to predict possible other genomic locations where the guide RNA could be targeted, other than the desired one. 

- by **optimizing** the **guide RNA.** 

- by **engineering** the **Cas9 protein.** 

One way to optimize the Cas9 protein is similar to the use of Fok I fused with the zinc finger. In this design, it is possible to use a modified Cas9 able to cut only one strand; thus, in order to generate a double-strand break, two different Cas9 must be used, one for each guide, so that each of them only targets one of the strands, creating a nick in DNA, that is not dangerous for the cell (so it can be repaired). The CRISPR technology has been used to treat **sickle-cell anemia** and **f}-thalassemia.** Researchers designed a CRISPR Cas9 system to target the **BclllA gene:** within an intron of this gene, there is an enhancer, a region of DNA important to drive transcription. In particular, this gene, when activated, is responsible for the synthesis of adult hemoglobin and for the downregulation of fetal hemoglobin. Thus, since in sickle cell anemia and B-thalassemia there are problems with adult hemoglobin, one way to solve the problem could be to induce again the synthesis of the **fetal hemoglobin.** Researchers, by using CRISPR-Cas9, were able to inactivate the enhancer that drives the substitution of fetal hemoglobin, thus restoring it; then, the precursors of red blood cells were taken, modified and then injected back in the patient. 

Another example regards **Leber congenital amaurosis,** an eye disease which leads to poor vision. It is caused by an **intronic splicing mutation** that creates a splicing site within an intron, causing the insertion of a **cryptic exon;** usually, the gene has exon 26 and exon 27 inserted in the final transcript. The mutation forms a cryptic exon that is recognized as an exon and inserted the mature RNA, consequently leading to a **premature termination codon** and to a degradation of the transcript. In this way, the protein is not produced, but by using the CRISPR-Cas9 technology, the region containing the mutation can be targeted to induce a deletion, and in theory, this should restore the synthesis of the protein. The system is packed in **adeno-associated virus 5,** directly injected in the eye of the patient; indeed, this treatment is administered in vivo, differently from others. 

Moreover, not only CRISPR can be used to perform knockouts ( eliminate parts) of the genome, but it can also be employed to perform **knocking experiments,** namely, to modify DNA by inserting something exogenous. One of the key progresses in the field of CRISPR technology has been the development of **base editing technologies,** able to precisely convert a single base into another without causing double strand DNA breaks. 

164 

### **Stem cells** 

Rate and pattern of cell turnover are extremely tissue-dependent. Most specialized cells are unable to divide, so they are **terminally differentiated.** Their replacement is possible thanks to the presence of **proliferating precursor cells,** which derive from a smaller number of self-renewing stem cells. Stem cells and precursor cells are retained in the corresponding tissue in **stem celi niches.** 

### _Self-renewing stem cells_ 

**Self-renewing stem cells** are not themselves terminally differentiated, meaning that they are not at the end ofthe pathway for differentiation. They do not perform a specialized function, but generate cells that will. They can divide without limit, or at least for the lifetime of the animai. When dividing, each daughter cell has a choice: it can remain a stem cell, or it can embark on a course that commits it to terminal differentiation **(symmetric and asymmetric division** respectively). 

**In environmental or extrinsic asymmetry,** daughters of the stem cell are initially similar and are directed into different pathways according to the environmental influences. 

**In divisional or intrinsic asymmetry,** the stem cell has an internal asymmetry and divides in such a way that its two daughters are already endowed with different **determinants,** concrete molecules in the cell that will define what the cell will become (like epigenetic modifications, and RNA, whose distribution in daughter cells is not identica!, as instead occurs for DNA). 

Environmental factors influence the ability to maintain the progenitor population, and a cell relies on contact with its niche to be able to self-renew (higher flexibility). Regulators of self-renewal are asymmetrically localized during mitosis, so that only one daughter cell inherits the regulators and thus takes on a different fate than its sister cell (predefined developmental program). 

### _Stem cells potency_ 

It is possible to distinguish stem cells based on their **potency:** 

- **totipotent cells** are able to differentiate into cell linages from all three germ layers (mesoderm, endoderm, ectoderm), including placenta! cells. They include cells at the level of the morula and zygote. 

- **pluripotent cells** (embryonic stem cells, induced pluripotent stem cells) are able to differentiate into cells from all three germ layers with the exception ofthe placenta. 

- **multipotent cells** (like mesenchymal stem cell, and hematopoietic stem cells) are able to differentiate into a limited number of types from one germ layer. 

Stem cells can be divided based on their origin: 

- **embryonic stem cells** are pluripotent stem cells isolated from the blastocyst, and characterized by endless proliferation. 

- **adult stem cells** include adipose tissue, bone marrow, and peripheral blood and are multipotent. They are responsible for replacing damaged and dead cells ofthe body (tissue maintenance role), and are localized in stem cell niches. 

165 

- **induced pluripotent stem cells** can actually be derived virtually from any kind of cell, like fibroblasts, keratinocytes, peripheral blood and mononuclear blood and can be obtained through a **reprogramming technique.** Tue method of their production was first described in 2006 by **Yamanaka** and **Takahashi.** 

Studies on the idea of reprogramming differentiated cells into stem cells are actually much older: first studies in 1960s were done by **Gurdon,** who demonstrated in 1962 that somatic cells can be reprogrammed to achieve a stem cell state. He replaced the nucleus in unfertilized frog eggs, by taking a nucleus from a fully differentiated somatic cell of another frog and putting it into the cytoplasm of the a- nucleated egg. This egg with the transplanted nucleus was able to generate the normal tadpoles, and this showed that the nucleus of an adult cell still contains all the necessary determinants that can be found in the nucleus of a zygote. The modified eggs developed into normal tadpoles, meaning that the nucleus of adult cells in frogs still contains all the genetic information as would be found in the nucleus of the zygote. 

A similar experiment was performed in 1996 to generate **Dolly the sheep.** A donor egg was denucleated (from a black sheep), while a somatic cell provided the nucleus (from a white sheep), generating a full cell. The embryo was implanted in a foster mother and gave birth to a white sheep, and this was the first successful attempt of cloning animals. 

A turning point that completely changed the efficacy of these attempts was in 2006: if normally, a pluripotent stem cell would gradually mature and reach the differentiated state, it was possible to reprogram it, going from the differentiated state to the pluripotent state. This discovery was worth the Nobel prize in 2012, awardedjointly to Gurdon and Yamanaka, who created a cocktail oftranscription factors that allows the **reprogramming** of the **maturation direction** and makes the cell become pluripotent again. Once reprogrammed, the induced pluripotent stem cells can differentiate to different pathways. 

### _Clinica! applications of stem cells_ 

The iPS discovery was a breakthrough for potential **transplantation** studies and **regenerative medicine.** Among adult stem cells, **hematopoietic stem cells** have the highest potential of differentiation, are located in the bone marrow and in the umbilical cord. These cells can be easily collected and transplanted from bone marrow, peripheral blood and umbilical cord. The transplant can be **autologous** (the patient's own stem cells are used) or **allogeneic** (the stem cells come from a donor). Associated risks include severe immunoreactions and contaminations. The recipient's bone marrow is usually destroyed with radiation or chemotherapy before transplantation. 

### _Skin stem cells_ 

**Skin stem cells** have been used since the 1980s to grow sheets of new skin in the lab for severely bumed patients. This technology can be combined with gene therapy approaches to treat skin diseases such as **epidermolysis bullosa.** Epidermolysis bullosa is a group of rare genetic conditions that results in easily blistering of the skin and mucous membranes. No treatments are currently available. Generalized Junctional Epidermolysis Bullosa (JEB) is a severe, often lethal genetic disease characterized by structural and mechanical fragility of the skin. The regeneration of virtually the entire epidermis on a 7- 

166 

year-old child suffering from a devastating farm of JEB by means of autologous transgenic keratinocyte cultures saved his life. 

### _Eye stem cells_ 

**Corneal renewal** and **repair** are mediated by stem cells of the **limbus,** the narrow zone between the cornea and the bulbar conjunctiva. Ocular bums may destroy the limbus, causing limbal stem-cell deficiency, and the cornea gets invaded by bulbar conjunctival cells. This process leads to neovascularization, chronic inflammation, and scarring, with corneal opacity and loss ofvision. 

**lt** is possible to transplant cells of the limbus, and it is the only way to prevent visi on loss. Restoring stem cells of the limbus is the only way to prevent the recurrence of corneal invasion and vision loss. **Autologous limbal cell cultures,** containing limbal stem cells, offer an opportunity to treat patients who have severe bilateral loss of healthy corneal epithelium (provided that a tiny part of the limbus is spared in one of the two eyes ). 

Adult stem cells are normally in very small numbers in each tissue, making it difficult to find and purify them. Currently, there is no technology available to generate large quantities of stem cells in culture. Allogeneic transplant requires antigen-matched donar. iPSCs are abundant in somatic cells of the donar, and issues of compatibility with donor/recipient transplant can thus be avoided. They are very useful far drug development and developmental studies. 

### _Disease modelling_ 

**Disease-specific iPS cells** can farm cell lineages reflecting the defects caused by a certain disease in patients. Some human diseases far which models have been established by patient-specific iPS cells include **amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, spinai muscular atrophy, Parkinson's disease, Gaucher disease.** Disease models based on iPS cells can result in both insights into the mechanisms of pathogenesis and the development of new drugs. 

Injury or diseases that destroys neurons leads to permanent disability, prompting interest in iPS cells as a source far cell replacement therapies. Preclinical studies indicate that iPS-derived dopaminergic neurons at a specific midbrain stage and of a particular subtype can function in rodent and primate models of Parkinson's disease. Clinical trials involving transplantation of iPSC-derived dopaminergic neurons are expected to begin soon. 

### _Regenerative medicine_ 

**Immunological rejection** is a major issue in organ transplantation and cell therapy, because long term treatment with immunosuppressive drugs would produce serious side effects. Patient-specific iPS cells have the immune markers ofthe patient, so they salve the problem of immunological rejection 

Disease-causing mutations can also be restored by gene editing in patient-specific iPS cells. Repaired cells can differentiate into targeted cells. After transplanted into the diseased area, they can alleviate disease symptoms. 

167 

### _Drug screening_ 

Test hypothesis of drug mechanisms _in vitro_ in a cost-effective manner before initiating timely and expensive clinical trials. Generating patient-specific cell types offer the possibility to conduct _in vitro_ "clinica! trials", in which drugs can be tested for toxicity and efficacy. This leads to more precise identificati on of what drugs can be eff ective, which can then be tested and correlated _in vivo_ in the same patients. iPSC-based models can be used to identify already available drugs, to be repurposed for the treatment of diseases with high medicai need, but little to non-exiting therapeutic options 

As an example, **ezogabine,** an anti-epileptic drug, showed efficacy in iPSC-derived motor neurons to model amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), leading to the subsequent initiation of clinical trials. 

iPS cells, however, carry the potential to form **teratomas** in the undifferentiated state. Cellular differentiation prior to cell transplantation decreases the risk of teratoma formation, but does not eliminate the risk of inadvertent transplantation of residuai undifferentiated cells. Tue generation of iPS can be performed with different methods: **virai vectors** are one of the most used strategies, but they integrate into the host genome. Thus, by far, the safest reprogramming methods are those which employ the use of RNA, protein, and **small particle chemical delivery,** all of which are transgene-free. Unfortunately, these methods are the least efficient and the slowest; nonetheless, they are being employed with greater frequency. 

### _Organoids_ 

**Organoids** are 3D multicellular aggregates derived from stem cells that differentiate and self-organize to recapitulate the structural features and cell-cell interactions of mature tissues. Engineers have developed powerful tools allowing the culture of organoids in 3D contexts that provide more defined environments. Instead of growing organoids without any spatial constraints in ill-defined 3D matrices, well-defined **biomaterials** and **microtechnology** may be applied to guide _in vitro_ development, through geometrie and/or mechanical inputs that mimic the embryo environment (3D bioprinting). Instead of flooding stem cells with high and uniform concentrations of key morphogens, it should be possible to utilize engineering technology, for instance **microfluidics,** to deliver morphogens in a highly controlled manner. Bioreactor technology or engineered blood vessel systems may be employed to address the major problem of nutrient availability in growing organoids and thereby allow proper long-term growth of complex systems.
